# Review cycle 01

Ten independent reviewer agents (R1–R10, distinct lenses) reviewed the manuscript, Supplementary Material, code and result files; a consolidation agent merged and prioritised their issues. Agent outputs are reproduced verbatim below; the response and the actions taken are recorded at the end of this file.

## Recommendations

|---|---|---|

| R1_novelty | major revision | 0/0/2/4/4 |

| R9_associate_editor | major revision | 0/0/5/4/5 |

## Consolidated action list

**Overall:** The 10 reviews contain 106 issues, merged here into 55 actions: 2 critical, 16 major, 23 moderate and 14 minor. No issue is fatal.

The core validity result is robust and was independently reproduced by several reviewers. With exchangeable random calibrants, the conformal windows reach about 1−α on average (P 0.911, J+ 0.909 at n=20 and α=0.10).

The two critical problems:
1. **A01:** A calibrated residual rule (±k̂·RMSE with k learned on training systems) is missing. The paper's own Table 2 implies it would be nearly equivalent to the 'Proposed' windows.
2. **A02:** The efficiency claims rest on system-level tests over 228 clustered systems, which are really 5 sources and about 159 LC methods. The source-level meta-analysis already in statistics.json shows no significant population-level gain (e.g. P vs Gaussian ratio 0.94, CI 0.80–1.10).

The major problems fall into three groups:
- **Overstated claims:** the guarantee is only ≥1−2α (A03), and per-laboratory coverage spread is not reported (A06).
- **Normalisation value:** the meta-learned σ does not show practical value. Its gain is confined to 2 of 5 sources, it gives no decoy-filtering gain, and the long-gradient rule fails externally (A04, A05).
- **Untested realistic conditions and selective reporting:** novel analytes (A07, A08), NORMAN comparators (A09), HILIC informativeness (A10), B7 reporting (A11), SASC validity (A12), base-model accuracy (A13), realistic candidate lists (A14), disclosure of the authors' own Talanta paper (A15), uncredited Bach retention-order work (A16), AI-use disclosure (A17) and the deployable bundle (A18).

The current Final_Manuscript.md (/home/user/p_c1_5/Target_Journal_Submission/manuscript/Final_Manuscript.md, built 13:50) predates commits 578ca24, 64f693c, b977618 and d3669d3. Several fixes are therefore half-done in code or results but not yet in the text: the B7 run-1 correction, Addendum F, the revision-1 analyses, the API and the bibliography. A rebuild is required after the revisions.

All actions except A39 (author details) can be done with the data and compute available here.

| A01 | critical | The simplest practical competitor is missing: a residual-multiplier window ±k̂·RMSE_LOO with k learned on public training systems. Its t-quantile version and plain (non-plus) jackknife are also missing. The paper's own numbers suggest near-equivalence with the 'Proposed' windows. Table 2: ±2·RMSE covers 0.919 with 0 % of systems <0.85, median width 5.17 min, IS 9.97. Width is exactly linear in k, and interpolation gives k≈1.8 → coverage ≈0.90 at ≈4.65 min (P: 0.911, 4.68 min) with the same decoy elimination (≈0.243 at 0.914 retention). On the primary IS metric the Gaussian ±z·RMSE window beats P at n=15 (paired +0.47 min, p=0.007) and ties at n=10, which the text does not report. B1 uses z rather than t_{n−1} (intervals.py l.52). The Abstract Significance and Highlight 1 nevertheless generalise the failure to all 'conventional windows'. | R10-1, R9-03, R10-6 | 1. Implement three comparators: (a) B1-cal: ±k̂_α·RMSE_LOO, with k̂_α estimated per leave-source-out fold by replaying random calibrant draws on the training systems (reuse the SASC code path); (b) B1-t: ±t_{n−1,1−α/2}·RMSE_LOO; (c) B0: plain jackknife, i.e. the (1−α)(1+1/n) empirical quantile of \|LOO residuals\| around the full-fit map. 2. Add them to Tables 2–4, S2–S4, S13–S16 and Fig. 2. 3. State the n=10 and n=15 IS reversals against the Gaussian window in §4.1. 4. Rewrite the Abstract Significance, Highlight 1 and Conclusions: the demonstrated failure applies to fixed windows and to per-system GP/P-spline intervals, not to residual multiples. Against a calibrated residual rule, the contribution of conformal windows is the finite-sample guarantee and per-system adaptivity, not higher average retention or better filtering. | Rerun on the 228 RP systems (all n, all α, the same 20 draws), on HILIC and on NORMAN (all three scenarios) with B0, B1-t and B1-cal. Report: - mean coverage and per-draw coverage dispersion; - median and mean width; - IS; - decoy elimination at matched retention; - paired tests on log IS ratios against P and J+, with source-level meta-analysis (see A02). B1-t reuses the existing LOO residuals, so it is cheap. | True |
| A02 | critical | The efficiency inference treats 228 systems as independent. R4 reports P vs J+ ≈0.92 (0.81–1.04), with sign-flip p 0.25–0.31. - Wilcoxon tests on minutes weight long-gradient systems most heavily. - The five-cluster bootstrap is degenerate: the cluster CI for the Gaussian window is narrower than the system CI, and p sits at the resampling floor 1/4001. Yet §4.1 says it gave 'practically identical' CIs. | R4-1, R3-4, R5-3, R4-5, R10-4 | 1. Make the source-aware analysis co-primary for every paired efficiency claim (Tables 3, S4, S8, S17, SMRT, HILIC, SASC). Report the random-effects pooled ratio (HKSJ) and/or an LMM with random intercepts for source and LC method, with τ², I² and a 95 % prediction interval for a new source. 2. Run Wilcoxon tests on per-system log IS ratios, not on minutes. 3. Merge pos/neg twins into LC-method units ('159 LC methods (228 RepoRT datasets)') and recompute CIs, '% <0.85', correlations and per-fold tests at that level. 4. Replace the naive five-cluster bootstrap with t(4) intervals on source means or a wild cluster bootstrap (Webb weights), and delete the 'practically identical' sentence. 5. Restate H3 as source-dependent: a gain in 2 of 5 sources, none externally. State that system-level p-values describe these 228 systems, not new laboratories. 6. Add a twin detector to the cleaning pipeline: same source and name apart from a pos/neg token, median \|dRT\| ≤ 0.05 min, r ≥ 0.995. | Re-analysis only: - random-effects meta-analysis or LMM for all paired comparisons; - leave-one-source-out influence analysis; - LC-method-level and RT-identity-cluster sensitivity analyses; - per-fold effective n. | True |

| Action | Verdict | Evidence | Minimal correct fix |

| A01 | partly | Confirmed: - code/src/conformrt/evaluation/intervals.py l.52-53 uses `z = stats.norm.ppf(1 - alpha / 2); hw = z * np.sqrt(np.mean(loo_resid ** 2))`. The Methods (Final_Manuscript.md l.79) state this openly. There is no t_{n-1} variant, no residual multiplier learned on the training systems and no plain-jackknife comparator. - tables/table9_fixed_windows.md, n=20: RMSE_x2 coverage 0.919, share_severe_under 0.000, median width 5.165, IS 9.970. Gaussian (k=1.645): 0.885, 4.248, 9.649. Width is linear in k (both give 2.58 min per unit k). Interpolating, k≈1.8 gives about 0.90 coverage at about 4.65 min median width, against P at 0.911 and 4.68 min. So the coverage and width part of the claim holds. - tables/tableS3_paired_winkler_all_n_alpha.csv, P vs B1_gauss_rmse:   - n=15, α=0.10: median diff +0.467 min (CI 0.25 to 0.65), P better in only 38 % of systems, p_holm=0.007. Mean IS is 11.28 (P) vs 10.27 (Gaussian).   - n=10: median diff -0.04 (CI -0.18 to 0.16), mean IS 12.07 vs 12.06. This is effectively a tie, although Wilcoxon p=0.028 slightly favours P.   - The issue misses one more reversal: n=30, α=0.05, +0.456, p=0.014.   - The text (l.129) mentions the n=15 reversal only for ±2·RMSE, never for the Gaussian window. Line 155 cites only the n=20 result (P better in 62 %). - Overgeneralisation: Significance (l.23) says "conventional windows discard many correct structures" and Highlight 1 says "Parametric and fixed RT windows lose correct structures". Yet ±2·RMSE never had a system with mean coverage below 0.85 at n≥15 (share_severe_under 0.000; 0.018 at n=10).  Overstated: 1. Near-equivalence on the primary IS metric at n=20. I ran a quick check on results/raw/main_errors.parquet, the per-compound rows of the stored n=20, α=0.10 draw. I learned k̂ leave-source-out, solving for mean per-system coverage = 0.90 on the other folds' systems:    - k̂ = 1.76, 1.69, 1.71, 1.70, 1.68 across the five folds.    - B1-cal: coverage 0.901, median width 4.08, mean IS 9.83.    - P: 0.924, 4.39, 9.00. Jackknife+: 9.99. B1-t(19): 0.903, 9.85.    - Paired IS, B1-cal minus P: median +0.47 min, P better in 70 % of systems, Wilcoxon p≈2e-8.    So a calibrated multiplier fixes average coverage but does not match P on IS at n=20. Because IS is convex in k, any k in [1.645, 2] gives about 9.65 to 9.97, against P's 9.07 in Table 2. 2. The filtering point is already conceded. Line 187 says "Conformal windows therefore do not filter more efficiently" (Gaussian 26.7 % vs P 26.3 % at 90 % matched retention). The Conclusions (l.254) say "do not remove more false candidates than conventional windows at the same retention". Part of fix item 4 is therefore already in the text. 3. The proposed fix is broader than needed:    - B0 (plain jackknife) adds little next to jackknife+, which is already reported.    - B1-t at n=20 nearly coincides with B1-cal (t_{19,0.95}=1.729, close to k̂). At small n it would still under-cover: t_9=1.833 interpolates to about 0.88 at n=10.    - Rebuilding Tables 2–4, S2–S4, S13–S16 and Fig. 2 is not required to settle the point.  Caveat: my check uses a single stored draw, and k̂ was learned from the other folds' target-system residuals. It was not learned from replays on training systems. | 1. Analysis.    - Add B1-cal: ±k̂_{n,α}·RMSE_LOO. Learn k̂ per n and α, leave-source-out, from simulated calibrant draws on the training systems (the SASC replay path), targeting mean coverage 1−α.    - Add B1-t: ±t_{n−1,1−α/2}·RMSE_LOO. It reuses the existing LOO residuals.    - Report both in Table 2, in the all-n/α tables (coverage, between-system and per-draw coverage dispersion, median width, IS, paired log-IS against P and J+), in Table 4 (robustness, including the most-frequent-calibrant scenario where a global k̂ may also break) and for HILIC and NORMAN.    - Plain jackknife is optional. Re-running every decoy table (S13–S16) is not needed: add only the matched-retention point for B1-cal. 2. Results text (§4.1, l.129/155): state that P has a higher IS than the Gaussian window at n=15 (median +0.47 min, Holm p=0.007; mean IS 11.28 vs 10.27) and at n=30, α=0.05 (+0.46, p=0.014), and is about equal at n=10. Explain this by the conservative discrete quantile index (0.945 coverage at n=15). 3. Abstract Significance, Highlight 1 and Conclusions: replace "conventional windows" with "fixed, uncalibrated residual and per-system parametric (GP/P-spline) windows". Add one sentence: a residual multiplier calibrated on public systems reaches nominal coverage on average. Against it, the conformal windows offer the finite-sample per-system guarantee, an explicit finiteness limit, and (at n≥20 in RP) a lower interval score from the difficulty model. They do not offer better decoy filtering, which is already stated. Do not claim that the conformal windows lack an IS advantage over B1-cal until the full rerun is done: the single-draw check shows P better at n=20 (IS 9.00 vs 9.83). |
| A02 | partly | The core problem is real and matters for the efficiency inference. Several details are overstated, and the proposed fix goes further than needed.  CONFIRMED (1) System-level analysis only. Methods line 116 of Final_Manuscript.md says: "The unit of analysis is the target system ... two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank tests ... on per-system interval scores". §4.2 (line 155) gives the P vs J+ result as "median of −0.43 min ... Holm-adjusted p < 0.001". These are differences in minutes, not log ratios.  (2) The source-level analyses were computed but are not reported. statistics.json['source_meta'] and tables/tableS18_source_meta.csv contain: - P vs Gaussian: ratio 0.937 (CI 0.797–1.100), PI 0.58–1.51, p_hksj 0.322, I² 0.977, LMM 0.935 (0.834–1.048), p 0.250. - P vs J+: ratio 0.918 (0.785–1.074), p_hksj 0.206, I² 0.979, LMM 0.917 (0.821–1.025), p 0.128. - P vs P-spline: p_hksj 0.208. - P vs GP: p_hksj 0.361. The per-source ratios change sign: BGC 1.03–1.06, PredRet 1.05–1.07, Folberth 0.83–0.85, Souihi+Stoffel 0.79–0.80. No HKSJ, LMM, τ² or I² appears in the manuscript or in Supplementary_Material.md. The Supplement's own "Table S18" is the hyper-parameter table. The source-level ablation results also undercut the line-193 claim that all five feature groups gave "Holm-adjusted p < 0.001": at source level P-system has p_hksj 0.079, P-novelty 0.095 and P-ion 0.071.  (3) The five-cluster bootstrap is degenerate. Table S3, n=20, α=0.10, Gaussian: system CI [0.883, 0.888], cluster CI [0.883, 0.887], so the cluster CI is narrower. coverage_validity gives cluster_p_under_one_sided = 0.00024994 = 1/4001, the resampling floor. Line 127 still says the cluster bootstrap "gave practically identical confidence intervals".  (4) My re-analysis from raw_results.csv: - t(4) test on the five source means, P vs J+: ratio 0.917 (0.784–1.073), p = 0.20. - Same test, P vs Gaussian: ratio 0.935 (0.796–1.098), p = 0.31.  (5) Twins exist. In the 228 targets, names such as 'RP21 pos'/'RP21 neg', 'Standard_pos'/'Standard_neg' and 'MSMS_pos_40_ACN_HSS T3'/'MSMS_neg_40…' have near-identical RTs.  OVERSTATED OR NOT REPRODUCED (a) Twin counts. My detector (same source, name equal after a pos/neg token swap, median \|dRT\| ≤ 0.05 min, r ≥ 0.995, ≥10 shared compounds) finds 53 twin groups, giving 175 LC-method units, and 121 RT-identity clusters. The issue says 69 groups, 159 units and 119 clusters, so its numbers depend on the detector's rules. Also, transitive chaining merges different temperatures and %ACN into one RT cluster (e.g. temp37.5–42.5).  (b) Merging twins does not overturn the main system-level result. Wilcoxon P vs J+: p = 3.5e-4 on 175 LC units and p = 0.014 on 121 RT clusters. For P vs Gaussian: p = 0.040 and p = 0.24. Wilcoxon on log IS ratios is also still significant (statistics.json p_wilcoxon_log_ratio: 1.7e-8 vs J+, 3.5e-5 vs Gaussian). The decisive problem is between-source heterogeneity, not twins and not minutes versus ratios.  (c) The manuscript already hedges part of this: - Abstract (line 21): "Normalisation improved efficiency for longer-than-median runs ... but not for shorter runs or the external methods". - Line 159 reports the per-fold medians, with BGC at p = 0.09. - Limitations (line 249): "systems within a source are not independent, and a cluster bootstrap over five sources is necessarily coarse".  (d) There is no "H3" in the manuscript; only H1 and H2 are labelled (line 127).  (e) Coverage holds up when clustered by source. The parametric windows are below nominal in every source: Gaussian 0.880–0.888, P-spline 0.842–0.863, GP 0.810–0.829, one-sided t(4) p ≤ 0.0002. P (0.903–0.915) and J+ (0.900–0.912) are at or above nominal in every source.  (f) Fix items 3 and 6 are too much as primary changes: - RepoRT pos/neg sets are distinct datasets. - Twins sit inside sources, so a source-level analysis already accounts for them. - Making LC-method units primary and changing the cleaning pipeline is unnecessary; a sensitivity analysis is enough. | This is a re-analysis and wording fix; no new experiments are needed.  1. Methods, line 116: state that the 228 systems are nested in 5 sources and include positive/negative-mode twins. Add a source-level inference that is reported for every paired efficiency claim:    - a random-effects pooled log ratio of per-system interval scores (HKSJ), with τ², I² and the 95 % prediction interval for a new source;    - an LMM with a random intercept for source.    State that system-level Wilcoxon p-values describe these 228 systems, not new laboratories.  2. Publish the existing results in tables/tableS18_source_meta.csv (statistics.json['source_meta']) as a new Supplementary table. Number it S21 or later, because S18 is already used. Add a column "source-level ratio [95 % CI]; HKSJ p" to Table 3.  3. §4.2: after the system-level result, add: "Across sources, however, the gain was heterogeneous (I² = 0.96–0.98; per-source ratio vs jackknife+ 0.79–1.05) and not significant at source level: pooled ratio 0.92 (95 % CI 0.78–1.07; HKSJ p = 0.21; LMM 0.92, 0.82–1.03, p = 0.13) versus jackknife+ and 0.94 (0.80–1.10; p = 0.32) versus the Gaussian window; the prediction interval for a new source (≈0.57–1.48) includes a loss. The gain was concentrated in the Folberth and Souihi+Stoffel sources." Make the matching change in §4.x line 193: at source level, the system, novelty and ionisation feature groups were not significant (HKSJ p 0.07–0.095). Carry the same source-dependence wording into the Conclusions and Limitations.  4. Line 127: delete "a cluster bootstrap over source folds gave practically identical confidence intervals". Replace it with: "Coverage of the parametric windows was below nominal in each of the five sources (Gaussian 0.880–0.888, P-spline 0.842–0.863, GP 0.810–0.829; one-sided t-test on source means, df = 4, p ≤ 0.0002), whereas the proposed and jackknife+ windows were at or above nominal in every source." In Table S3, replace the five-cluster pairs-bootstrap column with t(4) intervals on source means, or a wild cluster bootstrap with Webb weights. Note that p = 1/4001 is the resampling floor of the old method.  5. Add sensitivity rows, not primary units:    - pos/neg twins merged into LC-method units, with the detector rule stated and its counts reported (my rule: 53 groups → 175 units);    - RT-identity clusters (121 by my rule);    - leave-one-source-out pooled ratios.    Also give the Wilcoxon tests on per-system log interval-score ratios (already computed) as a sensitivity check. Do not change the cleaning pipeline. |
| A03 | real | The core problem is in the files. Three points in the issue are overstated (see "Overstated" below).  Theory proves only 1−2α: - Final_Manuscript.md l.59 says "P{t(x)∈C_α(x)} ≥ 1−2α … and coverage is close to 1−α when the fitted map is stable [Barber]". - The S2 Proposition (S2_coverage_proof.md l.11; Supplementary_Material.md l.51) states only "≥ 1−2α".  The headline text implies 1−α is guaranteed: - Highlight 2 (l.13): "keep a user-chosen retention rate". - Abstract Background (l.19): "keep the correct structure at a user-chosen rate". - Significance (l.23): "make the probability … a design choice". - §4.1 (l.127): "keep the promised error rate on average". - Ablation, the 7th Results subsection (l.193): "marginal coverage was unchanged, as guaranteed". The 1−2α bound cannot guarantee unchanged coverage across ablations. - Conclusions (l.254): "restored the promised rate … provided that at least 1/α−1 calibrants are measured". This treats the finiteness condition as if it were enough for the rate. - Cover_Letter.md l.17: "Conformal windows, which carry a distribution-free finite-sample guarantee, retained 90.9 %–91.1 % … At least 1/α−1 calibrants are required". Next to 90.9 %, this reads as a 1−α guarantee. - Limitations (l.249) says "finite-sample guarantee is marginal" but never gives the level. - Neither the Abstract nor the Highlights mentions 1−2α.  Recommendation (ii) conflates finiteness with the guarantee: - l.245: "Measure at least 1/α−1 calibrants — 19 for a 95 % window". - The algebra is right for finiteness: k_u = ⌈(1−α)(n+1)⌉ ≤ n ⟺ n ≥ 1/α−1. - At n = 19 and α = 0.05 the guarantee is only 0.90. A guaranteed ≥1−α window needs jackknife+ at α/2, so n ≥ 2/α−1 (19 for 90 %, 39 for 95 %). The issue's arithmetic is correct.  "Narrowest among valid methods" (l.155): - Here "valid" can only mean empirical coverage at or above nominal. Theoretically P is ≥1−2α, while split conformal is ≥1−α. - Code check: system_eval.py l.119 uses a random permutation split, so the split-conformal ≥1−α guarantee is legitimate. - statistics.json revision1.guaranteed_windows compares the methods at the same guaranteed level (≥0.90):  \| n \| P at α=0.05 (coverage, median width) \| Split conformal at α=0.10 (coverage, median width) \| \|---\|---\|---\| \| 20 \| 0.958, 7.10 min \| 0.909, 7.04 min \| \| 30 \| 0.970, 7.72 min \| 0.938, 7.32 min \| \| 40 \| 0.953, 5.37 min \| 0.905, 5.00 min \|  - So at a matched guaranteed level, split conformal is narrower at every n. This is stronger than the issue claims (it quoted only n = 20). - The analysis exists (analyze.py l.484–492, tables/tableS24_guaranteed_windows.csv) but is reported nowhere. grep finds no "guaranteed_level" in the manuscript or Supplementary. - Its file name also clashes with the Supplementary's Table S24, which is SASC (Supplementary_Material.md l.956).  Stability clause: grep for "stabil" finds only l.59. No diagnostic backs the "≈1−α when stable" statement.  Overstated: - **Title:** the ≥1−2α bound is itself distribution-free (it needs only exchangeability). "Distribution-free" in the title is therefore accurate and need not be dropped. The cover-letter phrase is likewise technically true, only misleading in context. - **Highlight 3** (l.14) already says "needed for a *finite* window" and is correct as written. It does not conflate the two n values. - **Empirical claims** (0.909–0.911 at nominal 0.90) are true. Only the words "promised" and "guaranteed" overreach. - **Stability diagnostic and full conformal** are not needed for correctness. The marginal ≈1−α claim is already supported empirically on 228 systems. The conditional-coverage section (l.181: outside-range coverage 0.755 for J+, 0.879 for P) already fits the stated hypothesis. | Wording (required):  1. **Abstract.** Add one sentence: "Under exchangeability of calibrants and analytes, jackknife+ windows carry a distribution-free guarantee of at least 1−2α, marginal over calibrant sets and compounds (≥1−α when run at α/2). Empirically they retained close to 1−α." Rephrase "user-chosen rate" and the Significance "design choice" to "controlled, with a guaranteed lower bound". 2. **Highlight 2.** Keep it within 85 characters, for example: "Calibrant jackknife+ windows guarantee ≥1−2α retention; ≈1−α observed". Highlight 3 can stay as it is. 3. **§4.1, Conclusions and Limitations.** Replace "promised error rate" / "promised rate" with "nominal rate". State that the guarantee is ≥1−2α, and that 1/α−1 calibrants is the condition for finite windows only. 4. **Ablation section.** Replace "unchanged, as guaranteed" with "unchanged". 5. **Recommendation (ii).** Split it into two parts:    - finite window: n ≥ 1/α−1;    - guaranteed ≥1−α: run jackknife+ (or P) at α/2 with n ≥ 2/α−1, i.e. 19 for 90 % and 39 for 95 %, or use split conformal at α. 6. **Cover letter.** Add "(≥1−2α)" after "finite-sample guarantee". Make the same "finite window" correction to its 1/α−1 sentence. 7. **Title.** It may stay unchanged.  Analysis (already computed, so only reporting is needed):  1. In §4.2, define "valid" as empirical mean coverage at or above nominal (one-sided test). 2. Add 2–3 sentences and a Supplementary table, renumbered to avoid the clash with the SASC Table S24, from revision1.guaranteed_windows for n = 20/30/40. Acknowledge that at a matched guaranteed level of ≥0.90, split conformal at α=0.10 is as narrow or narrower than P at α=0.05 (7.04 vs 7.10, 7.32 vs 7.72, 5.00 vs 5.37 min). P's advantage holds only at the nominal level, which rests on the empirical ≈1−α behaviour.  Stability clause at l.59: either reword it to "Barber et al. show coverage approaches 1−α for stable algorithms; we observed 0.909–0.911", or add the inexpensive LOO-stability diagnostic suggested in the issue (max_i\|g_{-i}−g\| / half-width, inside versus outside the calibrant range, n ∈ {10, 20}) as a supplementary figure. The diagnostic is recommended but optional. A full-conformal run is not required. |
| A04 | partly | Real: the main text still frames the σ-normalised variant as "the method". Final_Manuscript.md has "proposed" 40 times. The Theory heading reads "*Normalised jackknife+ with a meta-learned difficulty model (proposed).*". Table 2 shows "\| **Proposed** \| 0.911 ... \| 4.68 \| 9.07 \|" as a bold row. Table 3 is titled "Paired comparison of the interval scores of the proposed windows". §Efficiency opens "Among the methods with valid coverage, the proposed windows were the narrowest". The negative findings the reviewer cites are accurate: per-fold gains of −18 % (Folberth) and −10 % (Souihi+Stoffel) against +3/+0/+0 in the other folds (Table 3), HILIC "16.70 versus 14.33 min", and NORMAN calibration chemicals "reduced efficiency (Holm-adjusted p = 0.02 and 0.01)". §4.6 does not mention the α=0.20 loss. tableS8_decoy_tests.md gives "0.200 \| B5_jackknife_plus \| 228 \| -0.006 \| -0.203 \| 0.008". §4.6 also does not give J+ at 90 % retention (0.266 in tableS14_matched_retention.csv).  Overstated or wrong: (1) "§4.6 omits the J+ matched-retention value" is false at 85 %. §4.6 reads "at 85 % retention, decoy elimination ranged from 32.9 % (proposed) and 33.4 % (jackknife+)". (2) The α=0.20 result was picked selectively. At α=0.05 the normalised windows eliminate significantly more decoys than J+: "0.05 \| Jackknife+ \| 228 \| +1.4 \| +0.31 \| < 0.001" (Supplementary Table S14). At α=0.10 there is no difference (p=0.498). Retention is identical at each α (table7: J+ 0.961/0.915/0.812 vs P 0.961/0.914/0.812), so these are matched-retention comparisons. The differences run in both directions and are at most 1.4 percentage points, with unadjusted p-values. The data therefore support "no material difference in filtering". They do not support "P is worse". This also contradicts the panel's hypothesis that σ "cannot raise decoy elimination at fixed retention". (3) It is not true that the Abstract and Highlights present the normalised windows as the method. The Abstract says "jackknife+ conformal windows, optionally normalised by a meta-learned difficulty model" and "Normalisation improved efficiency for longer-than-median runs (−17 %) but not for shorter runs or the external methods, and reduced it for HILIC". The Highlights never mention σ. (4) Most of fix items 2, 3 and 5 are already in place. Recommendation (iii) reads "Use calibrant-based jackknife+ windows; the meta-learned normalisation is worthwhile for long reversed-phase gradients, whereas plain jackknife+ suffices for short gradients and HILIC". The Conclusions lead with jackknife+ and list where σ gives no gain. api.py says '"jackknife+" (recommended default)'. The prior-art report flags the same risk: "an editor may view the work as 'applying jackknife+ to RT'". (5) "No practical value" is too strong. Pooled over all systems, σ lowers the interval score versus J+ by a median −0.43 min (95 % CI −0.93 to −0.20; Holm p<0.001; better in 63 % of systems). It also gave higher coverage outside the calibrant range (0.879 vs 0.755), in the last third of the RT range (0.863 vs 0.805) and for unseen compounds (0.835 vs 0.798). The advantage held with the SMRT-only base model (11.12 vs 12.57 min). The value is real but depends on context. | Make wording changes only. No new experiment is needed. The stratified decoy rerun is optional supplementary material and is not required to support the claims.  (1) Relabel 'Proposed' everywhere to 'Normalised J+ (meta-learned σ)' or a short form such as 'J+σ'. This covers the Table 2/3 rows and titles, figure legends, the Theory heading "(proposed)" and the in-text "proposed windows". Remove the bold from that row, or bold both J+ variants.  (2) In §4.6, after the α=0.10 comparison, add: "Paired at the same α, where retention of the correct structure was identical, the normalised windows eliminated 1.4 percentage points more decoys than jackknife+ at α = 0.05 (p < 0.001), the same at α = 0.10 (p = 0.50) and 0.6 points fewer at α = 0.20 (p = 0.008, unadjusted; Table S14). At 90 % retention jackknife+ eliminated 26.6 %. The normalisation therefore does not materially change filtering efficiency." Also name J+ in the 90 %-retention sentence. Do not write that all of them were 4.5 %–21 % worse. |

Limitations only says "marginal". The closest text is the abstract: "probability ... on a laboratory's own system is unknown. We aimed at RT windows that keep the correct structure at a user-chosen rate". (2) The issue calls coverage "far less controlled" than the text implies. That is true of the wording, but the spread itself is what exact exchangeability gives. The existing reference (Beta(k, n_cal+1−k), infinite test set) matches split conformal closely: - n=20, α=0.10: 0.199 observed vs 0.197 expected - n=30: 0.098 vs 0.087 - n=40: 0.193 vs 0.176 - n=20, α=0.20: 0.248 vs 0.244 So the variability depends on which calibrants were drawn and is built into any marginal conformal method. Test-set noise adds little. A beta-binomial random-effects model is therefore not needed: the reference already exists and only needs reporting. (3) Fix 4 holds only at α=0.10. At n=40, α=0.05, the Gaussian window had 22.7 % of draws more than 0.05 below nominal, against 10.5 % for P. So Recommendation (i) should be qualified, not restricted. (4) Fix 5 (a PAC variant at n ∈ {20, 40}) is not feasible. Even with the most conservative order statistic (k = n_cal), P(cov ≥ 1−α) = 1 − (1−α)^n_cal. Reaching δ = 0.1 needs n_cal ≥ 22 at α = 0.10 (I computed this with scipy), ≥ 45 at α = 0.05 and ≥ 11 at α = 0.20. Split conformal at n = 20 or 40 uses n_cal = 10 or 20, so its PAC windows would be infinite. Jackknife+ has no training-conditional guarantee without stability assumptions. Add to Limitations: the guarantee is not calibration-conditional. A PAC guarantee at α = δ = 0.10 needs at least 22 calibration points even with the most conservative quantile (at least 44 calibrants with a half split, and at least 45 calibration points at α = 0.05), so it is not available at n ≤ 40, and jackknife+ has no training-conditional guarantee. 6. | A08 | real | The problem is real. The numbers match the files exactly, and the analysis below supports the reviewer's direction. Three parts of the issue are overstated or wrong, and the last three paragraphs set them out.  WHAT THE MANUSCRIPT REPORTS. Final_Manuscript.md l.181 (from 04_results.md) says "Coverage nevertheless remained below nominal for compounds that were neither seen by the base model nor similar to its training data (Tanimoto 0.4–0.7: 0.781; < 0.4: 0.848)". The Limitations at l.249 say "structurally novel compounds remained under-covered". Neither statement gives a CI. Supplementary Table S9 is captioned "Mean coverage (per-system coverage within the stratum, averaged over systems)". Its count column, for example "Tanimoto 0.4–0.7 \| 169,021", is pooled compound–draw evaluations.  THE UNREPORTED TABLE. code/experiments/analyze.py l.444-445 computes RV.strata_estimators. The result is stored in results/statistics.json under revision1.strata_estimators and in tables/tableS20_strata_estimators.csv. Neither appears in the Supplementary: its own "Table S20" is a different table, on calibrant selection. For P the unreported table gives: - sim0.4-0.7: systems 193, median_per_draw_count 12.95, sysavg 0.781 [0.737, 0.824], pooled 0.906 [0.898, 0.915], pooled_source_min 0.207, sysavg_ge10 0.922. - unseen: sysavg 0.835 [0.813, 0.856], pooled 0.905 [0.899, 0.912]. - rt_tertile late: sysavg 0.863 vs pooled 0.919.  FIG. 4. In code/src/conformrt/visualization/figures.py, fig_strata plots `d.coverage_sysavg`. Its x-label reads "system average". The tick label is built from `n={total.max()}`, which is a pooled evaluation count.  MY RE-ANALYSIS OF results/raw/main_strata.parquet (P, α = 0.10, n = 20). Coverage figures below are system average (sysavg) and pooled, with 95 % CIs from a two-level cluster bootstrap (source, then system within source).  \| Stratum \| All systems: sysavg [CI] \| All systems: pooled [CI] \| ≥20 evals per draw \| Excluding fold 1 (Folberth), sysavg \| \|---\|---\|---\|---\|---\| \| Tanimoto 0.4–0.7 \| 0.781 [0.500, 0.928] \| 0.906 [0.841, 0.936] \| sysavg 0.934, 80 systems \| 0.901 \| \| Not seen by base model \| 0.835 [0.740, 0.920] \| 0.905 [0.857, 0.931] \| sysavg 0.923, 98 systems \| 0.877 \|  Mixed logistic model, covered ~ stratum + (1\|system) + (1\|source), fitted by variational Bayes, implied coverage for a typical system: - Tanimoto ≥ 0.7: 0.914 - Tanimoto 0.4–0.7: 0.904 (β = -0.11 ± 0.04) - Tanimoto < 0.4: 0.880 (β = -0.37 ± 0.05) - Seen 0.912 vs unseen 0.908 (β = -0.055 ± 0.03)  So under this model the trend across Tanimoto bins is monotone, and the novelty effect is small but not zero.  WHAT IS OVERSTATED OR WRONG IN THE ISSUE  (a) The deficit is not mainly noise. It comes from one source. Fold 1 (Folberth, 32 systems, about 1.6 evaluations per system per draw, 1,020 evaluations in total) has Tanimoto 0.4–0.7 coverage of 0.179 averaged over systems and 0.207 pooled. Its <0.4 stratum has coverage of 0.997. The other four sources have 0.85–0.95. The non-monotone pattern is therefore a source-by-stratum composition effect, not binomial noise. The pooled estimate is also unrepresentative in the other direction: fold 4 supplies 102,126 of the 169,021 evaluations, about 60 %.  (b) A random-intercept-only mixed model would hide this source-specific failure. It needs a stratum-by-source term. With only 5 source clusters, the percentile cluster bootstrap is also unstable.  (c) The Fig. 4 caption is not actually inconsistent. It says "averaged over systems" and "compound–draw evaluations". The real problem is that the plot shows a system-average estimator next to a pooled sample size.  CONSEQUENCE. "Remained below nominal" holds only for the equally weighted system average with a CI that resamples systems alone. It does not hold for pooled or typical-system coverage, and not once source clustering is included in the CI. | 1. Add tables/tableS20_strata_estimators to the Supplementary as a new table, for example Table S21. Show, per stratum:    - the number of systems;    - the median evaluations per system per draw;    - the system-average and pooled coverage, each with a CI from a two-level bootstrap (source, then system), noting that there are only 5 source clusters;    - per-source pooled coverage.  2. Fit covered ~ stratum + (1\|system) + (stratum\|source) on main_strata.parquet. Alternatively, report per-source stratum coverage directly. Report:    - the stratum odds ratios;    - the implied coverage for a typical system (approximately 0.914 / 0.904 / 0.880 by Tanimoto bin, and 0.912 vs 0.908 for seen vs unseen, under random intercepts);    - a sensitivity analysis restricted to systems with at least 20 evaluations per draw (0.934 for Tanimoto 0.4–0.7, 0.923 for not seen).  3. Rewrite l.181 and the matching text in 04_results.md, and l.249 in the Limitations, along these lines: "Averaged over systems, coverage for novel compounds was 0.781 (Tanimoto 0.4–0.7; 95 % CI …) and 0.835 (not seen); pooled over compounds it was 0.906 and 0.905. The system-average deficit was concentrated in one source (Folberth; about 1.6 such compounds per system per draw; coverage about 0.2), whereas the other sources were at 0.85–0.95. A mixed model indicated a small reduction for the least similar compounds (Tanimoto < 0.4: about 0.88 in a typical system)." Keep the caution and the Mondrian recommendation, but drop the unconditional "remained below nominal".  4. Fig. 4:    - Annotate each stratum with the number of systems and the median per-system stratum size instead of the pooled n. Alternatively, plot both estimators with CIs.    - Edit the caption to match, and change the n column header of Table S9 in the same way. |

The Abstract gives only the pooled figure: "restored 90.2 % for common-compound calibrants".  (4) Post hoc. Addendum D: "Specified after observing ... that all methods under-cover when calibrants are not random draws".  (5) Frequency leakage. Addendum F.1 (EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md:161-166) admits that counts were made "over all systems including the held-out source group". results/raw/sasc_rows.parquet (05:58) predates Addendum F (19:15), and the rerun is unfinished. (7) Wording and labels. - Limitations (line 249): "gives an average-over-systems, not a per-system, guarantee". - Highlights: "known selection rules are corrected on average", with no post hoc label. - Abstract: no post hoc label. RMSE ≈ 1.3 min can only be inferred from Table 2 (l.134 Gaussian ±z·RMSE median width 4.25 min = 2·1.645·RMSE gives 1.29; l.141 ±2·RMSE 5.17 min gives 1.29). The reviewer's implied worry ("weak base model") is not borne out. I recomputed point errors from the stored residuals: the centre of the Gaussian window, (lo+hi)/2, in results/raw/*_errors.parquet, compared with the true RT y. Per-system medians at n=20: - RepoRT RP, order model, 228 systems: MAE 0.82 min, median AE 0.35 min, RMSE 1.44 min (9.9 % of span), Kendall τ 0.85. - SMRT base: MAE 1.30, RMSE 1.82 (12.9 %), τ 0.73. - HILIC, 37 systems: MAE 2.12, RMSE 2.88 (17.9 %), τ 0.66. - NORMAN, 37 methods: MAE 1.44 to 1.51, RMSE about 2.0 (13.9 to 15.0 %), τ 0.67 to 0.72, depending on scenario (random_all, fixed_mix, pool_random).  So the RP base model is reasonably accurate. The wide 90 % windows come from heavy-tailed errors (RMSE about 4 times the median AE), not from a poor model. The planned dose-response check is already in the data: the SMRT base model is less accurate, gives wider windows (6.56 vs 4.68 min, l.207) and keeps validity (Table 4). An oracle or stronger base-model experiment is therefore unnecessary.  Parts of the proposed fix go too far: - n ∈ {10, 40}: the error files have no n column. In system_eval.py these feed _sigma_matrix(sig_models["P"], Cd, ...). Unseen: P 0.835 vs J+ 0.798 coverage. Seen: P 0.915 vs J+ 0.920. - Same-formula database candidates (PubChemLite, CompTox) would be mostly unseen, so P's decoy elimination on real candidate lists would probably fall relative to J+. J+ would not change.  Several parts of the issue are overstated or wrong. (1) "Mostly measured/seen". I replayed decoys_for on the five leave-source-out folds against training_table(fold, smrt_n=15000). Only 56.4% of decoys (87,191 of 154,583) are in the base-model training set, compared with 74.1% of the true test compounds (48,576 of 65,590). 35.5% of decoys are SMRT compounds, mostly not in the 15k SMRT subsample. So the decoys are less "seen" than the true compounds, but far more seen than database candidates would be. (2) The manuscript claims no filtering advantage for P. §4.6, line 187: "not significantly different from jackknife+ (91.5 % and 24.6 %; p = 0.50)". At 85% matched retention P is lowest (32.9% vs 33.4% for J+). The text concludes "Conformal windows therefore do not filter more efficiently". So a bias would turn parity into a modest inferiority for P; it would not overturn a claimed advantage. Neither the Abstract nor the Conclusions mentions decoys. (3) The Limitations section (line 249) already says: "Isomeric decoys were RepoRT compounds rather than candidate lists from structure databases, and no prospective, wet-laboratory NTS study was performed." (4) "No real NTS case study" is partly wrong. §4.10 validates on 37 real NORMAN NTS methods, including laboratory-calibrant/suspect screening (P coverage 0.851). A NORMAN decoy analysis has already been run but is not reported: results/raw/norman_decoys.parquet (555 rows). At α=0.10, P retained 0.841 of true suspects and eliminated 0.237 of decoys; J+ 0.863 and 0.201; GP 0.784 and 0.316. (5) Candidate filtering is §4.6, not §4.7. §4.7 is the ablation; the manuscript numbers NORMAN as §4.10, which fixes the count. (6) In experiment (b), "top-1/top-5 rank with and without RT filter" is undefined. Same-formula candidates have no ranking score without MS/MS or metadata, and the NORMAN data used here supply none. NORMAN. Report the existing results/raw/norman_decoys.parquet analysis in the Supplementary. Optionally replace the RepoRT isomers with PubChemLite same-formula candidates. The quarter-to-a-third elimination figures apply to RepoRT isomers.  There is no need to describe the NORMAN work as a missing real-NTS study. Present it as external validation without database candidate lists. |
