## Individual reviews

### R10_hostile_competitor — major revision

Hostile-competitor reading: the paper's own tables show that its practical conclusions can mostly be reached with simpler methods, and the component labelled 'Proposed' (the meta-learned difficulty normalisation) does not generalise beyond two source groups.

(1) The obvious simple competitor, a residual-multiplier window ±k·RMSE_LOO with k calibrated on public systems, is missing. Interpolating Table S6/S16 shows it is practically equivalent to the proposed method: about 0.90 coverage at a median width of about 4.65 min (proposed: 0.911 at 4.68 min), and the same 24.3 % decoy elimination at 91.4 % retention. The Gaussian window already beats the proposed method on the primary metric at n = 15 (paired p = 0.007). At the recommended n = 40 it is equally valid (0.903 vs 0.903).

(2) Single-laboratory (per-draw) coverage dispersion is nearly identical across methods at n = 40 (17–18 % of draws below 0.85). This result was computed but not reported; Table 2's per-system averages over 20 draws hide it.

(3) On the external NORMAN data, the Gaussian, GP and P-spline windows all had lower interval scores than the proposed method, but only the comparison with jackknife+ is reported.

(4) The efficiency gain of the normalisation comes from 2 of 5 source groups (Folberth, Souihi+Stoffel) and is pseudo-replicated in a 228-system Wilcoxon test. An unreported out-of-sample check shows that the 'long gradient' rule predicts gains that turn into losses on HILIC and NORMAN. The proposed method is also last at matched retention in decoy filtering.

(5) Several comparators are weakened: B6 uses a 0.01-min normaliser offset, B7 fixes its hyper-parameters instead of using them as a prior, B1 uses z instead of t, SASC is applied only to the conformal methods, and the fixed windows are mismatched to the base model's accuracy.

(6) The evaluation population favours the method: random calibrants come from the same pool as the test compounds, 73 % of which were seen in training, while both realistic calibrant designs fail.

The benchmark itself is valuable, but the claims need to be recast around the finite-sample guarantee of plain jackknife+, with fair simple baselines.

* **[critical] R10-1: Missing simplest competitor (±k·RMSE_LOO with k calibrated on public systems); the paper's own numbers imply practical equivalence with the proposed windows** (Manuscript Sections 4.1–4.2, Table 2, Abstract 'Significance', Conclusions; Supplementary Tables S4, S6, S13, S16, S25; code/src/conformrt/evaluation/sasc.py; confidence: high for the reported numbers and the n=15/n=10 IS reversal, which are read directly from the tables. Medium for the interpolated k≈1.8–1.9 equivalence: width is exactly linear in k, but coverage is only approximately linear between k=1.645 and k=2, so the experiment is needed to confirm it.)
  * Evidence: Table S6 (tables/table9_fixed_windows.md, n=20, α=0.10): the Gaussian ±1.645·RMSE_LOO window covers 0.885 at a median width of 4.248 min, and ±2·RMSE covers 0.919 at 5.165 min. Width is exactly linear in k (5.165/4.248 = 1.216 = 2/1.645). Linear interpolation gives three results. (a) k≈1.80 gives coverage ≈0.900 at a median width of ≈4.65 min; the proposed method gives 0.911 at 4.68 min. (b) k≈1.90 reaches the jackknife+ level of 0.909 at ≈4.90 min, narrower than jackknife+ itself (5.06 min). (c) For decoys (Table S13/S16), Gaussian at α=0.10 retains 0.891 and eliminates 0.282, and ±2·RMSE retains 0.925 and eliminates 0.225. At the proposed method's retention of 0.914, the interpolated elimination is 0.282 − 0.676×0.057 ≈ 0.243, identical to the proposed method (0.243). ±2·RMSE also has 0 % of systems below 0.85, the same as the proposed method, with coverage of 0.898/0.912/0.919/0.927/0.933 for n = 10–40. On the paper's primary efficiency metric (Table S4, tables/tableS3_paired_winkler_all_n_alpha.md), the Gaussian window beats the proposed method at n=15: median paired difference +0.467 min [0.247, 0.648], proposed better in only 37.7 % of systems, p=0.007. At n=10 the two are tied (−0.039 [−0.181, 0.161]). Mean IS at n=15 is 10.27 for the Gaussian window, 10.60 for ±2·RMSE, 11.28 for the proposed method and 12.28 for jackknife+. Section 4.1 mentions only the ±2·RMSE exception at n=15. The authors already have the machinery to learn k without test leakage: SASC is a global multiplier fitted on training systems, and for the random rule c is stable across folds (0.94–0.98, Table S25). They applied it only to the conformal methods.
  * Corrective action: Add three comparators: (a) ±k̂_α·RMSE_LOO, with k̂_α estimated per leave-source-out fold on the training systems by replaying random calibrant draws (the SASC code path); (b) plain jackknife, i.e. the empirical (1−α)(1+1/n) quantile of |LOO residuals| around the full-fit map; (c) ±t_{n−1}·RMSE_LOO. Then revise the Abstract, Highlights and Conclusions. The claim that conventional windows discard many correct structures applies to fixed windows and per-system GP/P-spline intervals, not to residual-based rules. State that the contribution of conformal windows over a calibrated residual rule is the finite-sample guarantee, not higher retention or better filtering on average. Also report the n=15 and n=10 reversals against the Gaussian window in the text.
  * Required experiment: Rerun the main RepoRT design (all n ∈ {10,15,20,30,40}, α ∈ {0.05,0.10,0.20}, the same 20 draws), HILIC and NORMAN (all three scenarios) with comparators (a)–(c). Report mean and per-draw coverage, median and mean width, mean IS, paired Wilcoxon against the proposed method and jackknife+, and decoy elimination at matched retention (Table S15 format).
* **[major] R10-2: Single-laboratory (per-draw) coverage is unreported and nearly identical across methods at the recommended n=40; Table 2 averages over 20 draws** (Manuscript Table 2, Section 4.1, Section 4.4 (Number of calibrants), Practical recommendations (i)–(iii), Abstract 'Significance' ('a design choice'); results/statistics.json revision1.draw_coverage; confidence: high (numbers taken directly from statistics.json and Table S2))
  * Evidence: results/statistics.json → revision1.draw_coverage (tables/tableS19_draw_coverage.md; this table is not in supplementary/table_index.csv) gives the share of single calibrant draws with realised coverage below 0.85. At n=20, α=0.10: proposed 17.3 %, jackknife+ 18.4 %, Gaussian 27.8 %. Below 0.80: 8.4 %, 9.1 % and 14.7 %. The 5th percentiles are 0.764, 0.759 and 0.727. At n=40, the shares below 0.85 are 16.9 % (proposed), 16.8 % (jackknife+) and 17.7 % (Gaussian); below 0.80, 5.8 %, 6.4 % and 6.2 %; and the 5th percentiles are 0.792, 0.788 and 0.788. Table S2 at n=40, α=0.10 gives mean coverage of 0.903 [0.900, 0.905] for the Gaussian window, 0.903 for the proposed method and 0.904 for jackknife+, with IS 8.77 for the Gaussian window vs 8.71 for jackknife+. Table 2's column 'Systems with coverage < 0.85 (%)' (proposed 0, Gaussian 4) uses per-system means over 20 draws, but a laboratory has only one calibrant set. As a result, recommendation (i) ('Do not use … parametric RT tolerances') conflicts with recommendation (ii) ('preferably about 40'). At n=40 the Gaussian window is as valid as the conformal ones at α=0.10; it differs only at α=0.05 (0.930) and α=0.20 (0.851).
  * Corrective action: Add per-draw 5th/10th percentiles and the shares below 0.85 and 0.80 to Table 2 and Fig. 2. Change 'a design choice' to wording that makes clear the guarantee is an expectation over calibrant draws, and give the realised spread for a single laboratory. Restrict recommendation (i) by n and α: parametric windows fail mainly for GP/P-spline, for n ≤ 20, and at α=0.05. If a per-laboratory guarantee is the selling point, evaluate a training-conditional (PAC-type) conformal variant.
  * Required experiment: No new runs are needed for the per-draw statistics; they already exist and only need reporting. Optionally, evaluate a training-conditional (calibration-conditional) conformal window at n=20 and 40 and report the share of draws below 1−α−0.05.
* **[major] R10-3: External (NORMAN) efficiency is reported selectively: the parametric windows had better interval scores than the proposed method** (Manuscript Section 4.10 (External validation), Table 4, Conclusions; Supplementary Table S22; confidence: high (read directly from Table S22))
  * Evidence: Table S22 (tables/table10_norman_external.md), α=0.10. Random compounds, n=20, mean IS: Gaussian 10.49, GP 10.70, jackknife+ 10.70, P-spline 10.73, proposed 11.15. The proposed method is the least efficient finite window apart from split conformal (12.18). Laboratory calibrant mix: P-spline 10.62, jackknife+ 10.70, Gaussian 10.77, GP 11.61, proposed 11.99. Here the Gaussian window has the same coverage as the proposed method (0.851 vs 0.851) and fewer severely under-covered systems (35.1 % vs 54.1 %). Random calibration chemicals, n=20: jackknife+ 11.07, Gaussian 11.36, P-spline 11.45, proposed 12.33, with coverage 0.849 (proposed) vs 0.845 (Gaussian). Section 4.10 reports only proposed vs jackknife+ ('11.14 versus 10.70 min'). The Conclusions present the external validation as support for the approach.
  * Corrective action: Report IS and median width for all comparators on NORMAN in the main text (extend Table 4 with an IS column or add a table). State explicitly that on external systems the meta-learned normalisation was the least efficient finite construction, and that the Gaussian window matched its coverage under the laboratory mix. Revise the Conclusions accordingly.
  * Required experiment: None beyond reporting. Add paired Wilcoxon tests (Holm) of the proposed method against every comparator on NORMAN for all three scenarios.
* **[major] R10-4: The gain from the meta-learned normalisation is source-specific and pseudo-replicated, and the 'long-gradient' rule fails out of sample in an unreported analysis** (Abstract (Results), Section 4.3 (Heterogeneity), Section 4.9, Practical recommendation (iii), Conclusions, Fig. 1 and title framing of 'Proposed'; Supplementary Tables S8, S15; results/statistics.json revision1.span_model and span_external; confidence: high for the per-fold pattern and the unreported span_external result. Medium on how strongly the source clustering would change inference; the cluster-level test is needed to quantify it.)
  * Evidence: Table S8 (tables/table11_fold_heterogeneity.md), median relative IS change vs jackknife+ by source: BGC +2.7 % (proposed better in 39 %, p=0.09); Folberth −18.2 % (95 %); PredRet +0.4 %; Souihi+Stoffel −10.1 %; Other +0.5 %. The overall p<0.001 comes from a Wilcoxon test over 228 systems in which 63 Folberth systems (one study, median 81 compounds, all MeOH) are treated as independent. statistics.json revision1.span_model gives a slope of −0.062 [−0.127, 0.0035], p=0.064 (not reported). revision1.span_external (tables/tableS21_span_external.md, not in the Supplement index) shows that the span model predicted a mean log-ratio of −0.090 (a gain) for HILIC and NORMAN. The observed values were +0.146 [0.107, 0.186] for HILIC, +0.029 [−0.022, 0.079] for NORMAN random and +0.093 [0.043, 0.143] for NORMAN pool, i.e. losses or no gain. Long-span systems had IS ratios of 1.13 (HILIC) and 1.01 (NORMAN reversed phase). In end-task terms (Table S15), the proposed method is last at matched retention: at 85 % it eliminates 32.9 % of decoys vs 33.4 % (jackknife+), 33.7 % (Gaussian), 34.5 % (GP) and 35.3 % (P-spline); at 90 % it is also below jackknife+ and the Gaussian window. Overall, the normalisation helps in 2 of 7 independent data collections. The Abstract ('improved efficiency for longer-than-median runs … −17 %') and recommendation (iii) rest on this.
  * Corrective action: Use cluster-level inference for proposed vs jackknife+: a random-effects meta-analysis of the per-source median log IS ratio over the 5 RepoRT sources plus HILIC and NORMAN, reporting τ² or I², or a cluster bootstrap over sources for the median paired difference. Report span_model and span_external in the Supplement. Remove the long-gradient recommendation, or label it explicitly as a hypothesis that failed external validation. Make plain jackknife+ the recommended default and present the meta-learned σ as an exploratory variant. Justify its cost (4-min base model, 3–5×10⁵ simulated residuals, inner cross-fitting) given that it brings no gain in decoy filtering.
  * Required experiment: Run a cluster-level test of the IS difference (sources as units) and a leave-one-source-out check: does the gain persist if the Folberth and Souihi+Stoffel systems are removed from both the difficulty-model training and the evaluation? Run a within-source split by RT span (long vs short within BGC, PredRet and Other) to separate the span effect from the source effect.
* **[major] R10-5: Weakened comparators: B6 with a 0.01-min normaliser offset, B7 with fixed (non-prior) hyper-parameters, and SASC applied only to conformal methods** (Section 2.7 (Comparator windows), Section 4.1, Section 4.2, Table 2, Table 4; code/src/conformrt/evaluation/system_eval.py (B6 block); experiments/run_metagp.py; confidence: high that the B6 offset and B7 fixed hyper-parameters are as described (verified in code). Medium that corrected versions would match the proposed method's efficiency; this is speculative until the experiment is run.)
  * Evidence: B6 in code/src/conformrt/evaluation/system_eval.py: `sd_te[i] = np.std(pt, axis=0) + 0.01; sd_cal[i] = np.std(pc) + 0.01`. With an offset of only 0.01 min, calibrants with near-zero ensemble spread produce exploding normalised scores. The result is a mean width of 26.8 min against a median of 9.97 min, and IS 28.99 (Table 2). Standard normalised conformal regression regularises the denominator (σ+β with β tuned; Papadopoulos 2011, cited). Even so, B6 has the best conditional coverage for unseen compounds (0.899 vs 0.835 for the proposed method; Table S9), for Tanimoto <0.4 (0.899 vs 0.848) and under common calibrants (0.902, the only valid method). B7 in experiments/run_metagp.py: 'The fixed hyper-parameters are then applied to every target system'. These are point estimates, not a prior updated with the target calibrants, so the noise variance cannot adapt to a system. Its coverage hardly responds to α: at n=40 it is 0.954/0.973/0.982 for nominal 0.80/0.90/0.95, with median widths of 7.7–11.8 min (51–78 % of the RT span, Table S2). Section 4.1 nevertheless generalises: 'Hyper-parameters that maximise the predictive likelihood across systems … neither place the interval at the requested level nor adapt it', and labels B7 as García et al.'s idea. Table 4 shows '–' for SASC with the Gaussian and GP windows.
  * Corrective action: Re-implement B6 with β = the median calibrant σ_ens, or β tuned on training systems. Re-implement B7 with a hyperprior and a per-system MAP or posterior update of the noise and amplitude from the target calibrants, plus a variant whose Gaussian quantile is meta-calibrated on training systems. Apply SASC to B1, B3 and B7 for all three selection rules. Rephrase the B7 conclusion as specific to this implementation and do not attribute the failure to the meta-learned-prior approach in general.
  * Required experiment: Rerun n ∈ {10,20,40}, α ∈ {0.05,0.10,0.20} with the corrected B6 and B7 on the identical draws. Run SASC (random, span and common rules) for B1, B3 and B7. Report coverage, per-draw dispersion, IS, and stratum coverage for unseen and low-similarity compounds.
* **[moderate] R10-6: The 'common practice' Gaussian comparator uses z instead of t_{n−1}, which inflates the reported deficit of residual-based windows** (Section 2.7 (B1), Section 4.1, Table 2; code/src/conformrt/evaluation/intervals.py; confidence: high that the code uses z. Medium for the interpolated coverage values.)
  * Evidence: code/src/conformrt/evaluation/intervals.py, gaussian_rmse: `z = stats.norm.ppf(1 - alpha / 2)`. Classical prediction intervals, as in PredRet-type projection (which the paper's own B2 mimics with t_{n−edf}), use a t quantile. t_{0.95,19} = 1.729 gives windows 5.1 % wider than z at n=20, and t_{0.95,9} = 1.833 gives 11.4 % wider at n=10. Interpolating Table S6 coverage linearly in k gives ≈0.893 at n=20 (vs 0.885) and ≈0.881 at n=10 (vs 0.862). The headline deficit of the residual window at n=20 would then shrink from 1.5 to about 0.7 percentage points.
  * Corrective action: Add ±t_{n−1}·RMSE_LOO, or replace B1 with it, and describe B1 accurately as a z-based rule. Discuss the remaining deficit in percentage points rather than as a qualitative failure of 'current practice'.
  * Required experiment: Recompute B1 with t_{n−1} for all n and α on the same draws (cheap; it reuses the existing LOO residuals).
* **[moderate] R10-7: The fixed-tolerance comparison is mismatched to the base model's accuracy; the fair 'larger fixed window' and the cited ±15 % were not tested** (Highlights 1; Section 4.1 (paragraph 2); Table 2; Conclusions; Supplementary Tables S6, S16; confidence: high that the comparison is mismatched. Medium on how well a learned fixed window would perform; per-system dispersion would likely remain large.)
  * Evidence: The literature tolerances quoted in the Introduction (±0.1–0.5 min, Metz et al.; ±0.40 min, Kretschmer et al.) come from system-specific or condition-aware models. Here they are applied to a transferred, condition-agnostic order model whose valid 90 % windows have a median width of 4.68 min, i.e. 35 % of the RT span (width_rel_median 0.350, Table S6). A ±1 min window then cannot be valid, so the result measures base-model error, not window construction. The Introduction cites ±15 % of RT (McEachran), but only ±5 % and ±10 % were evaluated. No fixed window with its width chosen on public data (absolute, or proportional to the span) was tested. Yet the Highlights ('Parametric and fixed RT windows lose correct structures') and the Conclusions ('fixed ±1 min windows kept only 71 %') headline this comparison.
  * Corrective action: Evaluate fixed windows whose width is learned on training systems to give 90 % average coverage: one global ±w, and one proportional to the calibrant RT span. Include ±15 % of the predicted RT. Either drop the fixed-window figure from the Highlights and Conclusions, or state that the deficit depends on the accuracy of the base model.
  * Required experiment: Run the SASC-style replay on the training systems to fit w (absolute and span-relative) per fold for α=0.10, then evaluate on the target systems: coverage, per-system share below 0.85, IS and decoy elimination.
* **[moderate] R10-8: The evaluation population favours the method: calibrants are drawn from the same pool as the test compounds, 73 % of which were seen in training** (Abstract (Results), Highlights 2 and 5, Sections 3.2, 4.5, 4.8, 4.10; Supplementary Table S9; confidence: medium. Pooled coverage for unseen compounds (0.905, proposed) is much higher than the average over systems (0.835), so the size of the bias depends on the weighting. The proposed experiment resolves this.)
  * Evidence: Section 3.2: '73 % of test compounds at n=20 were present in the base model's training data'. The difficulty model uses an exact-match 'seen' flag (Section 2.5). Coverage averaged over systems (Table S9) for unseen compounds is 0.835 (proposed), 0.798 (jackknife+) and 0.765 (Gaussian). For Tanimoto 0.4–0.7 it is 0.781 (proposed). In real NTS, calibrants are well-known commercial standards and candidates are mostly unseen database structures. Both of the paper's realistic emulations fail: commonly measured calibrants give 0.815, the NORMAN laboratory mix 0.851, and NORMAN random calibration chemicals 0.849. The headline 90.9–91.8 % in the Abstract and Highlights holds only for random draws from the same pool.
  * Corrective action: Add a co-primary RepoRT scenario that emulates NTS: calibrants drawn only from compounds seen by the base model, with the test set restricted to unseen compounds. Report it, with and without SASC, next to the random-draw headline. Put the unseen-compound coverage in the Abstract.
  * Required experiment: For n=20 and 40, α=0.10, 10 draws per system: draw calibrants from 'seen' compounds and test on 'unseen' compounds. Evaluate the proposed method, jackknife+, Gaussian (z and t), ±k̂·RMSE and B6 with corrected β. Repeat with SASC fitted by replaying the same rule on the training systems.
* **[moderate] R10-9: Methodological novelty is incremental: a textbook bound appears as a Highlight, the proof 'applies verbatim', and SASC is a scalar recalibration** (Highlights; Sections 1 (last paragraph), 2.4–2.6; supplementary/prior_art_novelty_report.md; confidence: medium (a judgement of novelty; no web prior-art search was performed in this review))
  * Evidence: Highlight 3, 'At least 1/α − 1 calibrants are needed for a finite window', is a standard property of conformal quantiles; Section 2.4 itself cites Angelopoulos & Bates for it. Section 2.4 states that 'the argument of Barber et al. … applies verbatim to the scaled score'. SASC (Section 2.6) is a single multiplicative factor fitted on training tasks. The authors' own prior-art report (supplementary/prior_art_novelty_report.md, 'Medium' risk) anticipates that 'an editor may view the work as "applying jackknife+ to RT"'. The component that is genuinely new, the meta-learned σ, has no end-task benefit and no external benefit (R10-3, R10-4).
  * Corrective action: Reposition the paper as a large-scale, leave-source-out benchmark of RT-window constructions for transfer, plus an application of jackknife+ with documented limits. Replace Highlight 3 with an empirical finding. Shorten Section 2.4 and cite the bound as known. Describe SASC as a known recalibration idea applied to RT transfer.
  * Required experiment: None.
* **[minor] R10-10: The interval score ranks invalid windows above corrected valid ones, so 'lowest mean interval score of all methods' is not evidence of practical superiority** (Sections 3.3 and 4.2, Table 2, Table 3; Supplementary Table S24; confidence: high (read directly from Tables 2 and S24))
  * Evidence: Table S24 (tables/table12_sasc.md), common-calibrant rule: the proposed method scores IS 10.45 at coverage 0.815, while proposed+SASC scores IS 11.09 at coverage 0.902. Jackknife+ scores 12.00 at 0.746 and jackknife+ with SASC 12.49 at 0.889. In Table 2, the GP window (coverage 0.822) has IS 9.51, below jackknife+ (0.909, IS 9.86). The metric therefore rewards under-coverage relative to the paper's stated goal of coverage control, yet Section 4.2 uses it to call the proposed windows the best of all methods.
  * Corrective action: Make efficiency at matched realised coverage the primary efficiency comparison (for example, width interpolated to coverage 0.90 per system, as is already done for decoys in Table S15), and use IS as a secondary metric. Avoid 'lowest of all methods' statements that mix valid and invalid constructions.
  * Required experiment: Per system, interpolate each method's width over α to realised coverage of 0.90, then compare these matched widths with paired tests (no new model runs needed if the per-α widths per system are available).

Verified previous fixes: Claim audit 'better than every fixed or residual-based tolerance at every n fails at n=15': Section 4.1 now names the ±2·RMSE exception at n=15. The fix is incomplete: the Gaussian ±z·RMSE window also beats the proposed method at n=15 (paired IS +0.467 min, p=0.007; Table S4), and this is not mentioned (see R10-1).; Claim audit 'All windows under-covered with common calibrants ignores B6': fixed. Section 4.7 now says 'All windows except the very wide jackknife+ normalised by ensemble spread (0.902 …)'.; Claim audit '634 decoys in total': fixed. Now reads '634 decoys per system and draw' (Section 4.6).; Claim audit 'late-eluting third': fixed. Now reads 'last third of the system's RT range (16 % of test compounds)'.; Claim audit 'Abstract range omits B6': fixed. The Abstract now gives 90.9 %–91.8 %.; Claim audit 'signature of heavy tails': fixed. Now reads 'consistent with heavy-tailed or heteroscedastic transfer residuals'.; Claim audit 'span–gain correlation confounded by source': partly addressed. Within-fold ρ and a fold-adjusted partial ρ of −0.16 were added. However, the long-gradient recommendation remains, and the authors' own out-of-sample check (span_external) and the span-model slope (p=0.064) are not reported (see R10-4).; Claim audit 'Introduction says openly available': fixed. The text now says the code is provided as Supplementary Material and will be deposited upon acceptance.

### R1_novelty — major revision

I tried to invalidate the core novelty claim: "to our knowledge, conformal windows for this few-calibrant transfer setting have not been developed or evaluated" (Final_Manuscript.md l.36). Searches run on 2026-11-16:
- PubMed: conformal[tiab] AND retention[tiab] gave 217 hits and conformal AND chromatograph* gave 20; none was an RT or RI method. The jackknife/distribution-free + RT/RI query, the meta-learning/few-shot + RT query and the prediction-interval + RT query gave only the already-cited García 2022 and PredRet.
- Europe PMC, including bioRxiv and ChemRxiv preprints: 0 hits for conformal + retention time/index.
- Crossref and three web searches (small-molecule LC RT, GC retention index, peptide RT): no conformal or distribution-free RT or RI window method found.

The core claim therefore survives. The methodological novelty, however, is presented as larger than it is, and the honesty about what is not new is incomplete:
1. The overall framework is already in the authors' own in-press Talanta paper (bhargav2026group, PubMed 43025002). That paper shows that classical intervals under-cover on new instruments at a nominal 0.90, calibrates out-of-group with diagnostic scaling, and states the 1/α−1 design rule. The manuscript cites it only in passing, and the cover letter does not disclose it.
2. The closest few-calibrant RT-interval method, García et al.'s meta-learned GP, is still not benchmarked. Addendum E, committed after the manuscript, says comparator B7 will be reported "whatever its result", yet the manuscript claims the only deviation from the plan is the B6 similarity term.
3. The "proposed" meta-learned normalisation is an instance of meta-learned cross-validation-based conformal prediction (Park 2024; Fisch 2021, which includes chemistry tasks). It is benchmarked only against the weak ensemble-spread normaliser.
4. Bach et al. 2018 is in the .bib file but never cited, although the base model is cross-system pairwise retention-order learning.
5. The headline "user-chosen rate" overstates the guarantee, which is ≥1−2α.
6. The SASC positioning ignores stronger hierarchical, group-level and task-level constructions, including the authors' own.

All of these can be fixed, mostly by disclosure, rewording and inexpensive extra comparators.

Housekeeping: during the searches I accidentally wrote a temporary file, Target_Journal_Submission/ids.txt, into the project. I verified its content and deleted it. No other repository file was touched.

* **[major] R1-N1: Close methodological precedent from the authors' own in-press work is cited only in passing and not disclosed; the 1/α−1 rule is presented as a contribution** (Final_Manuscript.md Highlights l.14; Introduction l.36; Section 2.4 l.59–61; Section 2.6 l.73–75; Results 'Number of calibrants' l.173; Cover_Letter.md 'Main findings' bullet 2 and 'Declarations'; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: PubMed 43025002 (Talanta 2026, doi:10.1016/j.talanta.2026.130941; citation key bhargav2026group; prior_art_novelty_report.md marks it as 'same author group'). Its abstract quotes:
- 'classical partial least squares (PLS) intervals and random-split conformal prediction under-cover new groups ... covered 0.78-0.83 ... at a nominal 0.90'
- 'We propose group-conformal calibration (GC-D), which computes conformity scores out-of-group with group-balanced weights and scales them by Hotelling T2 and Q residuals'
- 'Finite-sample group-level guarantees required at least 1/α - 1 calibration groups'
- 'no interval method rescued uncorrected instrument transfer, for which a few labelled samples of the new group ... are needed'
This is the same storyline as the present paper: parametric intervals under-cover on a new system → out-of-system calibration with a difficulty scale → context-dependent efficiency gain → 1/α−1 rule → labelled samples from the new system are required. The ACA manuscript nevertheless:
- cites the paper only as 'Conformal methods have recently entered analytical chemistry for spectroscopic calibration [@wiens2026calibrated], including under deployment shift [@bhargav2026group]' (l.36);
- makes 'At least 1/α − 1 calibrants are needed for a finite window' a Highlight (l.14);
- credits the rule only to the generic angelopoulos2023gentle (l.59–61, l.173);
- describes SASC's 'training systems, which are then the exchangeable units' (l.73) without relating it to GC-D's out-of-group calibration.
The cover letter calls 1/α−1 'a simple design rule' and its Declarations do not mention the related in-press paper. REVIEW/citation_audit.md l.144 already asked that the 1/α−1 statement acknowledge this paper. The current text does not, so that fix was not applied.
  * Corrective action: 1. Add a paragraph at the end of the Introduction that states explicitly what carries over from bhargav2026group:
   - the deployment-shift framing and under-coverage of classical intervals;
   - out-of-group (system-level) calibration;
   - diagnostic/difficulty scaling of conformity scores;
   - the 1/α−1 requirement.
2. In the same paragraph, state what is new here:
   - re-use of the same n calibrants for both the monotone mapping and the calibration, through leave-one-out maps (jackknife+);
   - a scale function meta-learned across chromatographic systems by simulating the calibrant protocol;
   - candidate-filtering evaluation with isomeric decoys;
   - RepoRT- and NORMAN-scale validation;
   - replay of the calibrant-selection rule.
3. Cite bhargav2026group where n ≥ 1/α−1 is stated.
4. Remove the 1/α−1 Highlight or recast it as known theory quantified for RT.
5. In the cover letter, name the in-press Talanta paper as related work by the same group and supply it to the editor, as Elsevier policy expects.
  * Required experiment: None for the disclosure. Optional: apply a GC-D-style out-of-group (system-level) conformal calibration as an additional comparator for the selection-rule scenarios (see R1-N5). This shows whether the RT-specific elements add anything beyond the group's existing approach.
* **[major] R1-N2: Closest prior few-calibrant RT-interval method (meta-learned GP, García et al. 2022) is still not benchmarked; headline GP numbers come from a weaker target-only GP; plan-deviation statement is now false** (Final_Manuscript.md Abstract l.21; Section 2.7 l.79; Section 3.2 l.104; Table 2; Conclusions l.252; Cover_Letter.md Main findings bullet 1; code/EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md Addendum E; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: - Abstract l.21 ('Gaussian-process ... windows retained the correct structure in 82.2 %'), Conclusions l.252 ('Gaussian-process intervals discarded the correct structure 1.8 times as often as intended') and the cover letter all report comparator B3.
- Section 2.7 (l.79) itself says B3 fits hyper-parameters 'on the target calibrants — unlike the GP projection of García et al. ..., whose prior is meta-learned on other chromatographic methods'.
- code/EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md Addendum E (commit 34ea26f, 2026-11-16 13:33, after manuscript commit a8ffd49 at 13:30) states that 'B3 alone could understate the strongest parametric alternative' and that 'B7 is reported in all main tables as an additional comparator, whatever its result'.
- No B7 or meta-GP entry exists in results/metrics.json, results/statistics.json or tables/. Experimental l.104 still says 'the only deviation from it is the omitted similarity term of B6'.
- B7 as specified (five kernel hyper-parameters of a constant + linear + RBF GP on standardised scores) is a surrogate, not García et al.'s method. REVIEW/citation_audit.md l.116 notes that their prior uses a meta-learned constant mean and a polynomial kernel, and that public code exists (github.com/constantino-garcia/cmmrt).
- The citation audit raised this point. The current fix (a wording change only) does not resolve it.
  * Corrective action: 1. Run and report B7 in Tables 2–4, S2–S4 and the coverage tests, as Addendum E commits.
2. Rename B3 in the Abstract, Highlights, Conclusions and cover letter to 'GP projection with target-only hyper-parameters'. Qualify the claim that parametric windows under-cover if B7 is near nominal.
3. Call B7 a 'simplified meta-learned GP', not García et al.'s method, unless their pipeline is run.
4. Update the plan-deviation sentence on l.104 to list Addendum E.
  * Required experiment: 1. B7 on the identical calibrant draws for n ∈ {10, 15, 20, 30, 40} and α ∈ {0.05, 0.10, 0.20}: coverage, width and interval score; paired Wilcoxon versus the proposed windows and jackknife+; one-sided coverage test.
2. García et al.'s cmmrt meta-learned projection (or a faithful re-implementation with a meta-learned mean and polynomial kernel), at least on the PredRet fold where it was originally validated, at n = 10 and 20.
3. A 'conformalised meta-GP' ablation: jackknife+ normalised by B7's predictive SD, computed leave-one-out. This separates the benefit of meta-learning from that of conformal calibration.
* **[moderate] R1-N3: 'Proposed' meta-learned normalisation is an instance of existing meta-learned cross-validation conformal prediction, and its added value is benchmarked only against a weak normaliser** (Final_Manuscript.md Section 2.4 l.63–65; Section 2.7 l.79 (B6); Table 2 and 3; Results 'Heterogeneity' l.158 and 'Generalisation' l.205; code/EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md l.55; confidence: high for the prior-art overlap; medium on whether simple normalisers would match the gain)
  * Evidence: - Section 2.4 (l.63) is headed 'Normalised jackknife+ with a meta-learned difficulty model (proposed)'. Table 2 and all Results call it 'Proposed'. The only acknowledgement of prior work (l.65) is that learning the scale on related tasks 'has precedents' [fisch2021fewshot; park2024fewshot].
- Park et al., TPAMI 2024 (doi:10.1109/tpami.2023.3327300; 'meta-XB'): meta-learns the nonconformity score of cross-validation-based conformal prediction (the jackknife+/CV+ family) for few-shot tasks with per-task validity, and extends it to adaptive scores.
- Fisch et al., ICML 2021: meta-learned conformal prediction over exchangeable auxiliary tasks, demonstrated on few-shot regression including computational-chemistry (drug-discovery) tasks.
- So the construction class, including its use in chemistry, exists. The S2 proposition is a direct corollary of Barber Thm 1, as l.65 itself says ('applies verbatim').
- The paper's own results show no efficiency gain in BGC (+2.7 %), PredRet (+0.4 %) and other sources (+0.5 %); a worse score in HILIC (16.70 vs 14.33 min); no gain on NORMAN (11.14 vs 10.70); and worse scores with calibration chemicals (Holm p = 0.02 and 0.01).
- The only alternative normaliser, B6 (ensemble spread; mean width 26.8 min), is weak, and its pre-registered nearest-neighbour similarity term (EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md l.55) was dropped.
- No standard non-meta-learned normalised score was compared, for example kNN-residual scaling (Papadopoulos 2011, cited on l.63) or a calibrant-geometry heuristic.
  * Corrective action: 1. State plainly that meta-learned, normalised cross-validation-based conformal prediction already exists (Park 2024), including on chemistry tasks (Fisch 2021).
2. State that the contribution is its instantiation for chromatographic transfer: system descriptors, calibrant-geometry features recomputed leave-one-out, and simulation of the calibrant protocol with inner cross-fitting.
3. Replace the label 'Proposed' with a descriptive name, for example 'J+ (meta-learned σ)'. The paper's own evidence supports plain jackknife+ as the default.
  * Required experiment: Add three non-meta-learned normalised jackknife+ comparators, each computed leave-one-out from the calibrants only, on the main draws (n = 10, 20, 40; α = 0.10):
(a) σ = 1 + extrapolation distance / score span + gap to the nearest calibrant score;
(b) kNN-based σ from calibrant leave-one-out residuals (Papadopoulos 2011);
(c) B6 with the pre-registered nearest-neighbour similarity term.
Report Table 2/3 metrics and per-fold changes. If (a) or (b) matches the meta-learned σ, the meta-learning claim must be withdrawn.
* **[moderate] R1-N4: Headline framing ('user-chosen rate', 'distribution-free') overstates a guarantee that is only ≥1−2α** (Title; Final_Manuscript.md Highlights l.13; Abstract l.19, l.23; Practical recommendations l.243; Conclusions l.252; Cover_Letter.md Main findings bullet 2; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: - Title: 'Distribution-free retention-time windows'.
- Highlight l.13: 'Conformal windows from representative calibrants keep a user-chosen retention rate'.
- Abstract l.19: 'RT windows that keep the correct structure at a user-chosen rate'.
- Significance l.23: 'make the probability of keeping the correct structure a design choice'.
- Cover letter: 'carry a distribution-free finite-sample guarantee'.
- Recommendation (ii) l.243: 'Measure at least 1/α−1 calibrants — 19 for a 95 % window'.
- In contrast, Theory l.59 and the S2 proposition guarantee only P ≥ 1−2α (≥0.80 for the nominal 0.90 windows). Coverage near 1−α is an empirical property that requires stability of the fit (Barber 2021), and stability is not examined for the isotonic + PCHIP + Theil–Sen map.
- The only construction with a 1−α guarantee is split conformal (B4), the least efficient one.
- A 1/α−1 design rule therefore guarantees 1−2α, not the nominal rate: 19 calibrants at α = 0.05 guarantee 90 % retention, not 95 %.
- Earlier audits fixed only the symmetry and stability wording in the Theory sentence (claim audit C14; citation audit l.115), not the headline claims.
  * Corrective action: 1. In the Abstract, Highlights and Conclusions, state that the finite-sample guarantee is ≥1−2α and that ≈1−α was observed empirically with random calibrants.
2. For users who need a guaranteed 1−α, give the jackknife+ setting α' = α/2. It requires n ≥ 2/α − 1 calibrants: 19 for a guaranteed 90 %, 39 for a guaranteed 95 %.
3. Correct recommendation (ii) accordingly.
  * Required experiment: No new runs are needed. Report from existing results:
- the cost of a guaranteed 90 % at n = 20, i.e. the α = 0.05 windows: median width 7.10 vs 4.68 min, coverage 0.958;
- the share of systems whose per-system coverage falls below 1−2α;
- a simple stability diagnostic of the monotone map, e.g. the distribution of max_i |g_{-i}(s) − g(s)| over candidates, to support the ≈1−α statement.
* **[moderate] R1-N5: Selection-aware calibration is a mean-over-systems (CDF-pooling) factor without a finite-sample guarantee; stronger existing constructions, including the authors' own, are neither discussed nor tested** (Final_Manuscript.md Section 2.6 l.73–75; Results 'Selection-aware system-level calibration' l.215; Table 4 SASC rows; Introduction l.38 (v); code/EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md Addendum D; confidence: medium-high)
  * Evidence: - Section 2.6 (l.74) defines c_ρ = min{c : (1/S) Σ_s cov_s(c) ≥ 1−α}. This is equal-weight pooling of per-system coverage curves: the CDF-pooling idea of Dunn et al. (2023, cited), without the finite-sample (S+1)/S correction.
- The paper reports that '21 % of the systems remained below 0.85 under the common-calibrant rule' (l.215) and concedes only 'average coverage' (l.75).
- Existing constructions give stronger control:
  - Fisch et al. 2021 provide (ε, δ) task-level validity, i.e. coverage ≥ 1−ε for a new task with probability ≥ 1−δ;
  - the authors' own GC-D (bhargav2026group) claims 'finite-sample group-level guarantees' with ≥1/α−1 groups.
- The Introduction (contribution v) and Abstract still present SASC as a proposal.
  * Corrective action: 1. Position SASC explicitly as hierarchical (CDF-pooling) calibration applied to a replayed selection rule, and credit Dunn 2023, Fisch 2021 and bhargav2026group.
2. Add the finite-sample (S+1)/S correction.
3. Offer a task-quantile variant that controls per-system coverage with a stated probability, since per-system shortfall is the practical problem the paper reports.
  * Required experiment: Use the existing cov_s(c) grids from Addendum D, so no new model training is needed:
1. For each training system, compute c_s = min{c : cov_s(c) ≥ 1−α}.
2. Set c_ρ^δ to the ⌈(1−δ)(S+1)⌉-th smallest c_s, for δ = 0.1 and 0.2.
3. Apply it to the 228 target systems under the random, span and common rules (n = 20, α = 0.10).
4. Report mean coverage, the share of systems below 0.90 and below 0.85, and the median width, compared with the current SASC.
* **[moderate] R1-N6: Cross-system pairwise retention-order learning is not credited to its originators; the novelty map wrongly says it is** (Final_Manuscript.md Introduction l.32; Section 2.2 l.49; manuscript/references.bib l.66–88; supplementary/prior_art_novelty_report.md 'Elements that are NOT new' and novelty item 3; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: - Section 2.2 (l.49) presents 'a system-independent score s(x) whose ordering agrees with the elution order observed within each training system ... XGBoost, pairwise objective with query groups = chromatographic systems' as the authors' design. It cites only kretschmer2026order (order conservation) and XGBoost.
- Bach et al. 2018 (Bioinformatics, doi:10.1093/bioinformatics/bty590, Crossref-verified: 'Liquid-chromatography retention order prediction for metabolite identification'; RankSVM trained on pairwise orders across multiple LC systems) introduced this approach.
- bach2018retention and bach2022joint are in manuscript/references.bib (l.66, l.77), but neither is cited anywhere in Final_Manuscript.md (grep finds no 'bach').
- supplementary/prior_art_novelty_report.md nevertheless lists 'Pairwise retention-order learning (Bach 2018; Kretschmer 2026)' under 'Elements that are NOT new (stated as such in the manuscript)'. That statement is false for Bach.
- The same report also says '239 RP target systems', against 228 in the manuscript.
- Bach 2022 (joint retention-order + MS/MS annotation) is an existing alternative to RT windows for candidate ranking, and the paper does not discuss it.
  * Corrective action: 1. Cite Bach 2018 in Introduction ¶2 and Section 2.2 as the origin of multi-system pairwise retention-order learning, and say that the XGBoost ranker is an implementation choice.
2. Cite Bach 2022 where the case for windows is made, noting that order-based joint scoring is an alternative.
3. Correct the novelty report (the Bach statement and 239 → 228), or do not ship it with the submission.
  * Required experiment: None.
* **[minor] R1-N7: Introduction frames few-calibrant RT transfer without the proteomics literature, where it is standard** (Final_Manuscript.md Introduction l.32 and l.36; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Introduction l.32 and l.36 describe transfer 'with the help of a small set of calibrant compounds', typically from 10–50 calibrants, citing small-molecule work only. Proteomics has used a few reference calibrants to transfer RT and set windows for over a decade:
- iRT: 11 reference peptides used to transfer RT across LC set-ups and schedule RT windows (Proteomics 2012, doi:10.1002/pmic.201100463; Crossref-verified);
- ELUDE: 'Training, Selection, and Robust Calibration of Retention Time Models for Targeted Proteomics' (J Proteome Res 2010, doi:10.1021/pr1005058; Crossref-verified), which recalibrates pretrained models to new conditions with few peptides;
- the Δt95% window convention, an empirical coverage window without a guarantee.
The manuscript already cites peptide-RT uncertainty work (maboudi2017uncertainty), so the omission is selective. My searches found no conformal peptide-RT method, so the conformal novelty is not affected.
  * Corrective action: Add one or two sentences with these citations. Present the small-molecule contribution as bringing a finite-sample guarantee to a transfer setting already well established in proteomics, whose windows (e.g. Δt95%) are empirical and carry no guarantee. Also soften 'none provided a way to control the loss' (l.34): Boswell 2015 attempted exactly that.
  * Required experiment: None.
* **[minor] R1-N8: Proposed remedy for structurally novel compounds ignores the applicability-domain solution in the closest prior work** (Final_Manuscript.md Results 'Conditional coverage' l.179; Limitations l.247; confidence: high on the omission)
  * Evidence: For under-covered novel structures (Tanimoto 0.4–0.7: coverage 0.781), the Results (l.179) suggest only 'calibrating them on a Mondrian partition by novelty [@vovk2013conditional]'. The closest prior work, ferrandoplo2026candidate (PubMed abstract), already proposes 'the detection of the applicability domain of the conformal predictive system based on maximum common-edge subgraph' distance and reports that it 'allows to maintain the guarantees on the minimum percentage of true structures prioritized'. The manuscript does not mention this in the conditional-coverage discussion or in the Limitations.
  * Corrective action: Cite the MCES/applicability-domain approach of ferrandoplo2026candidate as the existing remedy and contrast it with Mondrian calibration.
  * Required experiment: Optional but cheap, using the existing per-compound outputs at n = 20 and α = 0.10:
1. Flag candidates outside an applicability domain, e.g. max Tanimoto to the training set < 0.4, or MCES distance.
2. Report coverage and the share of candidates retained inside and outside the domain for the proposed windows and jackknife+.
* **[minor] R1-N9: Cover letter still carries the coverage range corrected in the Abstract** (manuscript/Cover_Letter.md 'Main findings' bullet 2; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Cover_Letter.md, Main findings bullet 2: 'Conformal windows ... retained 90.9 %–91.1 %, also for HILIC and, with randomly drawn calibrants, for the interlaboratory data.' Claim audit C06 (REVIEW/claim_evidence_audit.md l.68) rated this exact wording PARTIALLY_SUPPORTED, and the Abstract (l.21) was corrected to 90.9 %–91.8 % (HILIC 90.3 %–91.4 %; NORMAN 90.4 %–91.2 %). The fix was not carried into the cover letter.

The same remark is resolved to 0.708 in Supplementary_Material.md (l.57). The review packet lists the standalone file as a supplementary deliverable.
