Verified previous fixes: Fisch et al. 2021 (ICML) and Park et al. 2024 (TPAMI) are now cited for meta-learned conformal prediction (citation_audit l.93; Final_Manuscript.md l.65). The citations are present, but the acknowledgement is too weak (see R1-N3).; Boswell et al. 2015 (projection-specific tolerance windows with stated confidence, relaxed by 50 % across seven labs) is now cited and contrasted in Introduction ¶3 (citation_audit l.174; l.34). Verified.; B3 is now explicitly distinguished from García et al.'s meta-learned GP (citation_audit l.116; l.79). The wording fix is verified, but the comparator is still missing even though Addendum E commits to it, so the fix is inadequate (R1-N2).; Papadopoulos et al. 2011 is cited for normalised nonconformity scores (citation_audit l.153; l.63). Verified.; The symmetry condition and the stability qualifier were added to the jackknife+ guarantee sentence (claim audit C14; citation_audit l.115; l.59). Verified, but the headline framing still overstates the guarantee (R1-N4).; Highlight C01 was reworded to 'from representative calibrants' (l.13). Verified; the remaining 1−2α overstatement is R1-N4.; Abstract coverage ranges were corrected (claim audit C06; l.21). Verified in the manuscript but not carried into the cover letter (R1-N9).; The 'typically from 10–50 calibrants' statement is now supported by citations (citation_audit l.178; l.36). Verified.; The request to acknowledge bhargav2026group where n ≥ 1/α−1 is stated (citation_audit l.144) was NOT implemented: l.59–61 and l.173 cite only angelopoulos2023gentle (R1-N1).

### R2_methodology — major revision

The core mathematics and the implementation agree. The jackknife+ indices are k_u = ceil((1-α)(n+1)) and k_l = floor(α(n+1)), and in floating point k_l = n+1-k_u for every n from 4 to 40 and α in {0.05, 0.10, 0.20}. The finite-window condition n ≥ 1/α - 1 and both proof steps in S2 (the deterministic step and the tournament step, which bounds the set to fewer than 2α(n+1) points) are correct. The difficulty-scale code also matches the proof: geometry_loo recomputes pos, extrap, gap, cal_span and n_cal on each leave-one-out set, _sigma_matrix builds its rows in the right order, and the diagonal gives σ_{-i}(x_i). The map (isotonic + PCHIP + Theil-Sen) treats calibrants symmetrically. The base and difficulty models never see the target's source, and t0 comes from metadata (column.t0). The Winkler code matches the stated formula. A simulation using the repository's own functions and a calibrant-dependent σ gave 0.908 coverage at nominal 0.90. The problems lie in what is claimed, how ablations are labelled, and how comparators are built. (1) The distribution-free guarantee is ≥1-2α (0.80 at the nominal 90 %). Yet the Highlights, Abstract, ablation text, Conclusions and Recommendation (ii) present the nominal 1-α as guaranteed, and the minimum for a finite window is mistaken for the minimum for the nominal level. (2) S2 Remark (2) blames the 0.708 coverage of the 'no leave-one-out refit' ablation on not refitting the scale. In the code, that ablation uses in-sample residuals of the full map. Refitting σ alone is never ablated, and dropping only the σ refit made windows more conservative in a simulation. (3) The paper says an under-covering method cannot score well on the interval score; Table 2 contradicts this. (4) The selection-aware calibration (SASC) is a plug-in threshold with no finite-sample correction. It assumes systems are exchangeable, which the leave-source-out design itself denies, yet the Limitations call it a guarantee. (5) The P-spline comparator keeps the leverage at the boundary value when extrapolating. (6) The ensemble-normalised comparator (B6) uses an undisclosed 0.01-min floor. Minor points concern the logic of the validity test, unit-test coverage, and how the small-n discreteness results are worded. The headline conclusions stand (good empirical coverage with random calibrants, a gain over jackknife+ that also holds on a scale-free log-ratio, p = 1.7e-8). However, several claims, one Highlight and one practical recommendation have to be corrected. Several comparator and ablation runs must be redone.

* **[major] R2-1: The guarantee is only ≥1-2α, but Highlights, Abstract, Recommendations and the ablation text present the nominal 1-α as guaranteed; the minimum n for a finite window is mistaken for the n that guarantees the nominal level** (Final_Manuscript.md (edit the templates in manuscript/src/): Highlights l.13-14; Abstract l.19-23; §2.4 l.59-61; §4.1 l.127; §4.2 l.154; §4.4 l.173; §4.7 l.191; §4.14 l.243; Conclusions l.252; supplementary/S2_coverage_proof.md; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: S2 Proposition and §2.4 (Final_Manuscript.md l.59) prove only P{t∈C_α} ≥ 1-2α, i.e. 0.80 at α=0.10 and 0.90 at α=0.05. The text claims more in several places. Highlights l.13: 'Conformal windows ... keep a user-chosen retention rate'. Abstract l.19/23: 'keep the correct structure at a user-chosen rate' and 'make the probability ... a design choice'. §4.7 l.191: 'marginal coverage was unchanged, as guaranteed'; the theorem only guarantees ≥0.80, so an unchanged ~0.91 does not follow from it. §4.1 l.127: 'keep the promised error rate'. Conclusions l.252: 'restored the promised rate'. Recommendation (ii) l.243, 'Measure at least 1/α-1 calibrants — 19 for a 95 % window', and Highlight l.14 also conflate the two minima. At n=19 and α=0.05, k_u = ceil(0.95·20) = 19 = n, so the window is finite, but its distribution-free guarantee is 0.90, not 0.95. The clause 'close to 1-α when the fitted map is stable' (l.59) has no stability diagnostic behind it. The stability results of Barber et al. 2021, Sect. 5, need leave-one-out stability, which is not examined for isotonic+PCHIP with Theil-Sen extrapolation at n=10-20. Width comparisons also mix guarantee levels. §4.2 l.154 calls the proposed windows (4.68 min) the narrowest among valid methods, ahead of split conformal (7.04 min). But split conformal (B4) has an exact ≥1-α guarantee, while the proposed window has only ≥1-2α. The proposed window that carries a distribution-free ≥0.90 guarantee is its α=0.05 version: median 7.10 min at n=20 (Table S2), about 52 % wider.
  * Corrective action: (1) In the Abstract, Highlights and Conclusions, state that the distribution-free guarantee at level α is ≥1-2α, and that ~1-α coverage is an empirical result for random calibrants on RepoRT/NORMAN. (2) Delete 'as guaranteed' (l.191) and 'promised rate'. (3) Split Recommendation (ii) and Highlight 3 into two statements. A finite window needs n ≥ 1/α-1. A distribution-free ≥1-α window needs jackknife+ run at α/2, which requires n ≥ 2/α-1 (19 calibrants for 90 %, 39 for 95 %). (4) In §4.2, say that split conformal is the only compared construction with a ≥1-α guarantee. Compare it with the proposed window at α/2 (7.10 vs 7.04 min) as well as with the window at α. (5) Back the ≈1-α statement with a leave-one-out stability diagnostic.
  * Required experiment: No new model runs are needed for (1)-(4). Tabulate, for n∈{20,30,40}, the proposed and jackknife+ windows at α/2=0.05 as 'guaranteed-90 %' windows, next to split conformal at α=0.10 (coverage, median width, IS). For (5), report per system and draw at n∈{10,20} the distribution over test compounds of max_i|g_{-i}(s(x))-g(s(x))| divided by the window half-width. Optional: run full conformal prediction (exact ≥1-α) with the same monotone map and leave-one-out σ on a random subset of at least 50 systems.
* **[moderate] R2-2: The 'no leave-one-out refit' ablation is mislabelled in S2: it uses in-sample residuals of the full map, so it does not test the per-set σ recomputation that S2 Remark (1) and §2.5 justify; dropping only the σ refit is expected to widen the windows** (supplementary/S2_coverage_proof.md Remark (2), l.17; Supplementary_Material.md l.57 and the Table S17 row 'no leave-one-out refit'; Final_Manuscript.md §4.7 l.191 and Fig. 6; code/src/conformrt/evaluation/system_eval.py l.144-152; confidence: high for the mislabelling; medium for the direction on real data (based on simulation))
  * Evidence: In system_eval.py l.146-152 the ablation computes sf_cal = _sigma_matrix(..., loo=False) and Rn = np.abs(y_cal - full.predict(s_cal)) / sf_cal, and builds the window as mu_full_te ± q*sf_te. These are in-sample residuals of the full MonotoneMap combined with a full-set σ. Manuscript l.191 describes this correctly as 'in-sample residuals of the full calibrant fit'. S2 Remark (2) (S2_coverage_proof.md l.17; Supplementary l.57) instead says: 'Using the scale computed from the full calibrant set for the calibrants' own residuals (the "no leave-one-out refit" ablation) breaks the symmetry ...; empirically, its mean coverage ... was 0.708'. Isotonic regression leaves exactly zero in-sample residuals for singleton blocks. I checked this with the repository functions (MonotoneMap, geometry, geometry_loo, jackknife_plus) on 300 synthetic systems with n=20, α=0.10, heteroscedastic t4 noise and a σ that depends on extrap and gap (script: /tmp/claude-0/-home-user-p-c1-5/aba5d053-3588-58c2-8bff-15bea55362ed/scratchpad/check_r2.py). Proper normalised jackknife+ covered 0.908. Using leave-one-out map residuals but σ(x_i; D_n) for the calibrants (σ not refitted) covered 0.932. The code's 'naive' variant covered 0.769, and 23.8 % of its in-sample residuals were exactly 0. The direction is expected: with full-set geometry a calibrant has gap=0 and extrap=0, so its σ is underestimated, R_i is inflated and the windows widen. The sentence 'an empirical confirmation that the leave-one-out construction is what makes the guarantee hold' therefore only confirms the textbook fact that in-sample residuals are invalid.
  * Corrective action: Rename the variant to 'in-sample residuals (map and scale fitted on all calibrants)' in Table S17 and Fig. 6, and rewrite S2 Remark (2) to match. Add a separate σ-only ablation and report it. In §4.7, limit the conclusion to the map refit unless the σ-only ablation shows a coverage loss.
  * Required experiment: Run on the 228 RP systems (n=20, α∈{0.05,0.10,0.20}, 20 draws) a variant that keeps the leave-one-out map but uses the full-set scale: R_i=|t_i-g_{-i}(s_i)|/σ(x_i;D_n), with test scales σ_{-i}(x) in one version and σ(x;D_n) in another. Report coverage, median width, IS, and coverage inside and outside the calibrant range.
* **[moderate] R2-3: The stated property of the interval score is false, and Table 2 contradicts it** (Final_Manuscript.md §3.3 Metrics l.110-112; Table 2 l.131-146; §4.2 l.154; Table 3; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Metrics l.112 says the interval score is 'a proper score that rewards narrow windows but penalises misses, so that an under-covering method cannot appear efficient merely by being narrow'. Table 2 (l.134-138) shows otherwise. The GP PI (coverage 0.822) has a mean IS of 9.51 min and the Gaussian ±z·RMSE window (0.885) has 9.65 min; both beat jackknife+ (0.909) at 9.86 min. The P-spline (0.851) at 9.92 min is about level with jackknife+. The interval score (Gneiting & Raftery 2007) is proper for the central α/2 and 1-α/2 quantiles and penalises a miss only linearly, with slope 2/α, so an under-covering interval can score better than a valid one. This weakens the use of IS superiority over the Gaussian and GP windows (Table 3) as evidence for the proposed windows beyond coverage. A central-quantile score also penalises symmetric |residual| windows when errors are skewed (55 % of misses elute late, §4.11). I checked that the paired gain over jackknife+ is not an RT-scale artefact: on a scale-free log-ratio it is a median of -6.8 % (Wilcoxon p = 1.7e-8; /tmp/claude-0/-home-user-p-c1-5/aba5d053-3588-58c2-8bff-15bea55362ed/scratchpad/is_scale.py).
  * Corrective action: Replace the sentence with an accurate description: the IS is proper for the central quantile pair and trades width against misses at rate 2/α; it does not certify coverage. Interpret IS rankings only among methods that reach nominal coverage, and state explicitly that the GP and Gaussian windows beat jackknife+ on IS despite under-covering. Report the paired log-ratio analysis as a scale-free secondary endpoint.
  * Required experiment: Re-analysis only: add Wilcoxon results on per-system log(IS_P/IS_comparator) to Table 3 and Table S8.
* **[moderate] R2-4: SASC has no finite-sample guarantee, even on average, and assumes systems are exchangeable although the leave-source-out design denies it; the Limitations nevertheless call it a 'guarantee'** (code/src/conformrt/evaluation/sasc.py l.44-48; code/experiments/run_sasc.py l.70-91; Final_Manuscript.md §2.6 l.71-75, §4.10 l.215, Limitations l.247, Highlights l.15; confidence: high for the theory; medium for the numerical impact, which is likely small for S ≈ 200)
  * Evidence: sasc.py l.44-48, choose_c: 'Smallest c with mean coverage >= 1-α'. This is a plug-in empirical threshold with no (S+1) correction, and it silently falls back to grid[-1]=6.0 if the target is never reached. Manuscript §2.6 l.73-75 says the training systems 'are then the exchangeable units, as in conformal inference for hierarchical data [dunn2023; lee2026]'; the cited methods include finite-sample corrections, but SASC does not. Limitations l.247 says SASC 'gives an average-over-systems, not a per-system, guarantee', and Highlight l.15 says 'known selection rules are corrected on average'. In run_sasc.py l.75-85, c for fold f is learned on systems from the other four sources and applied to the held-out source. The two populations also differ: training systems need ≥40 compounds and are scored by models excluding folds {f,g}, whereas targets need ≥60 compounds and are scored by models excluding only {f}. Sources are demonstrably not exchangeable: the per-fold change in IS ranges from -18 % to +3 % (Table 3).
  * Corrective action: Use conformal risk control (Angelopoulos et al., ICLR 2024): c_ρ = inf{c in grid : (S·mean_s miscov_s(c) + 1)/(S+1) ≤ α}. Both window ends are monotone in c because every term σ_{-i}(x)R_i ≥ 0, and miscov_s lies in [0,1]. Hence E[miscoverage of a new system exchangeable with the S systems] ≤ α. State the system-level exchangeability assumption explicitly and note that the leave-source-out evaluation violates it. Raise an error when the grid saturates. Replace 'guarantee' with 'average-coverage control under system exchangeability'.
  * Required experiment: Rerun run_sasc.py with the conformal-risk-control threshold; for S ≈ 191-241 and α=0.10 the required mean training coverage becomes about 0.905. Report c and target-system coverage per fold, and the spread across folds of the realised mean coverage under SASC.
* **[moderate] R2-5: The P-spline comparator (B2) keeps the leverage at its calibrant-boundary value, so its 'classical prediction interval' does not widen under extrapolation** (code/src/conformrt/models/mapping.py l.77-84 and 115-126; Final_Manuscript.md §2.7 l.79, Abstract l.21, Table 2; confidence: high for the code behaviour; medium for the size of the impact (H1 holds inside the range regardless))
  * Evidence: In mapping.py, PSplineMap._basis (l.82) uses sc = np.clip(s, self.lo, self.hi). predict_interval (l.121-126) computes h(x) from this clipped basis, while predict (l.115-119) extrapolates the mean linearly with the Theil-Sen slope. A check with the repository class on 20 calibrants over [0,5] gave the same 90 % half-width, 0.750, at s_max, s_max+1 and s_max+3. Table S9 (Supplementary l.498-499) shows B2 coverage of 0.869 inside versus 0.676 outside the calibrant range; at n=20, 9.5 % of test compounds are outside it. §2.7 l.79 describes B2 as the classical interval t̂ ± t σ̂√(1+h(x)), which is not what is computed for out-of-range x. The Abstract's headline figure for smoothing splines (85.1 %) therefore partly reflects this implementation choice.
  * Corrective action: Compute h(x) for out-of-range compounds with a basis extended linearly beyond the boundary knots (natural-spline tails), consistent with the linear mean extrapolation. Alternatively, disclose the clipping and cite B2's in-range coverage (0.869) as the evidence for H1. Update the Abstract and Table 2 if the numbers change.
  * Required experiment: Rerun B2 at n∈{10,20,40} and α∈{0.05,0.10,0.20} with extrapolated leverage; report overall and inside/outside-range coverage, width and IS.
* **[moderate] R2-6: B6 uses an undisclosed, untuned 0.01-min floor, and the resulting heavy-tailed windows are used to argue that 'the choice of the scale function matters'** (code/src/conformrt/evaluation/system_eval.py l.121-132; Final_Manuscript.md §2.7 l.79, §4.2 l.154, Tables 2-3; confidence: medium)
  * Evidence: system_eval.py l.130 sets sd_te[i] = np.std(pt, axis=0) + 0.01 and sd_cal[i] = np.std(pc) + 0.01, then Rens = np.abs(loo_res) / sd_cal. Neither §2.7 (l.79) nor EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md (l.55, 78) mentions the constant. When the five replicate maps nearly agree at a calibrant, R_i ≈ |r_i|/0.01 min, and this is then multiplied by the larger spread at test compounds. The symptoms are visible: mean width 26.8 min against a median of 9.97 min, and mean IS 28.99 against 9.86 for jackknife+ (Table 2, §4.2 l.154); Table 3 shows a gap of -5.26 min in favour of the proposed method on 99 % of systems. Normalised nonconformity scores need a sensitivity parameter β in σ+β (Papadopoulos et al. 2011, already cited). For the proposed method this role is effectively played by the log(|r|+0.01) target and the meta-learning.
  * Corrective action: Disclose β = 0.01 min. Choose β for B6 on the training folds, with the same leave-source-out logic used for the difficulty model (e.g. the median replicate spread over training-system calibrants), and report a β sensitivity analysis. Reword §4.2 to 'an untuned ensemble-spread normalisation'.
  * Required experiment: Rerun B6 at n=20, α=0.10 (and at n=10 and 40) with β ∈ {0.01 min, 0.1 min, median training spread}; report coverage, mean and median width, and IS.
* **[minor] R2-7: H2 is declared 'supported' because H0 (coverage ≥ 1-α) was not rejected** (Final_Manuscript.md l.116 and l.127; Table S3; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: §3.4 l.116 and §4.1 l.127: 'The pre-specified one-sided test found no evidence of under-coverage ... (p = 1.00 for the proposed windows) ... These results support ... (H2)'. Failing to reject H0 (mean coverage ≥ 1-α) cannot establish validity. The actual supporting evidence is the CI lower bound (0.908 > 0.90).
  * Corrective action: Base the validity statement on the CI lower bound, or on a non-inferiority test stated in advance (H0: coverage ≤ 1-α-δ, e.g. δ = 0.01) declared as a deviation from the plan. Keep the pre-specified p-value as secondary.
  * Required experiment: Re-analysis only: report a non-inferiority p-value per method from the existing per-system coverages.
* **[minor] R2-8: The coverage unit test for the normalised construction uses a fixed scale and never exercises the calibrant-dependent σ_{-i} code path** (code/tests/test_core.py l.44-65; code/src/conformrt/evaluation/system_eval.py l.16-52; S2_coverage_proof.md Remark (5); confidence: high)
  * Evidence: In test_core.py, _coverage('njk+') uses sig_c = 0.2 + 0.3 * sc, a fixed function of x. No test runs _sigma_matrix(loo=True), _sigma_cal_loo (the diagonal extraction, system_eval.py l.49-52) or the n_cal = n-1 input end to end. S2 Remark (5) and §3.6 l.120 nevertheless present the test as a check of the method. geometry_loo is tested against brute force (l.33-41), and my simulation with a geometry-dependent σ gave 0.908, so no bug is suspected.
  * Corrective action: Add a test that (a) builds σ_{-i}(x_j) through _sigma_matrix and _sigma_cal_loo with a stub model whose sigma() depends on every geometry column, and checks it against brute-force recomputation on D_{-i}; and (b) checks by Monte Carlo that coverage is ≥ 1-2α (and close to 1-α) with this data-dependent σ.
  * Required experiment: Unit test only (about 300 synthetic systems, n=20, α=0.10).
* **[minor] R2-9: Small-n windows at α=0.20 are called 'valid' although the discrete quantile index makes them strongly conservative, and the calibrant minimum is misquoted** (Final_Manuscript.md §4.4 l.173; Table S5; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: §4.4 l.173: 'At α=0.20, for which eight calibrants suffice, the proposed windows were valid (0.907 at n=8) while GP intervals covered 60 %'. The minimum is n ≥ 1/α-1 = 4, not 8. At n=8, k_u = ceil(0.8·9) = 8 = n and k_l = floor(1.8) = 1, so the window spans the extreme leave-one-out bounds. Its effective level is about k_u/(n+1) = 0.889, and the observed 0.907 is 10.7 points above the nominal 0.80. The comparison with GP (0.60) therefore sets a de facto 89 % window against a nominal 80 % interval.
  * Corrective action: Write 'four calibrants suffice'. Report k_u/(n+1) next to coverage for every (n, α) in Tables S2 and S5, and call these windows 'conservative' rather than 'valid'.
  * Required experiment: None (re-tabulation).

Verified previous fixes: Claim audit C14 (symmetry condition missing): fixed adequately. §2.4 l.59 now reads 'for any mapping algorithm that treats the calibrants symmetrically, as the monotone map does'. MonotoneMap is symmetric: sklearn pools tied scores, Theil-Sen is a median of pairwise slopes, and knots follow sorted scores.; Plan addendum A (B6 leave-one-out bug): fixed adequately. In system_eval.py l.121-132 the replicate maps are refitted without calibrant i for both the test-compound and the calibrant spreads. The undisclosed 0.01-min floor is a separate issue (R2-6).; Claim audit C15 (normalised jackknife+ implementation): confirmed. geometry_loo recomputes pos, extrap, gap and cal_span for each leave-one-out set and is tested against brute force (test_core.py l.33-41). _sigma_matrix builds the (n·m) design in the right row order (ravel, repeat and tile checked), and diag() gives σ_{-i}(x_i). k_u and k_l agree in floating point (k_l = n+1-k_u) for every n from 4 to 40 and α in {0.05, 0.10, 0.20}.

### R3_experimental_design — major revision

Much of the design is sound. Leave-source-out folds keep the target source out of every model. All comparators use identical seeded calibrant draws. B1, B4, B5, B6 and P share the base score and the monotone map. The system is the unit of analysis. The random-draw coverage figures can be recomputed from results/raw/main_rows.parquet. Several new problems remain, and they can be checked:
(1) The record of the post hoc comparator B7 (Addendum E) is contradicted by the repository. The amendment says the first run "was stopped before its evaluation stage", but the commit that adds the amendment also adds that run's complete target evaluation: 68,400 rows (228 systems × 5 n × 20 draws × 3 α), with variances at the exp(3) bound. B7 is also missing from the reviewed manuscript, although the plan promises to report it "whatever its result". The first run over-covers heavily: 0.974 at a nominal 0.90.
(2) The "longer-than-median runs" efficiency claim appears in the Abstract, the recommendations and the Conclusions. It is an unlabelled post hoc median split, and it reverses on the independent NORMAN methods: for long methods, P versus jackknife+ changes the interval score by +4.5 % to +8.6 %.
(3) SASC was designed after the common-calibrant failure and is validated only on that same scenario. The one other test (the NORMAN mix) failed. The common rule ranks compounds by frequencies counted over all RepoRT systems, including the held-out fold.
(4) Several comparators are weaker than defensible versions: B6 (5 replicates, 0.01-min floor), B3 (permissive ML-II bounds), the uncalibrated fixed-window grid, and RepoRT-only decoys, which favour the novelty-aware P.
(5) The efficiency tests treat 228 clustered systems as independent, although the gain is confined to 2 of 5 sources.
(6) Post hoc labels are missing from the Abstract, Highlights and Conclusions.
(7) The repository is not in a reproducible, committed state.
None of this invalidates the central point: under exchangeability, conformal windows hold coverage while parametric windows are miscalibrated. But the efficiency, selection-correction and practical-recommendation claims need re-analysis and relabelling.

* **[critical] R3-1: Addendum E amendment contradicts the repository: the first B7 run was fully evaluated and committed; the promised comparator B7 is missing from the manuscript** (code/EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md Addendum E and amendment; git commit 38de8b5; results/raw/metagp_rows.parquet, metagp_theta.csv, metagp_check.csv; experiments/run_metagp.py lines 31, 149-156; Final_Manuscript.md Sections 2.7, 3.2, Tables 2-4; code/run_all.py; confidence: high (verified from git history, file timestamps and file contents))
  * Evidence: EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md, Addendum E: "B7 is reported in all main tables as an additional comparator, whatever its result"; Amendment: "(2026-11-16, before any target-system result of B7 was computed or inspected) ... the bounds were widened ... and the run was restarted; the first run was stopped before its evaluation stage." Commit 38de8b5 (13:40:44, message "... before any B7 evaluation") adds three files. (a) results/raw/metagp_rows.parquet: 68,400 rows = 228 systems × n∈{10,15,20,30,40} × 20 draws × 3 α, the complete target evaluation. run_metagp.py writes it (line 156) only after evaluate_target has run for every target system (line 149). (b) metagp_check.csv: 228 systems. (c) metagp_theta.csv: vc = vl = vr = 20.0855 = exp(3) in all five folds, i.e. the bound-limited first run. All three files are time-stamped 13:40:23, 21 s before the commit. First-run B7 at n = 20 (my recomputation, per-system means): coverage 0.983/0.974/0.955 at nominal 0.95/0.90/0.80; at α = 0.10, median width 11.17 min and mean interval score 13.20 min (P: 4.68 and 9.07). Final_Manuscript.md (built 13:29) contains no B7, and Section 3.2 still says "the only deviation from it is the omitted similarity term of B6". The uncommitted template edits (02_theory.md and 03_experimental.md, 13:41) already describe B7 as post hoc. run_all.py (lines 42-44) runs NORMAN and SASC but not run_metagp.py.
  * Corrective action: 1. Correct the amendment text: the first run (variance bounds exp(3)) completed its target evaluation, and that evaluation was committed before the bounds were changed. 2. Report both B7 runs (exp(3) and exp(9) bounds) in Tables 2-4 and S2-S4, as Addendum E commits to. 3. Replace the "only deviation" sentence with a deviation table. 4. Add run_metagp.py to run_all.py and rebuild the manuscript from the current templates. 5. Because the meta-learned GP over-covers at every α, rephrase H1, the Abstract and the Conclusions from "parametric windows under-cover" to "parametric windows are miscalibrated, in a direction that depends on how their hyper-parameters are obtained".
  * Required experiment: Complete the exp(9)-bound B7 run on the identical draws (all n and α). Report both runs with the pre-specified statistics: paired Wilcoxon with Holm correction, and the one-sided coverage test. If B7 is to appear in Table 4, also evaluate it under the RT-spanning and commonly-measured calibrant rules and on NORMAN.
* **[major] R3-2: The 'long-gradient' efficiency claim in the Abstract and recommendations is an unlabelled post hoc median split and reverses on the independent NORMAN data** (Abstract; Section 4.3; Section 4.13 (iii); Conclusions; Table 3 last column; Table S8; confidence: high for the missing label and the NORMAN reversal (recomputed from the raw files); medium for interpretation, because NORMAN spans are spans of 82 compounds)
  * Evidence: Abstract: "Normalisation improved efficiency for longer-than-median runs (median interval-score change versus jackknife+ −17 %) but not for shorter runs". Section 4.3 splits at the sample median of 16.8 min (−16.8 % versus +2.3 %). Recommendation (iii): "the meta-learned normalisation is worthwhile for long reversed-phase gradients". Conclusions: "more efficient in long reversed-phase gradients". Neither the RT-span covariate nor the median split appears in EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md or any addendum. The analysis entered with commit d354c1e ("Main results analysed; ... heterogeneity and matched-retention analyses"), i.e. after the results were seen. Per-fold medians of the relative interval-score change, P versus J+ (main_rows, n = 20, α = 0.10): BGC +2.7 %, Folberth −18.2 %, PredRet +0.4 %, Souihi+Stoffel −10.1 %, other sources +0.5 %; the fold-adjusted partial ρ is only −0.16. Independent check on NORMAN (norman_rows, α = 0.10): per-method RT span = width_mean/width_rel_mean, the same max−min definition that gives the 16.84-min RepoRT median; 14 of 37 methods are 'long'. Random calibrants, n = 20: median change +4.5 % for long methods (P better in only 36 %) versus +0.6 % for short methods. Laboratory mix: +8.6 % versus +6.1 %. Random calibration chemicals: +8.2 % versus +10.2 %. The subgroup effect does not replicate; for long methods it reverses.
  * Corrective action: 1. Label the RT-span analysis as exploratory (defined after the results were seen) wherever it appears. 2. Remove the Abstract sentence and recommendation (iii), or restate them as a hypothesis that was not confirmed externally. 3. Report the NORMAN long/short contrast. 4. Replace the data-derived median split with a continuous within-source analysis.
  * Required experiment: Fit a mixed-effects model on RepoRT: log(IS_P/IS_J+) ~ centred RT span + (1|source). Use the fitted span coefficient to predict the held-out NORMAN and HILIC systems, and report the estimates with CIs. If the prediction fails, drop the gradient-length recommendation.
* **[major] R3-3: SASC is validated only on the scenario that motivated it, and its common-calibrant rule uses compound frequencies from the held-out folds** (code/EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md Addendum D; code/scripts/build_dataset.py lines 11-15; src/conformrt/evaluation/driver.py:196; system_eval.py:66-68; Section 4.11; Table 4; Tables S24-S25; Abstract; Highlights; confidence: high for the code path, the missing labels and the in-sample design; the size of the frequency-leakage effect is unknown and may be small)
  * Evidence: Addendum D: SASC was "Specified after observing (robustness run, commonly-measured calibrants) that all methods under-cover". It was evaluated on "rules random/span/common on all 228 target systems (10 draws; same seeds as the robustness runs)". The only test outside the motivating scenario, the NORMAN mix, failed (0.842; J+ 0.733). The common rule ranks compounds by n_systems: build_dataset.py lines 11-15 load RP and HILIC systems (load_repo_rt(..., method_types=("RP","HILIC"))) and compute n_systems=("system","nunique") over all folds. driver.py:196 passes this vector unchanged to every outer fold. So when fold f is held out, the rule replayed on the training systems to estimate c_ρ ranks compounds partly by their occurrence in fold f's systems (for example, up to +66 for the BGC compound list). This conflicts with the stated rule that "no information from the target system other than its calibrants" and nothing from the held-out source group is used. The rule is deterministic apart from random tie-breaking (system_eval.py:66-68, lexsort((rng.random(N), -n_systems_global))), so the reported "10 draws" are near-replicates. The Abstract ("restored 90.2 % for common-compound calibrants") and the Highlights ("known selection rules are corrected on average") present SASC without the post hoc label.
  * Corrective action: 1. Recompute compound frequencies per outer fold from training-fold reversed-phase systems only. 2. Rerun the common-calibrant robustness experiment and SASC. 3. Report the number of distinct calibrant sets per system under the common rule. 4. Label SASC as post hoc in the Abstract and Highlights. 5. Present SASC as a proposal supported by one in-sample demonstration until it has been validated on a rule that was not used to design it.
  * Required experiment: Validate SASC on at least one new selection rule that is specified and committed before computation. Examples: the 20 earliest-eluting compounds; the compounds of a commercial standard mix present in RepoRT; the common rule at n = 10 and 40; the HILIC systems. Report mean coverage and the share of systems below 0.85, with SASC computed from leakage-free frequencies.
* **[moderate] R3-4: Efficiency inference treats 228 clustered systems as independent; the gain over jackknife+ is confined to 2 of 5 sources** (Section 3.4; Section 4.2; Table 3; Tables S4, S8, S17; Limitations; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Section 3.4: "The unit of analysis is the target system". Table 3: Jackknife+ "−0.43 [−0.93, −0.20] ... Holm-adjusted p < 0.001", with per-fold medians "+3 / −18 / +0 / −10 / +0". BGC (66 systems, one data set, median 395 shared compounds) and Folberth (63 systems) are groups of near-replicate systems that share compounds and a laboratory. With five clusters, an exact two-sided sign test cannot go below p = 0.0625 even if every source favoured P, and only two do. On NORMAN with random calibrants the median change is +1.3 % (p = 0.32). The Limitations acknowledge non-independence only for coverage ("a cluster bootstrap over five sources is necessarily coarse"), and none of the efficiency claims (Tables 3, S4, S17) uses cluster-aware inference.
  * Corrective action: 1. Report source-level effect estimates with CIs. 2. Use a cluster-aware test for every efficiency comparison. 3. Restate H3 as source-dependent: an efficiency gain over jackknife+ was observed in 2 of 5 source groups and not in the external data.
  * Required experiment: Fit a linear mixed model on the per-system log interval-score ratio with a random intercept for source (and for laboratory where identifiable), or run a wild cluster bootstrap or a source-level permutation test. Report it alongside the Wilcoxon results for P versus each comparator and for the ablations.
* **[moderate] R3-5: The primary validity test makes calibrants exchangeable by design, so H2 cannot fail if the code is correct; realistic calibrant designs are only secondary** (Section 3.2; Section 4.1 ("These results support the pre-specified hypotheses ... (H2)"); Abstract (Results, Significance); confidence: high on the logic; the severity is a judgement)
  * Evidence: Section 3.2: calibrants are "drawn uniformly at random" from the system's own compounds, and "all remaining compounds of the system formed the test set". Under this design, coverage is at least 1−α for split conformal and at least 1−2α for jackknife+ by theorem (Section 2.4), and the plan's H2 is itself conditional on exchangeability. The only non-trivial empirical content is that jackknife+ reaches about 1−α rather than 1−2α. The realistic designs that test the practical claim gave: RT-spanning ("usual when a calibrant mix is designed") 0.928, i.e. over-coverage; commonly measured 0.815; NORMAN laboratory mix 0.851. The Abstract leads with the random-draw validity, and the Significance states that "Conformal RT windows make the probability of keeping the correct structure a design choice".
  * Corrective action: 1. Present the random-draw coverage as a check of the implementation against the theoretical bound, stating which bound applies to each method. 2. Give the realistic calibrant designs co-primary status in the Abstract and Conclusions, at minimum the RT-spanning design. 3. State that validity was not achieved for realistic fixed mixes without correction, and temper the Significance sentence accordingly.
  * Required experiment: No new computation is strictly required. Preferably, report the RT-spanning and commonly-measured designs with the same depth as the main analysis (20 draws, all n and α, the same tests).
* **[moderate] R3-6: The heuristic normalised comparator B6 is degenerate, and no simple non-learned scale baseline is tested** (src/conformrt/evaluation/system_eval.py lines 121-132; Section 2.7 (B6); Section 4.2; Tables 2-3; confidence: high on the implementation facts; medium on how much a regularised B6 or B8 would close the gap)
  * Evidence: system_eval.py:123-131 builds the B6 scale from 5 bootstrap replicates (200 trees each): sd_cal[i] = np.std(pc) + 0.01, i.e. a scale estimated from 5 values (4 degrees of freedom) with a negligible 0.01-min floor. Rens = |loo_res|/sd_cal therefore explodes whenever a calibrant's spread is near the floor. Results: mean width 26.8 min versus a median of 9.97 min (Section 4.2), interval score 28.99 versus 9.86 for J+ (Table 2), rank-biserial −1.00 (Table 3). Section 4.2 infers from this that "the choice of the scale function matters". Normalised conformal practice adds a β > 0 term (σ + β, as in the cited Papadopoulos et al. 2011); this is not implemented. The gains of P are concentrated outside the calibrant range (Section 4.6), which a transparent geometry-based scale could also capture, but the ablation only removes feature groups from the meta-learned model and never tests a simple, non-learned scale.
  * Corrective action: 1. Re-implement B6 with a regularised scale (σ + β, with β = median calibrant spread or chosen on training systems) and 20 or more replicates. 2. Add a non-learned geometry-scale baseline. 3. Withdraw the 'choice of scale function matters' inference unless it survives the fair version.
  * Required experiment: Run two comparators on the identical draws, at n ∈ {10, 20, 40} and all α, compared with P by the pre-specified tests: (a) B6' = B6 with regularised scale and 20 members; (b) B8 = jackknife+ normalised by σ(x) = 1 + κ·(extrapolation distance) + λ·(gap to the nearest calibrant score), with κ and λ fitted on training systems only (leave-source-out).
* **[moderate] R3-7: The decoy experiment favours P because the decoys are RepoRT compounds that the novelty features recognise as 'seen'** (Section 3.3; Section 4.7; Fig. 5; Tables S13-S16; Limitations; confidence: medium (the direction follows from the mechanism; the magnitude is unknown))
  * Evidence: Section 3.3 defines decoys as "RepoRT compounds with the same molecular formula". The difficulty model's novelty features are the "maximum Tanimoto similarity of x to the base-model training compounds and an exact-match flag" (Section 2.6), so decoys measured in training systems get the 'seen' scale and narrower windows. Realistic candidate lists (PubChem, PubChemLite, CompTox) consist mostly of compounds never measured, for which P widens windows relative to J+: in the unseen stratum, coverage is 0.835 for P versus 0.798 for J+ (Section 4.6). J+ width does not depend on novelty. The P-versus-J+ elimination comparison (24.3 % versus 24.6 %; matched retention in Table S15, Fig. 5) is therefore biased towards P. The Limitations mention RepoRT decoys but not this differential effect.
  * Corrective action: 1. Stratify decoy elimination by decoy seen/unseen status and by Tanimoto bin. 2. Add realistic database decoys. 3. Qualify Section 4.7 until both are done.
  * Required experiment: For each test compound, draw up to 30 same-formula candidates from PubChemLite (or CompTox) that are absent from RepoRT. Compute their base scores and windows, and recompute the retention-elimination curves and matched-retention elimination for P, J+, B1 and B3, on draws 0-4 at n = 20.
* **[moderate] R3-8: The fixed-tolerance baselines are an uncalibrated grid, and the headline '±1 min retained 70.5 %' misrepresents practice** (Section 4.1 paragraph 2; Table 2; Table S6; Abstract; Conclusions; Highlights; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Table 2 and Table S6 apply ±0.25 to ±2 min and ±5/±10 % of the predicted RT to cross-system transfer predictions. On these predictions, valid conformal windows need a median width of 4.68 min, on systems whose median RT spans range from 7.8 to 25.9 min (Table 1). Abstract: "fixed ±1 min windows in 70.5 %"; Conclusions: "fixed ±1 min windows kept only 71 %". The tolerances cited in the Introduction (±0.40 min, ±3 min, ±15 % of run time) were tuned for their own models, and ±15 % of run time was not evaluated. A laboratory that uses a fixed window would tune its width on validation data. The defensible argument, that one width cannot adapt across systems (±2 min falls below 0.85 in 43 % of systems), needs a calibrated fixed width to be made fairly.
  * Corrective action: 1. Add a calibrated fixed tolerance: a single width in minutes, and one in % of run time, chosen on training systems (leave-source-out) to give 90 % average coverage, reporting its mean and per-system coverage dispersion. 2. Evaluate ±15 % of run time. 3. Remove the arbitrary ±1 min figure from the Abstract and Conclusions, or state that the grid widths were not tuned.
  * Required experiment: Run the calibrated fixed tolerances (minutes and % of run time) on the identical draws at n ∈ {10, 20, 40}, α = 0.10, labelled post hoc.
* **[moderate] R3-9: Post hoc labelling is incomplete, and analyses designed after seeing results are not classified at all** (Abstract; Highlights; Section 1 item (iv); Section 3.2; Sections 4.3, 4.7, 4.8, 4.9, 4.10; Table 4; Fig. 5; Tables S15, S17, S20, S22; Conclusions; code/EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md heading; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: "post hoc" occurs only four times in Final_Manuscript.md: Section 3.2, Section 4.1 paragraph 2, the Table 2 caption, and the Table 4 caption (for SASC only). Not labelled: the NORMAN external validation (Section 4.10, the NORMAN rows of Table 4, Abstract, Highlights, Introduction item (iv), Conclusions); SASC in the Abstract and Highlights; the fixed windows in the Abstract, Highlights and Conclusions. Several analyses were defined after the results and are covered by no addendum: the matched-retention interpolation (Fig. 5, Table S15), the coverage-loss mechanism analysis (Table S20), the RT-span heterogeneity analysis (Section 4.3), and the 'physicochemical' ablation, which is not among the plan's E3 ablations (novelty, ionisation, system, calibrant-geometry, naive no-LOO, linear map) yet is reported as the largest effect (0.61 min). Bootstrap resamples of 1,000-4,000 replace the plan's 10,000 without being listed as a deviation. Section 3.2 defines post hoc only as "added after the plan was written but before the corresponding results were computed", which leaves result-driven analyses unclassified. The plan file's heading still reads "Pre-registered experimental plan", while Section 3.2 says the plan was not registered.
  * Corrective action: 1. Adopt three labels: pre-specified; post hoc (specified before computation); exploratory (specified after seeing results). 2. Apply them in every table and figure caption and in the Abstract, Highlights and Conclusions. 3. Add a Supplementary deviation table covering B6, B7, the bootstrap resamples, the physicochemical ablation and the RT-span analysis. 4. Retitle the plan file 'Pre-specified analysis plan'.
  * Required experiment: None.
* **[moderate] R3-10: The GP comparator B3's under-coverage may be partly caused by its hyper-parameter bounds; the headline GP figure generalises from one implementation** (src/conformrt/models/mapping.py lines 130-157; Section 2.7 (B2, B3); Section 4.1; Abstract; Conclusions; confidence: medium (that the bounds cause the under-coverage is a hypothesis until the sensitivity run is done))
  * Evidence: mapping.py:139-140: WhiteKernel noise bounds (1e-6, 1.0) on standardised RT, RBF length-scale lower bound 0.01 on the standardised score, and n_restarts_optimizer=1, with ML-II estimation on 10-20 points. These are conditions under which ML-II often drives the noise to its lower bound and interpolates the calibrants; B3 coverage is 0.714 at n = 10 and 0.822 at n = 20. The meta-learned GP (first B7 run) instead over-covers (0.974 at nominal 0.90), which shows that GP calibration here is governed by how the hyper-parameters are handled. B2 and B3 also use their own mapping families, so their deficits mix mapping and interval effects. Abstract and Conclusions: "Gaussian-process intervals discarded the correct structure 1.8 times as often as intended".
  * Corrective action: 1. Report how sensitive B3 is to its noise floor and number of restarts. 2. Separate the effect of the mapping family from that of the interval construction. 3. Qualify the GP headline as applying to ML-II GP projection fitted on the target calibrants only.
  * Required experiment: (a) Rerun B3 with noise lower bounds of 1e-2 and 1e-1 (standardised units) and 10 restarts. (b) Run jackknife+ around the GP and P-spline means (J+GP, J+PS) on the same draws at n ∈ {10, 20, 40} and all α.

### R4_statistics — major revision

The central validity result survives every statistical check I ran. Mean coverage of the conformal windows is at or above 1 − α at every n and α, and in every source fold. Every system-level 95 % lower bound for the conformal methods lies above nominal (coverage_validity in statistics.json).

Most of the remaining inferential claims are weaker than stated.
(1) The efficiency claims rest on Wilcoxon tests that treat 228 systems from five correlated sources as independent replicates. These are the Holm p < 0.001 values in Table 3 and the tests behind H3. The effect reverses sign between sources (I² = 0.98). A source-level random-effects analysis gives a pooled interval-score ratio for the proposed method versus jackknife+ of 0.92 (95 % CI 0.81–1.04). The source-level sign-flip permutation p is 0.25. Results are similar against the Gaussian, GP and P-spline windows.
(2) Statements about a laboratory's own system use per-system averages over 20 draws. For a single calibrant set (n = 20), coverage was below 0.85 in 17 % of system–draw combinations.
(3) The SASC figure of 90.2 % averages held-out sources that range from 0.868 to 0.929.
(4) The stratum coverages for novel compounds depend on the estimator: the system average gives 0.781, while pooling over compound–draw evaluations gives 0.906. Neither is reported with a CI.
(5) The five-cluster bootstrap is degenerate and should not be presented as reassurance.
(6) Several smaller problems remain: an RT-span subgroup analysis that was not pre-specified and is confounded by source; comparators left out of the text in the HILIC and NORMAN sections; validity inferred from failing to reject the null; matched-retention comparisons with no uncertainty; incomplete reporting of the multiplicity structure; and two small mismatches between the text and the code or tables.

All numbers I report were recomputed from results/raw_results.csv, results/raw/main_rows.parquet, results/raw/sasc_rows.parquet and statistics.json. The script is at /tmp/claude-0/-home-user-p-c1-5/aba5d053-3588-58c2-8bff-15bea55362ed/scratchpad/r4.py.

* **[critical] R4-1: Paired efficiency tests treat 228 systems from five correlated sources as independent; the advantage of the proposed windows does not hold up at the source level** (Manuscript Section 3.4 (line 116); Results 'Efficiency' (line ~155) and 'Heterogeneity'; Table 3; code/src/conformrt/statistics/analysis.py paired_tests(); EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md l.67; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Section 3.4 says: 'The unit of analysis is the target system'. EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md l.67 says: 'systems are the independent replicates'. Table 3 reports Holm p < 0.001 for every comparator. Yet the per-fold relative changes in Table 3 change sign: Gaussian +6/−16/+5/−13/−1, GP +5/−12/+4/−21/−2, jackknife+ +3/−18/+0/−10/+0.

fold_heterogeneity in statistics.json shows that in BGC (66 systems) the proposed windows are significantly worse than both the Gaussian window (+5.7 %, p = 0.0022) and the GP windows (+4.6 %, p = 0.0007). The text mentions only the Gaussian result.

My re-analysis of raw_results.csv (n = 20, α = 0.10) used a DerSimonian–Laird random-effects model of the per-source mean log(IS_P/IS_comp):
- vs jackknife+: ratio 0.918, 95 % CI 0.81–1.04 (t(4)-based 0.77–1.10), I² = 0.98
- vs Gaussian: 0.937 (0.83–1.06)
- vs GP: 0.949 (0.85–1.06)
- vs P-spline: 0.915 (0.81–1.03)
The exact source-level sign-flip permutation p is 0.25–0.31. With five sources the smallest attainable two-sided p is 0.0625.

The signed-rank test on differences in minutes also weights systems by their RT scale. The mean |rank| of the P − J+ differences is 174.9 in Folberth versus 53.3 in BGC, so the pooled p and r = −0.47 mainly reflect the long-gradient sources. The claim 'lowest mean interval score of all methods (9.07 min)' is an untested mean, also dominated by long-gradient systems.
  * Corrective action: Make clear that the system-level p-values (Table 3, ablation, SMRT, HILIC and SASC tests) apply to the 228 systems of these five sources, not to new laboratories.

Add a source-aware analysis as a co-primary sensitivity analysis:
- a linear mixed model of log(IS_P/IS_comp) with random intercepts for source (and system, with draws as replicates), or a random-effects meta-analysis over sources;
- the pooled ratio with its 95 % CI, τ², I², and a 95 % prediction interval for a new source.

Use scale-free effect measures (median log-ratio or % change) for the paired comparisons, and report the signed-rank test on log-ratios.

State in Results and Discussion that the advantage over jackknife+, Gaussian and GP windows appeared in 2–3 of 5 sources and reversed in BGC (and PredRet for Gaussian and GP). Mention the significant BGC deficit against GP. Temper the H3 wording in the Abstract and Conclusions.
  * Required experiment: No new data are needed. Extend analyze.py with:
(i) a mixed model or random-effects meta-analysis over sources for every paired interval-score comparison in Table 3, the SMRT comparison and the ablation;
(ii) Wilcoxon tests on log-ratios;
(iii) a leave-one-source-out influence analysis of the pooled effect.
* **[major] R4-2: Claims about a laboratory's own system use coverage averaged over 20 calibrant draws; coverage for a single calibrant set varies much more and is not guaranteed** (Table 2 (column 'Systems with coverage < 0.85'); Results 'Coverage of current RT windows' (line 127) and 'Candidate filtering' (line ~187); Abstract 'Significance'; Conclusions; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Table 2's column 'Systems with coverage < 0.85' (0 % for the proposed windows) is computed on per-system means over 20 draws (summary_table: share_severe_under applied to the per-system mean).

From results/raw/main_rows.parquet (n = 20, α = 0.10; 4,560 system–draw combinations), per-draw coverage was:
- proposed: < 0.85 in 17.3 %, < 0.80 in 8.4 % (5th percentile 0.764)
- jackknife+: 18.4 % and 9.1 %
- Gaussian: 27.8 % and 14.7 %
For the proposed windows the share below 0.85 barely changes with n (17.0 %, 17.3 %, 16.9 % at n = 10, 20, 40).

The text nevertheless says that 'a laboratory that chooses α=0.10 obtains about 90 % retention of correct structures on its own system' and that 'the operating point is known before the analysis'. The Abstract says the probability is 'a design choice'. The jackknife+ guarantee is marginal over the random calibrant set. The paper cites training-conditional coverage (bian2023training) only for SASC.
  * Corrective action: Report the draw-level (calibration-conditional) coverage distribution for every method: share below 0.85 and below 0.80, and the 5th and 10th percentiles, at each n.

Compare it with the reference distribution expected under exact exchangeability, so that finite-test-set noise is separated from calibration-conditional variability. For split conformal this is Beta-binomial; for jackknife+, obtain it by simulation.

Rephrase the laboratory-level statements as 'on average over calibrant sets', and state the spread a laboratory should expect for one calibrant set of size n. Optionally discuss or implement a training-conditional (PAC-type) variant with a more conservative quantile index, and report the n it requires.
  * Required experiment: No new data are needed.
(i) Fit a beta-binomial random-effects model to the per-draw covered/total counts in main_rows or main_strata. This separates between-draw (calibration-conditional) variance within systems from binomial noise and gives the estimated distribution of conditional coverage per n.
(ii) Optionally, add a PAC-conformal variant at n = 20 and 40 on the existing draws.
* **[major] R4-3: SASC 'restored 90.2 %' averages over-coverage in three held-out sources and under-coverage in two; the system-level CI understates uncertainty for a new laboratory** (Abstract; Results 'Selection-aware system-level calibration' (line ~217); Table 4; Table S24; analyze.py SASC block; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: From results/raw/sasc_rows.parquet (common-calibrant rule, n = 20, α = 0.10), mean coverage per held-out source:
- P+SASC: BGC 0.884 (32 % of systems < 0.85), Folberth 0.929, PredRet 0.910, Souihi+Stoffel 0.868 (25 % < 0.85), Other 0.921
- J+SASC: 0.830, 0.929, 0.923, 0.863, 0.934

The pooled 0.902 [0.888, 0.915] (table12_sasc; system bootstrap with B = 2000) is the weighted mean of these. The text states that the training systems 'are then the exchangeable units', but c is estimated on systems from other sources. For a new laboratory, the relevant variability is therefore between sources, which the system-level CI ignores.

The Abstract reports 'restored 90.2 % for common-compound calibrants' and the text 'restored the coverage ... from 0.815 to 0.902'. Neither gives the per-source range.
  * Corrective action: Add the per-source SASC coverage and the share of systems below 0.85 to Table S24. Report a source-level summary (range across the five held-out sources, or a random-effects mean with its prediction interval).

Qualify the Abstract, for example: '90.2 % on average over 228 systems (0.87–0.93 across the five held-out sources)'.

Discuss that replaying the rule on other sources' systems equalises the average over those systems but not the coverage for a new source. Consider estimating c with sources weighted equally, and report the leave-one-source-out variability of c.
  * Required experiment: Re-analysis only: per-source SASC coverage with CIs, and source-weighted estimation of c on the existing simulation outputs.
* **[major] R4-4: Stratum coverages are given only as unweighted system averages without CIs; pooled estimates lead to a different conclusion for novel compounds** (Results 'Conditional coverage' (line ~181); Fig. 4 and its caption; Table S9; analysis.py strata_coverage(); Limitations; Conclusions; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: statistics.json 'strata' (proposed windows):
- Tanimoto 0.4–0.7: coverage_sysavg 0.781 (193 systems) vs coverage_pooled 0.906 (169,021 compound–draw evaluations)
- Tanimoto < 0.4: 0.848 vs 0.888
- not seen by the base model: 0.835 vs 0.905 (jackknife+: 0.798 vs 0.880)

The text states: 'Coverage nevertheless remained below nominal for compounds that were neither seen ... (Tanimoto 0.4–0.7: 0.781; < 0.4: 0.848)'. Limitations and Conclusions build on this.

The system average weights systems with only a few compounds in a stratum as heavily as systems with many. The non-monotone pattern (0.781 at 0.4–0.7 but 0.848 at < 0.4) suggests noise. Fig. 4 plots system averages but annotates the bars with pooled evaluation counts. No CI is given in the text, Fig. 4 or Table S9.
  * Corrective action: Report both estimators with cluster-bootstrap CIs over systems (and over sources). Give the number of systems and the median per-system stratum size.

Fit a mixed-effects logistic model (covered ~ stratum + (1|system) + (1|source)) to estimate the stratum effects. Explain the difference between the estimators: under-coverage appears concentrated in systems where novel compounds are a small minority.

Revise 'remained below nominal' so the wording matches what the CIs support, and make the Fig. 4 caption consistent with the estimator plotted.
  * Required experiment: Re-analysis of results/raw/main_strata.parquet: both estimators with bootstrap CIs, the mixed-effects logistic model, and a sensitivity analysis restricted to systems with at least 20 evaluations in the stratum.
* **[moderate] R4-5: The five-cluster bootstrap is degenerate yet is presented as confirming the system-level CIs** (Results line 127; Section 3.4; Table S3; analysis.py boot_ci() and one_sided_coverage_test() (clusters branch); confidence: high)
  * Evidence: For the Gaussian window (coverage_validity), the cluster CI [0.8833, 0.8867] is narrower than the system-level CI [0.8825, 0.8879]. With positive within-source correlation, a valid cluster CI cannot be narrower.

The cluster one-sided p-values for the Gaussian, P-spline and GP windows are all 0.00025 = 1/(4000+1), the floor of the resampling. Five clusters allow only 126 distinct resamples, and an exact source-level randomisation test cannot go below 1/32.

The text says: 'a cluster bootstrap over source folds gave practically identical confidence intervals (Table S3)'. Limitations calls it 'necessarily coarse', but Results use it as confirmation.
  * Corrective action: Drop the 'practically identical' reassurance. Replace the naive cluster bootstrap with a method suited to few clusters: a wild cluster bootstrap with Webb weights, t(4) intervals on the five source means, or a random-effects model. Report per-source coverages with their intervals.

State explicitly that five sources do not allow statistical inference about between-source generalisation; it can only be described.
  * Required experiment: Re-analysis only.
* **[moderate] R4-6: The run-length effect in the Abstract comes from an analysis that was not pre-specified, splits at the sample median, and is confounded with source** (Abstract; Results 'Heterogeneity' (line ~158); Practical recommendation (iii); analyze.py gain_vs_rt_span; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Abstract: 'Normalisation improved efficiency for longer-than-median runs (median interval-score change versus jackknife+ −17 %) but not for shorter runs'. EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md specifies no RT-span subgroup analysis, and the manuscript does not label this analysis post hoc, unlike Addenda B–D.

All 66 BGC systems fall below the median span (16.84 min) and all 63 Folberth systems above it, so 55 % of the 'long' group is Folberth.

My fold-fixed-effects regression of log(IS_P/IS_J+) on a long-run indicator gives a ratio of 0.907 (−9 %; SE 0.037; p = 0.009 if systems are independent). That is about half the unadjusted contrast of −16.8 % versus +2.3 %.

Within the folds that contain both run lengths (short vs long):
- PredRet: +6.4 % vs −2.4 %
- Souihi+Stoffel: +9.5 % (7 systems) vs −33.2 %
- Other: +0.8 % vs −4.4 %
The direction is consistent, but the size is overstated.
  * Corrective action: Label the RT-span analysis post hoc and exploratory. In the Abstract, report the fold-adjusted contrast (or partial ρ = −0.16) rather than the unadjusted −17 %.

Model span as a continuous variable together with source (mixed model) instead of splitting at the median. Soften recommendation (iii) to reflect the within-source effect size and its uncertainty.
  * Required experiment: Re-analysis: a mixed model of log(IS_P/IS_J+) on span (continuous) with a random intercept for source; report the estimate with its CI.
* **[moderate] R4-7: Comparators left out of the text for HILIC and the NORMAN laboratory-mix scenario** (Results 'Generalisation ... HILIC' (line ~207) and 'External validation' (line ~213); Conclusions; Practical recommendation (i); confidence: high)
  * Evidence: HILIC (gen_hilic in statistics.json): the proposed windows had a better interval score than the Gaussian window in only 8 % of systems (median +1.78 min), than P-spline in 14 % (+1.89) and than GP in 22 % (+1.44); all Holm p < 0.001. The text reports only the comparison with jackknife+ (16.70 vs 14.33 min).

NORMAN laboratory mix (norman_tests fixed_mix): compared with the Gaussian window, the proposed windows had a median difference of +0.83 min, were better in 32 % of systems, Holm p = 0.024. Coverage was identical: proposed 0.851 [0.825, 0.873], Gaussian 0.851 [0.820, 0.881] (Table S22, Table 4).

The text instead says '0.851 ... for the proposed windows and 0.869 for jackknife+, i.e. below the nominal 0.90 but still above the GP intervals (0.777)'. In the scenario the paper calls 'the realistic use case', the proposed windows were no better calibrated than the Gaussian window and were significantly less efficient.
  * Corrective action: Report the Holm-adjusted comparisons with all comparators for HILIC and NORMAN in the text. State that the Gaussian window matched the coverage of the proposed windows with a better interval score in the laboratory-mix scenario, and that the proposed windows had worse interval scores than every comparator except the ensemble-normalised jackknife+ and split conformal in HILIC.

Qualify the Conclusions ('their value is that the retention rate becomes a design choice') for non-exchangeable calibrants.
  * Required experiment: None (the results already exist).
* **[moderate] R4-8: Validity and equivalence are inferred from failing to reject H0 (mean coverage ≥ 1 − α), with no non-inferiority margin** (Section 3.4; Results lines ~127, ~207, ~217, ~237; Table S18; analysis.py one_sided_coverage_test(); confidence: medium)
  * Evidence: one_sided_coverage_test tests H0: mean coverage ≥ nominal. Not rejecting it cannot show that coverage is valid, yet the text makes such claims:
- HILIC: 'the conformal windows again had nominal coverage (0.903 proposed, 0.903 jackknife+)'. The CIs are 0.897–0.909 and 0.897–0.910 (table6). 23 of the 37 HILIC systems come from one source fold, so even these CIs are too narrow.
- SASC: 'restored ... 0.902' with CI 0.888–0.915.
- 'coverage of the proposed windows did not differ between ionisable and neutral compounds (0.904 versus 0.903)', with no CI. Jackknife+ shows 0.889 vs 0.914.
- 'The results were insensitive to the difficulty-model hyper-parameters' (Table S18), with no margin.
The analysis plan defines no margin.
  * Corrective action: Pre-state a non-inferiority margin δ (e.g. 0.01) and report one-sided 95 % lower confidence bounds, clustered by source where possible. Call coverage 'non-inferior to 1 − α − δ' only when the lower bound exceeds 1 − α − δ.

Give CIs for every 'no difference' or 'insensitive' statement, or rephrase them as 'no evidence of a difference'. Make clear that the existing one-sided test can only detect under-coverage.
  * Required experiment: Re-analysis: non-inferiority and TOST tests on the existing per-system coverages, with source-clustered intervals where feasible.
* **[moderate] R4-9: Matched-retention decoy comparisons have no uncertainty and rely on interpolation between three α levels** (Results 'Candidate filtering with isomeric decoys' (line ~187); Fig. 5; Tables S15–S16; confidence: medium)
  * Evidence: Table S15 gives decoy elimination at 85 % retention: proposed 0.329, jackknife+ 0.334, Gaussian 0.337, GP 0.345, P-spline 0.353. These come from 'linear interpolation of each method's retention–elimination curve over α = 0.05, 0.10 and 0.20'. For the proposed windows, the 85 % point lies between α = 0.10 (91.4 % retention) and α = 0.20, so the interpolation spans about 10 points of retention.

The differences interpreted in the text are only 0.4–2.4 points: 'parametric windows slightly ahead' and 'Fixed ±2 min windows ... eliminated fewer decoys (30.4 %) than the model-based windows at 85 % retention'. The latter compares a single point (85.1 %) with interpolated values. No CI or paired test is reported, and only draws 0–4 are used.
  * Corrective action: Compute elimination at matched retention on a dense α grid, per system, with paired 95 % bootstrap CIs over systems (and source-level summaries) for the differences between methods. Rephrase 'slightly ahead' and 'fewer' to match the intervals.
  * Required experiment: Re-evaluate the existing decoy sets (draws 0–4, n = 20) on α = 0.02–0.40 in steps of 0.02. This needs only post-processing of the residuals and windows. Then bootstrap the paired elimination differences at 85 % and 90 % retention.

- α = 0.05: median +1.4 percentage points (proposed eliminates more), p = 0.0001
- α = 0.20: −0.65 points, p = 0.008
Only α = 0.10 (p = 0.50) is reported, and none of the 12 decoy tests is adjusted.

The per-fold tests (Table S8) are unadjusted, as the caption says, but the text quotes 'p = 0.09' and 'p = 0.002' without saying so, next to 'Holm-adjusted' values. In Table S4, Holm is applied separately within each (n, α) cell: 13 families of 3–7 tests, 59 tests in total. Methods says only 'Holm correction within each metric family'.
  * Corrective action: Define the test families explicitly in Section 3.4: primary (n = 20, α = 0.10) versus exploratory (other n and α, per-fold, decoy tests). Mark every unadjusted p-value as such. Report the decoy test at all three α levels, or Holm-adjust across them.

  * Evidence: Methods: '10,000 resamples for the main analyses and 1,000–4,000 for the supplementary analyses'. analyze.py line 336 builds the small-n table (Table S5) with summary_table(snp, n, al, ..., B=500). The earlier audit flagged this as C30; it is only partly fixed.
  * Corrective action: Either state '500–4,000' or raise B to at least 1,000 for Table S5 and regenerate.
  * Required experiment: None.
* **[minor] R4-12: The new sentence on B7 misstates Table 2** (Results 'Efficiency: interval score and width' (line ~155); Table 2; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: The text says: 'the meta-learned GP windows (12.00 min) had the second-highest mean interval score of all constructions, after the ensemble-normalised jackknife+ (Table 2)'. In Table 2, 12.00 min is B7's median width; its mean interval score is 14.16 min. That is lower than Fixed ±0.5 min (16.66) and Fixed ±10 % (14.64), so B7 is fourth-highest of the 13 rows. ### R5_dataset_leakage — major revision

Fold-level leakage control is implemented as described. The difficulty model's inner cross-fitting uses base models trained without folds {f,g} (driver.difficulty_training_rows). B7 does the same (run_metagp.py). SASC learns c with sigma_fg fitted without fold g and with base models trained without folds {f,g}. Missing metadata reach LightGBM the same way for RepoRT and NORMAN, as S1 states. I checked the processed data directly (data_processed/systems.csv, rt_long.parquet; pairwise screen of all 297 RP systems; NORMAN-vs-RepoRT matching on InChIKey connectivity blocks) and found these problems: (1) The test populations consist mostly of compounds already in the training library (73% in the main study; 39/41 NORMAN suspects and 39/41 calibration chemicals). No compound-disjoint (strict-novel) test exists, although H5(e) was pre-registered, and the strata already show unseen compounds under-covered (0.835). (2) Target system 0209 ('SMRT', PredRet) is a copy of METLIN SMRT: 229 of 247 compounds, identical RTs. SMRT is in every training set, and the SMRT regressor predicts 0209 in-sample, giving it the narrowest windows of all 228 systems in E5. (3) 69 positive/negative-ionisation twin groups mean the '228 systems' are about 159 LC methods, or 119 clusters with identical RTs. The main inferences survive my re-analysis, but n and the secondary tests are inflated. (4) The 'RT-spanning' calibrant rule stratifies on the test compounds' own RT quantiles, an oracle, and the SASC 'span' result depends on it. (5) NORMAN method DS_AWW is practically identical to RepoRT system AA_LC1, which is in the training data. (6) Cleaning drops 2,357 blocks whose RTs disagree, 25% of one system, using a fixed 0.2-min threshold, and the main text describes the rule incorrectly. (7) Third-party NORMAN ESI files are redistributed without a license statement. Nothing here is fatal: the coverage results for library-like compounds are probably robust. But the external-validity claim for NTS unknowns and some reporting need new experiments and corrections.

* **[major] R5-1: Test compounds are mostly in the training library; no compound-disjoint (strict-novel) evaluation; the realistic case of library calibrants with novel analytes is untested; NORMAN validation is 95% seen** (Manuscript Abstract; Highlights; Section 3.2 (last sentence); Section 4.5 Conditional coverage; Section 4.10 External validation; code/EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md H5(e) and E4; code/experiments/run_norman.py (ex.fm.get(frozenset()) trains on all RepoRT RP); confidence: high for the data facts (seen shares and missing H5(e) verdict); medium for the size of the coverage shortfall expected under (b))
  * Evidence: Section 3.2: '73 % of test compounds at n=20 were present in the base model's training data'. Section 4.5: coverage for compounds absent from the training data was 0.835 (proposed), 0.798 (jackknife+) and 0.765 (Gaussian), and 0.781 for unseen compounds at Tanimoto 0.4-0.7. Section 4.10: the NORMAN models were 'trained on all RepoRT reversed-phase systems, which contain 95 % of the NORMAN compounds'. My check of the ik14 connectivity blocks in NORMAN_interlab_RT_data.csv against the RepoRT RP rt_long table found 39/41 suspects and 39/41 calibration chemicals present. EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md H5 states that 'conclusions hold for ... (e) strict novel-compound subsets'. The manuscript gives verdicts only for H1/H2 and says 'the only deviation from it is the omitted similarity term of B6'; no strict-novel experiment exists, only a post hoc seen/unseen stratification. In the main protocol, calibrants and test compounds are drawn from the same pool (73% seen), so exchangeability on the seen/unseen axis holds by design. In NTS practice, calibrants are reference standards that are nearly always in public RT libraries, while the correct candidate for an unknown feature usually is not; this is the exchangeability violation that matters most, and it is evaluated nowhere, including the NORMAN 'realistic use case'. The abstract headline (90.9%-91.8%) and the highlights are not qualified by the seen share.
  * Corrective action: Give an explicit verdict on pre-registered H5(e), which the strata indicate is not supported. Qualify the abstract and highlights: coverage was obtained on test compounds of which 73% (NORMAN 95%) were in the training library. Add the two experiments below. Use the new results for the claims about NTS unknowns and for recommendation (iv).
  * Required experiment: (a) Compound-disjoint rerun. For each outer fold f, remove from the base-model training table (all other-fold systems and the 15k SMRT subsample) every ik14 that occurs in any target system of fold f. Apply the same removal to the inner {f,g} models used to simulate the difficulty-model rows. Rerun n=20, alpha in {0.05, 0.10, 0.20}, 20 draws, for proposed, jackknife+, Gaussian, GP and P-spline; report coverage, width and interval score. (b) Seen calibrants with unseen analytes. On systems with at least 20 seen and at least 10 unseen compounds, draw the 20 calibrants only among seen compounds and evaluate coverage on unseen test compounds, with and without SASC replaying this rule on training systems. (c) NORMAN. Retrain the all-RepoRT base and difficulty models without the NORMAN ik14 blocks and rerun the random_all and fixed_mix scenarios.
* **[moderate] R5-2: Target system 0209 is a PredRet copy of METLIN SMRT, which is in every training set (cross-fold duplicate; target leakage, in-sample in the SMRT-regressor experiment)** (data_processed/systems.csv (0186, 0209); code/src/conformrt/evaluation/protocol.py Data.targets / Data.training_table; manuscript Sections 3.1, 3.2, 4.9; Table S21; Table 1 (PredRet: 41 systems); confidence: high)
  * Evidence: In systems.csv, 0209 has name 'SMRT', source 'PredRet', column 'Agilent ZORBAX Extend-C18' and 247 compounds; 0186 is 'SMRT_METLIN' (Domingo-Almenara, 79,931 compounds, fold 'Other sources'). The two share 229 of 0209's 247 connectivity blocks, with median |dRT| = 3e-9 min and r = 0.999997. protocol.Data.targets drops only smrt_id 0186, and Data.training_table always adds a 15,000-compound SMRT subsample. 0209 is therefore a PredRet-fold target whose own chromatographic system (and, in expectation, about 19% of its exact compound-RT pairs) is in every base model. In the E5 SMRT-regressor run (trained on all 79,931 SMRT RTs, i.e. on 93% of 0209's compounds with identical RTs), 0209 has the narrowest relative windows of all 228 systems (results/raw/gen_smrt_rows.parquet, n=20, alpha=0.10: proposed 0.199 of span vs median 0.461; jackknife+ 0.166 vs 0.486). In the main run it is unremarkable (rank 195/228). 0209 also enters the difficulty-model rows for outer folds 0, 1, 3 and 4 (fold-2 rows are simulated with inner models that include SMRT), and the B7 and SASC training sets. This contradicts Section 3.1 ('excluding METLIN SMRT, which served only as an auxiliary training library') and Section 3.2 ('no retention time or chromatographic system of a held-out source group entered model development'). It also shows that no duplicate-system screen was run. My screen of all 297 RP systems (pairs with at least 15 shared compounds) found 0209-0186 to be the only cross-fold pair with median |dRT| <= 0.05 min and r >= 0.995. Eleven further cross-fold pairs agree within a median of 0.2 min (r >= 0.99), e.g. PredRet CS23/OBSF (HSS T3) vs BGC HSS T3 systems; these appear to be similar but distinct methods.
  * Corrective action: Remove 0209 from the targets, and from training, since it duplicates SMRT. Add a documented duplicate-system screen to the cleaning pipeline (shared compounds, median |dRT|, r) and report its output in Section S1. Correct the Section 3.1/3.2 statements and update the counts (227 targets; PredRet 40).
  * Required experiment: Rerun the main analysis (n=20, alpha=0.10), the E5 SMRT-regressor analysis, B7 and SASC without 0209 and report the changes (expected to be small). Add a supplementary table of all cross-source system pairs with median |dRT| <= 0.2 min and r >= 0.99.
* **[moderate] R5-3: Pseudo-replication: positive/negative-ionisation twins of the same LC method are counted as separate target systems ('228 systems' is about 159 LC methods)** (Manuscript Highlights, Abstract, Section 3.1 (Table 1), Section 3.4 Statistical analysis, Sections 4.1-4.3 (Tables 2, 3), Table S3, Table S8, Limitations; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Among the 228 targets, 69 groups share source and name except for a pos/neg token. Examples: Folberth 'RP14 pos'/'RP14 neg'; Stoffel 'flow0.35_pos'/'flow0.35_neg'; BGC 'MSMS_pos_30_ACN_HSS T3'/'MSMS_neg_30_ACN_HSS T3'; Huber 'SPECIMEN_Pos'/'SPECIMEN_Neg'. Merging them leaves 159 distinct LC methods. In the 67 twin pairs with at least 15 shared compounds, the median number shared is 159, median |dRT| is 0.015 min (90% of pairs <= 0.05 min) and median r is 0.9997, i.e. the same chromatographic run. Clustering by RT identity (median |dRT| <= 0.05 min, r >= 0.995) reduces the 228 targets to 119 clusters (Folberth 63->26, BGC 66->18, Stoffel 18->5). Section 3.4 states 'The unit of analysis is the target system'. Bootstrap CIs, the Wilcoxon tests, the '% systems < 0.85' column, the Spearman/partial correlations and the per-fold tests (Table S8) all treat the twins as independent. The Limitations mention only that 'systems within a source are not independent'. My re-analysis, averaging twins (159 units), gives: Wilcoxon interval score proposed vs jackknife+ p = 1.1e-9 -> 9.1e-5; mean coverage CI unchanged (0.908-0.913); Spearman(relative gain, RT span) -0.53 -> -0.47. The main conclusions therefore survive, but the reported n is inflated by about 43%. Borderline secondary results (BGC p = 0.09; fold-adjusted partial rho = -0.16, p = 0.014; per-fold tests) were computed with that inflated n.
  * Corrective action: Use the LC method as the unit: merge pos/neg twins, either as the union of compounds with averaged RTs or by averaging metrics within each twin group. Report '159 LC methods (228 RepoRT datasets)'. Recompute all system-level CIs, tests, '% below 0.85' and correlations at that level, and describe the twin structure in Section S1.
  * Required experiment: Recompute Tables 2, 3, S3, S4 and S8 and the span correlation analysis with LC-method units (and, as a sensitivity analysis, RT-identity clusters), adding a cluster bootstrap at the LC-method level; report the effective n per fold.
* **[moderate] R5-4: The 'RT-spanning' calibrant rule uses the target system's test-compound RTs (oracle selection), and the SASC 'span' factor inherits this** (code/src/conformrt/evaluation/system_eval.py lines 63-65; code/experiments/run_sasc.py (select_calibrants with rule 'span'); manuscript Section 3.2 robustness (i), Section 4.8, Section 4.12, Table 4, Tables S19, S20, S24, S25; confidence: high for the code; medium for the direction and size of the effect)
  * Evidence: In system_eval.select_calibrants, `if strategy == "span": order = np.argsort(y); return np.array([rng.choice(b) for b in np.array_split(order, n)])`, where y holds the RTs of all compounds of the system, including the test compounds. The calibrants are therefore stratified on quantiles of the analytes' RT distribution, which no laboratory knows when designing a mix. Section 4.8 presents this as 'as is usual when a calibrant mix is designed' (coverage 0.928). Section 4.12 reports that SASC 'for RT-spanning calibrants ... removed the conservativeness and narrowed the windows from 4.60 to 3.95 min at a coverage of 0.902' (c = 0.84-0.86, Table S25); the same oracle rule is replayed on the training systems using their full RT distributions. Claim-evidence audit C26 accepted this code without considering the oracle aspect.
  * Corrective action: Either relabel the scenario as an oracle upper bound, or replace it with a deployable rule. Examples: the compound closest to each of n equally spaced times between t0 and the gradient end (from metadata), or quantile strata of a pre-specified calibrant pool such as the commonly measured compounds or the NORMAN calibration chemicals, never of the analytes. Do not present the 'span' SASC factor as usable in practice unless it is recomputed with a deployable rule.
  * Required experiment: Rerun robustness scenario (i) (n=20, 10 draws) and the SASC 'span' rule (learning c on training systems and applying it to targets) with a time-equispaced or pool-based spanning rule; report coverage, width and c next to the current oracle values.
* **[minor] R5-5: NORMAN external methods are not fully independent of the RepoRT training systems (DS_AWW is practically identical to RepoRT AA_LC1)** (Manuscript Section 4.10 (first paragraph) and Section 4.12; code/experiments/run_norman.py; code/experiments/run_sasc.py (NORMAN-mix rule); RepoRT systems 0382-0387; confidence: high for the overlap; low for its impact)
  * Evidence: I matched each of the 37 NORMAN methods (CS_code) against the 297 RepoRT RP systems on shared ik14 blocks (at least 10). DS_AWW vs 0382 'AA_LC1' (Publication - Aalizadeh, a NORMAN retention-time-index dataset in the training set of the all-RepoRT model) share 53 compounds with median |dRT| = 0.066 min and r = 0.998, i.e. effectively the same LC method. Close relatives exist for DS_JBB-AA_LC6 (13 shared, 0.22 min, r 0.995), DS_TSF-AA_LC4, DS_TL-AA_LC5, DS_HT-'Kruve Lab_23', DS_BIJ-'Kruve Lab_11' and DS_QDJ/DS_BQW-Eawag_XBridgeC18 (median |dRT| 0.22-0.30 min, r >= 0.98). Section 4.10 states that the NORMAN data are 'retention data that played no role in model development'. DS_AWW shows no visible advantage (proposed, 20 random calibrants: coverage 0.897, relative width 0.535 vs median 0.551, rank 17/37), so the effect on Table S22 is probably negligible. The RepoRT systems used to learn the NORMAN-mix SASC factor may include AA_LC1.
