  * Corrective action: Reword the claim (e.g. 'methods not used as target systems; one method closely matches a RepoRT training system'). Add a NORMAN-RepoRT method-overlap table to Section S1.
  * Required experiment: Sensitivity analysis: retrain the all-RepoRT base and difficulty models without the RepoRT systems that match a NORMAN method (at least 10 shared compounds, median |dRT| <= 0.3 min, r >= 0.98), or drop DS_AWW, then recompute Table S22 and the NORMAN SASC result.
* **[minor] R5-6: Connectivity-block cleaning drops compounds in an outcome-related way, using a fixed 0.2-min threshold; main text misdescribes the rule** (code/src/conformrt/data/repo_rt.py load_system (spread/keep); manuscript Section 3.1; Supplementary S1 lines 14-18; data_processed/systems.csv column n_dropped_dup_blocks; confidence: high for the facts; low for the magnitude)
  * Evidence: repo_rt.load_system drops every ik14 block whose RT spread exceeds 0.2 min. This removes not only separated stereoisomers but also replicate entries of the identical InChIKey: S1 says '(stereoisomers and duplicate entries)', while main-text Section 3.1 says only 'stereoisomer entries ... removed otherwise'. In systems.csv, 2,357 blocks were dropped from the 228 targets, concentrated in a few systems: 0390 Brungs_NIH 871 (n_raw 7,333 -> 2,609 kept; 25% of its blocks), 0391 Brungs_MCE 662, 0383 AA_LC2 192; 12 targets lose more than 5%. 5,152 of the 65,590 retained RTs are averages of several entries. The absolute 0.2 min is 2.6% of the median BGC RT span (7.8 min) but 0.8% of the Folberth span (25.9 min). The dropped compounds are exactly those whose 2D structure has an ambiguous RT, where windows are most likely to fail, so the bias favours coverage (probably small in aggregate). Column length and particle size are also coded 0 for 22 RP systems, which S1 does not list.
  * Corrective action: Correct the main-text description. Report the dropped blocks per system in Table S1 and the missing col_len/particle counts in S1. Justify the absolute threshold or switch to a span-relative one.
  * Required experiment: Cleaning sensitivity analysis for the main setting (n=20, alpha=0.10; proposed, jackknife+, Gaussian): (a) tolerance = 1% of the system's RT span; (b) keep the dropped blocks and score a 2D candidate as retained only if the observed RT (one stereoisomer entry, chosen at random) lies in the window. Report the coverage change overall and for the Brungs/Aalizadeh systems.
* **[minor] R5-7: Licensing and redistribution: third-party NORMAN ESI files are redistributed without a license statement; CC BY-SA compliance for derived RepoRT data is incomplete** (code/data/raw/norman_ilc/*; code/README.md Section 7; manuscript Data availability and Section 3.1; confidence: medium (the RSC ESI license was not verified here))
  * Evidence: The code package contains code/data/raw/norman_ilc/malm_kruve_2025_supp.zip and the extracted RSC ESI files (AN-150-D5AN00323G-s001.zip, -s002.xlsx, SI/data/NORMAN_interlab_RT_data.csv), the RepoRT raw data, and the RepoRT-derived files code/data/processed/*.parquet and features.npz. README Section 7 says only 'Code: MIT. Data derived from RepoRT remain under CC BY-SA 4.0'. No license or attribution is given for the NORMAN ESI files, and the Data availability statement says only '(open access)'. The manuscript states that 'All code and results are provided as Supplementary Material', so these files would be redistributed with an Elsevier article. CC BY-SA 4.0 Section 3 requires attribution, a link to the license, an indication of changes (here: connectivity-block merging and dropping, metadata coding) and ShareAlike licensing of adapted material. The RepoRT archive SHA-256 is given only in truncated form ('4eeeb46a…6091').
  * Corrective action: Check and state the license of the Malm & Kruve ESI. If it is not CC BY, or is unclear, remove the raw NORMAN files from the package and rely on download_data.py. Add a DATA_LICENSE/ATTRIBUTION file for RepoRT (authors, version v1.0, DOI, license link, list of modifications) that licenses the derived data as CC BY-SA 4.0. Deposit the derived data on Zenodo under CC BY-SA 4.0 rather than only in the publisher's supplementary files. Give the full SHA-256 values in Section S1.
  * Required experiment: None (documentation and packaging only).

Verified previous fixes: No dataset/leakage fixes from earlier cycles to check (cycle 1). Earlier audits checked: claim-evidence audit C26 marked the calibrant-selection code SUPPORTED but did not address its use of test-compound RTs (now R5-4); citation audit confirmed the RepoRT v1.0 Zenodo record is licensed CC BY-SA 4.0 (consistent).; Checked and correct: inner cross-fitting of the difficulty model. driver.Experiment.difficulty_training_rows scores fold-g systems with fm.get(frozenset({f,g})) and excludes SMRT from the simulation, as described in Section 2.5.; Checked and correct: SASC factors are learned with sigma_fg fitted on rows from folds other than g and base models trained without {f,g} (run_sasc.py), as stated in Section 2.6; B7 also uses {f,g}/{f} models (run_metagp.py lines 134/141).; Checked and correct: S1's statement on missing metadata. RepoRT temperature/flow/t0 are coded 0 (116/33/37 systems, no NaN in training), NORMAN passes NaN, and LightGBM routes NaN like 0 when no NaN was seen in training, so both data sets are encoded the same way.

### R6_robustness_generalisation — major revision

The core result holds up. With randomly drawn calibrants on RepoRT, the conformal windows reach nominal coverage on average, and the numbers can be rebuilt from the raw per-draw outputs (proposed windows, n = 20, α = 0.10: mean 0.911 over 4,560 system×draw evaluations). The generalisation claims, however, go further than the evidence supports.

(1) In the one realistic external test (NORMAN laboratory calibrant mixes), the proposed windows did no better than the ±z·RMSE rule that the paper tells readers not to use. Coverage was 0.851 for both. The proposed windows had a worse interval score (11.99 vs 10.77 min, also worse than GP at 11.61), and more methods fell below 0.85 (54 % vs 35 %). The text compares the proposed windows only with GP.

(2) Practitioners are told the normalisation pays off for long RP gradients. This post hoc rule fails on the 14 NORMAN methods with long gradients: the proposed windows were 4.5–21 % worse than jackknife+ (p = 0.035–0.27). It also fails on the 18 long HILIC systems (+13 %).

(3) The guarantee holds only on average over calibrant sets. One calibrant draw in six gave less than 85 % retention, at both n = 20 and n = 40. At n = 40 and α = 0.10 the Gaussian rule matched the proposed windows on both mean and per-draw coverage. The claim that 'a laboratory ... obtains about 90 % retention ... on its own system' is therefore not supported.

(4) HILIC coverage was valid, but with windows covering 66–77 % of the RT range at n = 20 and more than 100 % at n = 10. No decoy analysis was run for HILIC, and 23 of the 37 HILIC systems come from one fold.

(5) 73 % of the RepoRT test compounds were in the base model's training data (95 % for NORMAN), so the headline retention does not represent NTS unknowns. The paper's own strata give 0.78–0.84 for novel compounds. No experiment uses seen calibrants with unseen analytes.

The calibrant-error and 'RT-spanning' stress tests are built so that they can only make the windows conservative or close to exchangeable. The pre-registered robustness hypothesis H5 failed for commonly measured calibrants and for novel compounds, but the manuscript never gives a verdict on it.

Issues 1–3 come from my own re-analysis of results/raw/*.parquet (main_rows, norman_rows, gen_hilic_rows). The other numbers come from Tables S2, S9, S19, S21, S22 and S23. Earlier audit fixes in this area are in place (see verified_previous_fixes).

* **[major] R6-01: Realistic external test (NORMAN lab calibrant mixes): the proposed windows were no better than the ±z·RMSE rule the paper advises against, and the text compares them only with GP** (Results 'External validation on interlaboratory NTS data', paragraph 2; Table 4; Practical recommendations (i) and (iii); Highlight 1; Conclusions, first two sentences; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Source: Table S22 (tables/table10_norman_external.csv), fixed_mix scenario, α = 0.10, 37 methods.
- Mean coverage: proposed 0.851, Gaussian ±z·RMSE 0.851, jackknife+ 0.869, P-spline 0.837, GP 0.777, split 0.916.
- Mean interval score: proposed 11.99, Gaussian 10.77, jackknife+ 10.70, P-spline 10.62, GP 11.61, split 13.81. The proposed windows scored worse than every comparator except split conformal, GP included.
- Share of methods with coverage < 0.85: proposed 54.1 %, Gaussian 35.1 %, jackknife+ 37.8 %, P-spline 43.2 %.
- Per method (results/raw/norman_rows.parquet), the proposed windows covered less than the Gaussian window in 18 of 37 methods and more in 12.
- At α = 0.20, the proposed windows had the lowest coverage of all non-GP windows: 0.735 vs Gaussian 0.764, P-spline 0.757, jackknife+ 0.749.
- With random subsets of 20 calibration chemicals: proposed 0.849 vs Gaussian 0.845; interval score 12.33 vs 11.36.

The manuscript reports only 'mean coverage of the suspects' RTs was 0.851 ... for the proposed windows and 0.869 for jackknife+, i.e. below the nominal 0.90 but still above the GP intervals (0.777)'. Meanwhile, Recommendation (i) says 'Do not use fixed, proportional or parametric RT tolerances ...' and Highlight 1 says 'Parametric and fixed RT windows lose correct structures on new LC systems'.
  * Corrective action: - Report every comparator (coverage, interval score, share of methods < 0.85) for the lab-mix and 20-calibration-chemical scenarios in the main text.
- Add an interval-score column to Table 4.
- State plainly that with real laboratory mixes the proposed windows were no better than ±z·RMSE and were less efficient.
- Delete 'still above the GP intervals' as the only comparison.
- Limit Recommendation (i) and Highlight 1 to the conditions the evidence supports: random or representative calibrants; for the Gaussian rule, n ≤ 30 or α ≠ 0.10.
  * Required experiment: - Run paired two-sided Wilcoxon signed-rank tests, with Holm correction within each metric family, of the proposed windows against Gaussian, P-spline and jackknife+. Use the per-method interval score and |coverage gap| in the fixed_mix (37) and pool_random (32) scenarios, and add the results to Table S22.
- If a second dataset with a real fixed calibrant mix is available (e.g. RTI calibrant sets), repeat the comparison there.
* **[major] R6-02: The post hoc rule 'normalisation pays off for long RP gradients' fails on the external long-gradient methods and on long HILIC systems** (Abstract ('Normalisation improved efficiency for longer-than-median runs (... −17 %)'); Results 'Heterogeneity of the gain across systems' ('the normalised version pays off as runs get longer'); Practical recommendation (iii); Conclusions ('more efficient in long reversed-phase gradients'); Limitations; confidence: high that the rule does not replicate; medium on effect size (14 long external methods))
  * Evidence: Method: my re-analysis of results/raw/norman_rows.parquet. RT span = width_mean / width_rel_mean, which equals max−min RT as defined in system_eval.py:75,170. I used the paper's own threshold, the RepoRT median span of 16.8 min; 14 of 37 NORMAN methods exceed it.

Median relative change in the per-method mean interval score, proposed vs jackknife+ (positive = proposed worse), for these 14 long methods:
- random calibrants, n = 20: +4.5 % (proposed better in only 36 %; Wilcoxon p = 0.27)
- random calibrants, n = 10: +21.2 % (p = 0.035)
- laboratory mix: +8.6 % (p = 0.035)
- random 20 calibration chemicals: +8.2 % (p = 0.042)

Spearman ρ between span and relative change was +0.15 to +0.22, the opposite sign to RepoRT (−0.53).

HILIC systems with span > 16.8 min (18 of 37; gen_hilic_rows.parquet, n = 20): +13.3 %.

In RepoRT itself, the link with span is mostly between source groups: the fold-adjusted partial ρ is −0.16, and the gain is concentrated in Folberth (−18.2 %) and Souihi+Stoffel (−10.1 %) (Table 3).
  * Corrective action: - Present the long-gradient rule as a post hoc, source-confounded observation that did not replicate externally, and put the NORMAN and HILIC long-gradient results next to it.
- Remove the rule from Recommendation (iii) and from the Abstract, or qualify it there.
- Recommend plain jackknife+ as the default and present the meta-learned normalisation as exploratory.
- Qualify the 'Proposed' method's efficiency advantage, which is shown only for two RepoRT source groups.
  * Required experiment: - Report the span-stratified comparison of the proposed windows against jackknife+ for NORMAN (all scenarios, n = 10 and 20) and HILIC in the Supplement, with Wilcoxon tests.
- On RepoRT, fit a mixed model (relative interval-score change ~ log(RT span) + (1 | source group)) to estimate how much the span effect holds within sources.
- If the rule is kept, pre-specify it and test it on a further external dataset.
* **[major] R6-03: Retention for a single calibrant set is much less controlled than the text implies; at n = 40 the Gaussian rule matches the conformal windows** (Results 'Candidate filtering with isomeric decoys' (last sentences); Results 'Coverage of current retention-time windows' ('no target system had a mean coverage below 0.85'); Highlights 2–3; Significance; Conclusions ('a design choice rather than an unknown'); Practical recommendation (ii); confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Source: results/raw/main_rows.parquet, n = 20, α = 0.10, 4,560 system×draw evaluations.
- Proposed windows: 5th/10th percentile coverage 0.764/0.811; 17.3 % of calibrant draws < 0.85 and 8.4 % < 0.80. Jackknife+: 18.4 % and 9.1 %.
- For systems with ≥ 300 test compounds (binomial test-sampling SD ≈ 0.017), 15.6 % of draws are still < 0.85. The spread therefore comes from the calibrant set, not from test-set noise.
- 28.5 % of systems had at least a quarter of their draws below 0.85.
- n = 10: 17.0 % of draws < 0.85 (5th percentile 0.723).
- n = 40: proposed 16.9 % vs Gaussian 17.7 % of draws < 0.85, with mean coverage 0.903 for both (Table S2: Gaussian at n = 40, α = 0.10, 0.903, with 0.9 % of systems < 0.85).

The manuscript nevertheless states: 'a laboratory that chooses α=0.10 obtains about 90 % retention of correct structures on its own system, whereas the retention delivered by a nominal 90 % parametric interval is unknown in advance'. It also stresses that 'no target system had a mean coverage below 0.85', which is an average over 20 draws that no laboratory experiences. Jackknife+ has no training-conditional guarantee (Bian & Barber 2023, already cited).
  * Corrective action: - State that the guarantee is an average over calibrant sets and compounds.
- Give the per-calibrant-set spread in the main text, e.g. 'with 20 or 40 calibrants about one laboratory in six would retain < 85 %'.
- Qualify the 'unknown in advance' contrast with parametric windows by n and α: at n = 40 and α = 0.10 the Gaussian window was equally well calibrated, both on average and per draw.
- Present 1/α − 1 as the minimum for a finite window, not as a sufficient or recommended minimum.
  * Required experiment: - Add a table of per-draw coverage quantiles (5th, 10th, 50th) and the shares < 0.85 and < 0.80 for all methods, n and α.
- Implement a calibration-conditional (PAC) variant: split or cross-conformal, with the quantile index taken from the Beta/binomial bound so that P(coverage ≥ 1 − α) ≥ 1 − δ, with δ = 0.1.
- Report its width and interval-score cost, and the number of calibrants needed to control retention per laboratory.
* **[major] R6-04: Headline retention is measured mostly on compounds already in the training data; the NTS-relevant novel-analyte case is not tested directly** (Abstract (Results); Experimental 'Evaluation protocol and leakage control'; Results 'Conditional coverage' and 'External validation'; Limitations; Practical recommendation (iv); confidence: medium-high (numbers verified; the claim that NTS unknowns are mostly unseen is domain reasoning))
  * Evidence: - 73 % of RepoRT test compounds at n = 20 were in the base model's training data (Experimental), and 95 % of the NORMAN compounds were (Addendum C). The external validation therefore does not test novelty at all.
- The paper's own strata (Table S9, random calibrants) give 0.835 for unseen compounds and 0.781 for unseen compounds with Tanimoto 0.4–0.7. That is 6–12 points below nominal for the case closest to an NTS unknown.
- Calibrants are purchasable reference standards and are likely to be in public RT data. The correct structure for an unidentified feature usually is not. Exchangeability between calibrants and analytes is therefore systematically violated in the target application, in a direction that lowers coverage.
- No experiment draws calibrants from seen compounds and evaluates unseen ones.
- Pre-registered H5(e), 'strict novel-compound subsets' (EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md), was addressed only by splitting the random-draw results by novelty afterwards.
- The Abstract reports 90.9–91.8 % retention without mentioning the 73 % seen share.
  * Corrective action: - State in the Abstract and the Conclusions that the headline retention refers to test compounds of which 73 % (95 % for NORMAN) were in the base model's training data, and that unseen compounds were covered at 0.78–0.84.
- Limit the NTS claims accordingly.
  * Required experiment: (a) Seen calibrants, unseen analytes: draw n = 20 calibrants only from seen compounds and evaluate on unseen compounds and on Tanimoto bins (10 draws per system).
(b) Compound cold-start: for a fold-stratified subset of about 50 systems, retrain the base and difficulty models without the test compounds and their Tanimoto ≥ 0.7 neighbours, then report coverage and width.
(c) Reweight the existing strata to the novelty distribution of a realistic candidate list (e.g. the share of NORMAN SusDat or PubChemLite structures that have a RepoRT RT), and report the expected retention.
(d) Test whether SASC with a 'seen → unseen' rule, replayed on the training systems, restores coverage.
* **[major] R6-05: HILIC coverage is achieved with windows covering two-thirds to all of the RT range; no filtering value is shown and one fold dominates** (Abstract; Results 'Generalisation to another base model and to HILIC'; Table 4; Practical recommendation (iii); Conclusions; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Source: Table S21 (tables/table6_generalisation.csv), HILIC, α = 0.10. Median window width relative to the RT span:
- n = 20: proposed 0.773, jackknife+ 0.656
- n = 40: 0.713 and 0.563
- n = 10: 1.062 and 0.816, so the proposed windows were wider than the whole chromatographic range
- For comparison, the main RP setting: 0.35

By fold (results/raw/gen_hilic_rows.parquet): 23 of the 37 HILIC target systems belong to fold 1 (median 50 test compounds); the other folds have 8, 2 and 4. The two fold-3 systems had relative widths of 1.24 (proposed) and 1.12 (jackknife+).

No isomeric-decoy analysis was run for HILIC. Even so, the Abstract lists 'HILIC 90.3 %–91.4 %', the Conclusions say the windows 'restored the promised rate ... on HILIC systems', and Recommendation (iii) says 'plain jackknife+ suffices for ... HILIC'.
  * Corrective action: - Report HILIC widths relative to the RT span in the main text and in Table 4, and state that windows this wide remove few candidates.
- Report per-fold HILIC results and note that one fold supplies 23 of the 37 systems.
- Replace 'suffices for HILIC' with a statement that, with the available training data, order transfer gives nearly uninformative windows in HILIC.
  * Required experiment: - Run the isomeric-decoy filtering analysis on the HILIC systems (draws 0–4, n = 20, α = 0.05, 0.10 and 0.20) and report retention against decoy elimination.
- Report per-fold HILIC coverage and width, with a cluster bootstrap over source groups.
* **[moderate] R6-06: No verdict is given for the pre-registered robustness hypothesis H5, two of whose conditions failed; the 'only deviation' statement is inaccurate or incomplete** (Experimental 'Evaluation protocol and leakage control' (deviation sentence); Results 'Coverage of current retention-time windows' (hypothesis statements); Abstract; confidence: high for the reporting gap; medium on whether H5(e) counts as 'not run')
  * Evidence: EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md, H5: 'conclusions hold for (a) both base retention models, (b) calibrant counts 10–40, (c) non-random calibrant selection (retention-spanning; commonly-measured compounds), (d) noisy calibrant RTs, (e) strict novel-compound subsets.'

What the results show:
- (c) failed: commonly measured calibrants gave 0.815, with 53.5 % of systems < 0.85 (Table S19).
- (e) failed if the novelty strata answer it (unseen 0.835; Tanimoto 0.4–0.7 0.781). Otherwise it was not run as planned.

The manuscript gives verdicts only for H1 and H2 ('These results support the pre-specified hypotheses ... (H1) ... (H2)'). It states that 'the only deviation from it is the omitted similarity term of B6'. SASC (Addendum D) was specified after the H5(c) failure was seen, yet it appears in the Abstract with the same prominence as the pre-specified results.
  * Corrective action: - Add a short table of H1–H5, listing each H5 sub-condition, with a verdict (supported / not supported / not tested) and the supporting evidence.
- State in the Results that H5(c) and H5(e) were not supported, and that SASC is a post hoc response to that failure.
- Correct the 'only deviation' sentence if H5(e) was not run as planned.
  * Required experiment: None, apart from R6-04 if H5(e) is to be tested as planned.
* **[moderate] R6-07: The calibrant-error tests can only make conformal windows conservative; errors that break exchangeability (drift, analyte RT error) are untested** (Results 'Robustness to calibrant selection and calibrant errors'; Introduction (iv); Table 4; code/src/conformrt/evaluation/system_eval.py:87–90; confidence: high on the design limitation; low-medium on the size of the effect)
  * Evidence: system_eval.py:87–90 perturbs only the calibrant RTs:
- 'y_cal = y_cal + rng.normal(0, 0.02 * span_sys, size=n)'
- 'y_cal[rng.integers(n)] += 0.10 * span_sys'
The test RTs are untouched.

Inflating the calibration residuals while the analytes stay unchanged widens the windows by construction. That is why every conformal method became conservative (0.913–0.928; Table 4).

In practice the main risk runs the other way:
- calibrants measured in a different sequence or batch, or before column ageing, which shifts the analytes systematically relative to the calibrants;
- larger apex-RT error for low-abundance features than for calibrant standards.
Neither is simulated, yet Introduction (iv) and Table 4 present the experiment as a test of robustness to calibrant errors.

The size of any resulting under-coverage is unknown and may be small given median widths of about 4.7 min. This is a gap in the evidence, not a demonstrated failure.
  * Corrective action: - Describe the tested perturbations as benign by construction, since they enlarge the calibration residuals, and do not generalise from them to 'calibrant errors'.
- Add the experiments below and report coverage as a function of perturbation size.
  * Required experiment: At n = 20 and n = 40, with the calibrants unperturbed, add to the test RTs:
(i) a constant shift δ of 0.5, 1, 2 and 5 % of the span;
(ii) a linear drift that reaches δ at the end of the gradient;
(iii) Gaussian noise with an SD of 1–2 % of the span.
Also run:
(iv) the same noise added to both calibrants and test RTs;
(v) a swap of the RTs of two calibrants, to mimic misassignment.
Report coverage and interval score for the proposed windows, jackknife+, Gaussian and split conformal.
* **[moderate] R6-08: 'RT-spanning' calibrants are drawn with knowledge of the analytes' own RT distribution; designed mixes and truncated calibrant ranges are untested** (Results 'Robustness to calibrant selection and calibrant errors', paragraph 1; Results 'Selection-aware system-level calibration' (span factor); Practical recommendation (ii); code/src/conformrt/evaluation/system_eval.py:63–65; confidence: high on the code; medium on the practical impact)
  * Evidence: system_eval.py:63–65: 'order = np.argsort(y); return np.array([rng.choice(b) for b in np.array_split(order, n)])'. Here y holds the RTs of all compounds of the target system, test compounds included.

Each calibrant is therefore a random draw from a quantile block of the analytes' own RT distribution. A laboratory does not have this information. The procedure keeps the calibrants close to exchangeable with the analytes, which explains the slight conservativeness (0.928).

How the manuscript uses it:
- It calls this the situation 'usual when a calibrant mix is designed'.
- It uses the result to support Recommendation (ii).
- It derives a SASC factor c = 0.84–0.86 that narrows the windows from 4.60 to 3.95 min.

No scenario truncates the calibrant range. The external data barely test extrapolation: only 82 of 1,245 suspect evaluations in NORMAN fell outside the calibrant range (Table S23). In RepoRT, coverage outside the calibrant range stayed below nominal (0.879; 0.746 with commonly measured calibrants).
  * Corrective action: - Describe the span scenario as quantile-stratified random sampling from the analytes' RT distribution, not as a designed mix.
- Do not recommend applying c < 1 to real RT-spanning mixes until this has been tested with a design that does not use the analytes' RTs.
  * Required experiment: (a) Time-equidistant mix: the compound nearest to each of n equally spaced times between the dead time and the last eluter.
(b) Chemically diverse mix: MaxMin selection on Morgan fingerprints.
(c) Truncated-range mix: calibrants drawn only from the central 60 % of the RT range.
For each, report coverage, coverage against extrapolation distance (in bins of distance beyond the calibrant score range), and a rerun of SASC with the matching rule.
* **[moderate] R6-09: Transfer across mobile-phase pH and for ionisable compounds is untested (214 of 228 systems acidic), and the external strata contradict the internal 'no difference' result** (Table 1; Results 'Error analysis'; Theory 'Meta-learned difficulty model' (ionisation features; pH class among the system descriptors); Table S23; confidence: medium)
  * Evidence: Table 1: 214 of the 228 RP target systems use acidic mobile phases; 14 do not.

The manuscript reports that 'coverage of the proposed windows did not differ between ionisable and neutral compounds (0.904 versus 0.903)'. This result is dominated by acidic systems, where charge states rarely differ between training and target systems. No results are reported separately for the non-acidic target systems.

In NORMAN (Table S23, lab mix):
- proposed windows: ionisable 0.962 (over-covered), neutral 0.825 (under-covered)
- jackknife+: ionisable 0.942, neutral 0.851
The learned normalisation shifted width from neutral to ionisable compounds. Neutral compounds, the majority class (1,014 of 1,245 evaluations), ended up worse than without normalisation.
  * Corrective action: - State that transfer to neutral or basic-pH systems is essentially untested.
- Report the NORMAN ionisable/neutral reversal and discuss it as evidence that the ionisation and system features do not transfer.
- Do not imply general robustness for ionisable compounds.
  * Required experiment: - Report coverage, width and interval score for the 14 non-acidic RP target systems, including ionisable × pH-class strata, for the proposed windows, jackknife+ and Gaussian.
- Report the NORMAN results by mobile-phase pH class.
- Run the ablations without ionisation features and without system features on NORMAN, to test whether these feature groups cause the under-coverage of neutral compounds.
* **[minor] R6-10: Infinite windows are counted as covered in the mean coverage of the external analyses** (Results 'External validation on interlaboratory NTS data', paragraph 2; Table S22; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Table S22 (fixed_mix):
- Split conformal at α = 0.10 had 2 infinite systems (mix sizes 12 and 15; norman_rows.parquet). They are counted with coverage 1.0 inside the reported 0.916; over finite windows the figure is about 0.911.
- The proposed windows at α = 0.05 had 4 infinite systems (10.8 %) inside the reported 0.910; over finite windows it is about 0.899, against a nominal 0.95.

The text says 'split conformal windows, which are wider, reached 0.916' without mentioning the infinite windows.
  * Corrective action: - Report coverage over finite windows separately from the share of infinite windows, as is already done for Fig. 3.
- Say in the text that two methods had infinite split-conformal windows.
  * Required experiment: None; recompute from the existing outputs.
* **[minor] R6-11: The 'second base model' uses the same featurisation and model family, so it tests little of base-model generality for the efficiency claims** (Results 'Generalisation to another base model and to HILIC'; Limitations; code/src/conformrt/evaluation/driver.py:147–150; confidence: high on the facts; low on the impact)
  * Evidence: driver.py:147–150 fits SMRTRegressor on the same FeatureStore (data.fs: 217 RDKit descriptors plus Morgan counts) as the XGBoost ranking model. Both are gradient-boosted tree ensembles. The two base models differ in training target and data, not in representation or model class.

The Limitations state that for 'stronger, condition-aware base models ... the conformal layer applies to them unchanged'. That holds for validity, which follows from the theory, but the efficiency gain of the meta-learned σ depends on how the base model's errors are structured and has not been shown for other models.
  * Corrective action: - Describe the second base model as a change of training data and objective within the same feature space.
- Limit the 'applies unchanged' statement to validity.
  * Required experiment: Optional: on two folds, repeat the main comparison of the proposed windows against jackknife+ with a structurally different base model, e.g. a graph neural network or a published pretrained RT model, retraining the difficulty model on its residuals.

Verified previous fixes: C70: the commonly-measured-calibrant sentence now excludes B6 ('All windows except the very wide jackknife+ normalised by ensemble spread (0.902; median width 10.9 min)'). Adequate.; C71: the mechanism for common calibrants is now hedged ('presumably easier to predict ... for example, more often present in the base model's training data'). Adequate.; C74/C75: the SMRT base-model comparison now reports the paired median of −0.39 min, better in 68 % of systems, and discloses the training-data confound. Adequate.; C76: the HILIC causal attribution is hedged ('possibly too noisy', 'although HILIC also differs in its retention mechanism', 'appears to require'). Adequate as hedging, but the widths are still not reported (see R6-05).; C79: the 95 % overlap of NORMAN compounds with the training data is disclosed. Adequate as disclosure, but the consequence for novelty is not drawn (see R6-04).; C80: the NORMAN random-calibrant result now reads 'no efficiency gain (p = 0.32)', with reduced efficiency for calibration chemicals (Holm p = 0.02 and 0.01). Adequate, but see R6-02 for the remaining long-gradient claim.; C83: the number of systems in the 20-calibration-chemical scenario (32) is now stated. Adequate.; C86: the SASC widths now use per-system mean widths (6.01 vs 3.86 min; 4.60 to 3.95 min), consistent with Table S19. Adequate.; C90: the ionisation mechanism is now marked as untested, with ionisable vs neutral coverage (0.904 vs 0.903). Adequate for RepoRT, but contradicted by the NORMAN strata (see R6-09).; C01/C02/C06/C98: the Highlights, Abstract and Conclusions now limit NORMAN validity to random calibrants, add the exchangeability proviso, and say 'corrected on average'. Adequate.

### R7_computation — major revision

Through the lens of computational efficiency and deployment, the conformal layer is cheap in absolute terms. Its validity comes from plain jackknife+, which needs only the base model and n leave-one-out monotone maps. The paper's own data do not show that the added "proposed" machinery is worth its cost on the application endpoint. That machinery is a meta-learned LightGBM difficulty model with inner cross-fitting, RDKit physicochemical and ionisation features, a Tanimoto search against the training set, and nine metadata fields. At matched retention it eliminates slightly fewer isomeric decoys than plain jackknife+ (Table S15: 32.9 vs 33.4 % at 85 %, 26.3 vs 26.6 % at 90 %). Its interval-score gain is confined to one source group and absent on the external, HILIC and short-gradient systems. The cost reporting (Section 3.13, Table S26) understates the method-specific cost. The benchmark sets the novelty features to constants, so the nearest-neighbour Tanimoto search is never timed. I measured that search with the repository's own code at about 7.4 ms per candidate, 21 times the reported 349 µs, so the earlier audit fix C93 is inadequate. The one-off training timing omits the four or five extra inner cross-fitted base models and the protocol simulation. The leave-one-out sigma step is not chunked: its memory grows as n·m·52·4 bytes, and I measured an extra 1.67 GB and 121 s for 200,000 candidates at n = 40. No time or memory figures are given for large candidate lists or for plain jackknife+. For a lab, there is no user-facing API or CLI, no declared set of pretrained deployable models and no documented metadata schema, although the Significance statement presents deployment as needing only calibrants, metadata and candidates. No simpler normalisation, such as a geometry-only scale or a Mondrian split by inside or outside the calibrant range, was tested to show that meta-learning across more than 200 systems is needed for the one benefit that is clearly shown: better coverage outside the calibrant range. Measurements were made in the scratchpad with the repository code: per-candidate Tanimoto timing over 300 random RepoRT compounds against 28,916 training compounds, and _sigma_matrix plus jackknife_plus with m = 200,000 and n = 40.

* **[major] R7-1: The added complexity of the meta-learned normalisation brings no gain over plain jackknife+ on the candidate-filtering endpoint, and Recommendation (iii) rests on interval score alone** (Manuscript Section 3.6 (Candidate filtering), Section 3.3 (Heterogeneity), Section 3.14 Practical recommendations (iii), Abstract 'Results'; Supplementary Tables S14, S15; code/EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md H3/H4; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Table S15 (matched retention): Jackknife+ 85 % → 33.4 % decoys eliminated, Proposed 32.9 %; Jackknife+ 90 % → 26.6 %, Proposed 26.3 %. Table S14: at α = 0.20 the proposed windows eliminate fewer decoys than jackknife+ (−0.6 pp, r = −0.20, p = 0.008); at α = 0.10 −0.1 pp (p = 0.498). Section 3.6 reports only 'not significantly different from jackknife+ (91.5 % and 24.6 %; p = 0.50)', and its matched-retention sentence ('at 90 % retention from 26.3 % (proposed) to 26.7 % (Gaussian)') leaves out the jackknife+ value of 26.6 %. The interval-score gain is concentrated in one source group (Table 3: BGC +3 %, Folberth −18 %, PredRet +0 %, Souihi+Stoffel −10 %, other +0 %; fold-adjusted partial ρ with RT span −0.16). It is absent or negative on NORMAN (11.14 vs 10.70 min, p = 0.32; worse with calibration chemicals, Holm p = 0.02/0.01) and in HILIC (16.70 vs 14.33 min). Recommendation (iii) still says 'the meta-learned normalisation is worthwhile for long reversed-phase gradients', and the Abstract and Highlights present the normalised windows as the proposed method. Pre-specified H4 (EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md l.22, 'practical value') is the filtering endpoint, and the paper never tests it in the long-gradient subgroup where the normalisation is recommended.
  * Corrective action: Make plain jackknife+ the recommended default and present the meta-learned normalisation as an optional add-on. Its demonstrated benefit is conditional coverage outside the calibrant range (0.879 vs 0.755) and under the common-compound rule (0.815 vs 0.746), not candidate elimination. In Section 3.6, report the jackknife+ matched-retention values and the significant α = 0.20 loss. Rewrite Recommendation (iii) so that it rests on the filtering endpoint, not only on the interval score. Add to the Discussion a short cost-benefit statement (inputs, one-off training, per-candidate time, memory) for jackknife+ versus the proposed method.
  * Required experiment: Re-run the decoy analysis (draws 0–4, n = 20, α ∈ {0.05, 0.10, 0.20}) stratified by RT span (above or below the 16.8 min median) and by source fold. Report decoy elimination at matched 85 % and 90 % retention for proposed versus jackknife+ with paired Wilcoxon tests per stratum. Recommend the normalisation for long gradients only if it eliminates more decoys at matched retention there.
* **[major] R7-2: No deployable interface, pretrained models or metadata schema, although the Significance statement says only calibrants, metadata and candidates are needed** (Abstract (Significance); Section 2.9 Implementation; Data availability; code/README.md §6; code/src/conformrt/models/difficulty.py encode_system; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Abstract/Significance: 'The implementation needs only calibrant structures and RTs, routine chromatographic metadata and candidate structures.' code/README.md §6 instead tells users: 'The window for each candidate is computed with `jackknife_plus(...)` using leave-one-out monotone maps and the difficulty model trained on RepoRT (see `evaluation/system_eval.py` for the exact sequence of calls).' I found no function or CLI in src/ that takes calibrant SMILES/RTs, candidate SMILES and system metadata and returns windows. The search for def predict_window/fit_system/calibrate/argparse/entry_points in src/ found nothing. code/cache/base_models holds fold-specific research models (41 files, 168 MB, e.g. ens_fold0_rep0_t200.joblib), and no deployable all-data model set is declared. The difficulty model needs metadata in RepoRT's internal encoding: difficulty.py encode_system expects keys ph_class, modifier, usp, col_len, particle, temp, flow, grad_dur, t0, with fixed vocabularies, for example usp ∈ {L1, L11, L7, L10, L109, L43, other}. It also needs the training-set fingerprints for the 'maxsim'/'seen' features and the exact build_dataset featurisation (217 descriptors, Morgan counts, ionisation SMARTS). None of this is documented for users. Data availability says code 'will be deposited … upon acceptance'.
  * Corrective action: Provide a user-facing entry point, for example conformrt.predict_windows(calibrants_df, candidates_df, system_meta, alpha, method={'jackknife+','normalised'}) and a CLI (calibrants.csv, candidates.csv, system.yaml → windows.csv). Ship versioned pretrained deployable models: a base model trained on all RP sources, the difficulty model and the reference fingerprint set, with checksums. Document the metadata schema, allowed values and missing-value handling. Add an end-to-end worked example on one NORMAN method with expected output. Qualify the Significance sentence to say that the method also needs the released pretrained models and the reference fingerprint set.
  * Required experiment: Add an end-to-end integration test (tests/) that starts from raw SMILES/RT/metadata files of one NORMAN method, runs the public API with the shipped models and reproduces the windows and coverage reported for that method in Table S22 within numerical tolerance.
* **[moderate] R7-3: The reported per-candidate cost omits the Tanimoto novelty search that only the proposed method needs (audit fix C93 is inadequate)** (Manuscript Section 3.13 Computational cost; Supplementary Table S26; code/experiments/benchmark_compute.py; code/src/conformrt/evaluation/protocol.py max_similarity; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: code/experiments/benchmark_compute.py: 'C = fs.physchem(r); C["maxsim"] = 1.0; C["seen"] = 1.0'. The max-Tanimoto search to the base-model training compounds (protocol.py _maxsim_chunk: max(DataStructs.BulkTanimotoSimilarity(_BV[q], _REF))) is therefore never timed. I re-measured it with the repository code (single thread, 300 random RepoRT compounds against the 28,916 unique compounds of the all-RP+SMRT training table): 7,380 µs per candidate, 21 times the reported 349 µs (Table S26). Section 3.13 still says 'computing the window of a candidate structure 349 µs at n = 20 once its descriptors and base score are available, so that the conformal step for 10,000 candidates takes a few seconds (featurisation and base-model scoring excluded)' and concludes 'The conformal layer thus adds no practical cost'. The novelty search is neither featurisation nor base-model scoring; it is a method-specific step that plain jackknife+ does not need. With it, 10,000 candidates take about 74 s on one thread, not 'a few seconds'. Audit item C93 flagged that maxsim was set to 1, but the applied wording does not disclose it.
  * Corrective action: Time the full per-candidate path and report each component separately. Shared components: RDKit featurisation and base-model scoring. Proposed-only components: novelty search and the leave-one-out σ predictions. Both methods: monotone-map predictions and the jackknife+ quantile. Correct the 'few seconds' and 'no practical cost' statements. Because the novelty group gives the second-smallest gain (Table S17: +0.18 min), consider dropping it from the deployable model or replacing the exhaustive search with an indexed or popcount-bounded search, and report its effect.
  * Required experiment: Re-run benchmark_compute.py with the real maxsim/seen computation for 10^3 and 10^4 candidate structures that are not RepoRT compounds (e.g. PubChem isomers of the test formulas), on 1 and 4 threads, and report the time per component. Also evaluate a model without the novelty group on the main protocol (coverage, interval score, decoy elimination at matched retention).
* **[moderate] R7-4: Unchunked leave-one-out σ computation: memory and time scale as O(n·m) with a 52-feature matrix; scalability to large candidate lists is not reported** (code/src/conformrt/evaluation/system_eval.py _sigma_matrix; code/src/conformrt/models/difficulty.py geometry_loo; Manuscript Section 3.13; Table S26; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: system_eval.py _sigma_matrix (loo=True) allocates 'X = np.empty((n * m, len(cols)), dtype=np.float32)' with len(cols) = 52 (21 + 15 + 2 + 5 + 9 features in difficulty.py GROUPS) and predicts all n·m rows with a 400-tree LightGBM in one call. I re-measured it with the repository code (difficulty model fitted on 150,000 fold-0 rows, n_jobs = 1) for m = 200,000 candidates and n = 40: σ step 121 s (606 µs per candidate) and peak RSS +1,672 MB. The jackknife_plus quantile step for the same arrays took 0.21 s. Extrapolating linearly, 10^6 candidates at n = 40 need about 8.3 GB for X alone and about 10 min. The benchmark used 1,000 candidates on one system (`te = ...[:1000]`), and the only memory figure is the 2660 MB peak of a process that also trained the base model. Table S26 shows the per-candidate cost growing with n (180/349/545 µs), and the paper itself recommends about 40 calibrants. The code also has an unused redundancy: in geometry_loo, removing calibrant i changes pos/extrap only if i is the lowest- or highest-scoring calibrant, gap only if i is the nearest calibrant, and cal_span only if i has the minimum or maximum RT, while n_cal = n − 1 for every i. Each candidate therefore has at most about 6 distinct feature rows rather than n.
  * Corrective action: Process candidates in chunks (e.g. 10^4 per block). Evaluate σ only for the ≤6 distinct leave-one-out feature rows per candidate and broadcast the result, which removes most of the time and memory dependence on n while giving identical windows. Report deployment memory separately from training memory.
  * Required experiment: Scaling benchmark for m ∈ {10^3, 10^4, 10^5, 10^6} candidates and n ∈ {10, 20, 40}, for plain jackknife+ and the proposed method (current and deduplicated implementations), with wall time on 1 and 4 threads and peak RSS. Use at least 5 repetitions and report median and IQR. Include a unit test showing that the deduplicated implementation reproduces the current σ_{-i}(x) matrix exactly.
* **[moderate] R7-5: The one-off training cost of the proposed pipeline is understated: inner cross-fitting, protocol simulation, novelty search and SASC replay are not timed** (Manuscript Section 3.13, Section 3.14 (iv), Section 2.9; code/experiments/benchmark_compute.py; code/src/conformrt/evaluation/driver.py; code/experiments/run_sasc.py; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Section 3.13: 'training the retention-order base model on 82,924 retention times took 4.0 min and training the difficulty model on 149,295 simulated residuals 5.3 s … both are done once, on public data.' The difficulty model, however, requires inner cross-fitting. driver.py l.82 'mp = self.fm.get(frozenset({f, g}))' trains one extra base model per inner source group, which is 4 extra per outer fold and 5 extra plus the final model for a deployable all-source model, about 24 min on one thread at 4.0 min each. simulate_calibration_rows runs outside the timer in benchmark_compute.py. The timed difficulty model used novelty features fixed to ones ('nov = {s: (np.ones(...), np.ones(...))}') and fewer rows than the deployed models (332,391–511,908; NORMAN 550,326). The training-set novelty search took 54 s on 4 cores in the main run (manifest_main_full.json maxsim_s). Recommendation (iv) asks laboratories to 'calibrate the windows by replaying that rule on public data' (SASC), whose cost and tooling are not reported anywhere. The total wall time of the full study is not given either (pipeline_status.txt: relaunch 03:11 → PIPELINE_DONE 06:05 on 4 cores, with cached base models).
  * Corrective action: Report the end-to-end one-off cost of building deployable models from raw RepoRT for (a) plain jackknife+ (base model only) and (b) the proposed method (base model, inner cross-fitted models, simulation, novelty search, difficulty model), and (c) the cost of one SASC factor for a user-specified rule. Release the pretrained models so that laboratories do not pay costs (a)–(b) themselves. Report the total wall time and CPU hours of the full study.
  * Required experiment: Instrument run_main.py, run_sasc.py and benchmark_compute.py to log the time and peak memory of each training stage on 1 and 4 threads using the actual fold-sized residual sets. Report the results in an expanded Table S26.
* **[moderate] R7-6: No simpler normalisation was tested, so it is not shown that meta-learning across more than 200 systems is needed for the benefit actually observed** (Manuscript Section 3.7 Ablation; Section 3.5 Conditional coverage; Supplementary Table S17; confidence: medium)
  * Evidence: Table S17 contains only drop-one-group ablations (– novelty +0.18, – ionisation +0.08, – system +0.32, – geometry +0.58, – physicochemical +0.61 min), plus 'no leave-one-out refit' and 'linear map'. The main demonstrated benefit of normalisation is coverage outside the calibrant range (Section 3.5: 0.879 vs 0.755 for jackknife+). That benefit could plausibly come from much cheaper constructions: a Mondrian jackknife+ split by inside or outside the calibrant score range (the authors already suggest Mondrian partitions for novelty), or a σ that uses only the five calibrant-geometry features and needs no RDKit descriptors, Tanimoto search, metadata or inner cross-fitting. Without such a minimal comparator, the cost-benefit question ('is the meta-learned model worth it?') cannot be answered. This is speculative only in that the outcome is unknown.
  * Corrective action: Add minimal-complexity comparators to the ablation and discuss whether the full meta-learned model is needed. If a geometry-only or Mondrian variant performs comparably, recommend it as the low-cost deployable option.
  * Required experiment: On the main protocol (228 RP systems, n ∈ {10, 20, 40}, 20 draws, α = 0.10), evaluate: (a) jackknife+ normalised by a σ trained on geometry features only; (b) a geometry+system σ without compound features or novelty search; (c) a Mondrian jackknife+ with separate quantiles for compounds inside and outside the leave-one-out calibrant score range. Report marginal coverage, interval score, coverage outside the calibrant range, decoy elimination at matched retention, and the per-candidate time and one-off training time of each.
* **[minor] R7-7: The compute benchmark is a single unrepeated run on one system, with no cost figures for jackknife+ or the comparators** (Supplementary Table S26; Manuscript Section 2.2 and Section 3.13; code/experiments/benchmark_compute.py; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: benchmark_compute.py times one calibrant draw (rng seed 0) on one system ('sid = data.targets(60).sort_values("n_compounds").index[-1]', system 0391) with 1,000 test compounds and no repetitions. Table S26 lists only the proposed method. The paper restricts the P-spline, GP and ensemble comparators to n ∈ {10, 20, 40} 'because of their computational cost' (Section 2.2) but never quantifies that cost, and gives no cost for plain jackknife+, which is the alternative the reader needs to weigh. The logged main-run timings (results/raw/main_timing.parquet) show the per-system σ time at n = 20 to be 5.2 times the map time (median), against about 0.9–1.0 at the other n. This unexplained n = 20 anomaly is probably due to extra decoy or ensemble work at the primary n, but it is not documented.
  * Corrective action: Expand Table S26 to one row per method (jackknife+, proposed, split conformal, Gaussian, P-spline, GP, meta-learned GP, ensemble σ), giving calibration time and per-candidate time as median and IQR over ≥5 repetitions on several systems of different size. Explain or remove the n = 20 timing anomaly in main_timing.
  * Required experiment: Repeat the benchmark on 5 systems spanning the size range (e.g. the 10th, 25th, 50th, 75th and 100th percentiles of compounds per system), 5 calibrant draws each, for all methods, on 1 thread.
* **[minor] R7-8: Reproduction runtimes conflict between the manuscript and the README, and the full-mode runtime is not reported** (Manuscript Section 2.9 Implementation; code/README.md §3; code/run_all.py; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Manuscript Section 2.9: 'a quick end-to-end check from a fresh copy (`--mode quick`) runs in about 20 min on 4 CPU cores'. code/README.md §3: 'python run_all.py --mode quick    # pipeline check: tiny models, 2 systems per fold, ~5 min'. run_all.py docstring: '(tiny models, few systems; minutes)'. The full mode is described only as 'several CPU-hours' (README) and is not quantified in the manuscript.
  * Corrective action: Measure the quick and full modes from a clean checkout (including data download and build_dataset, about 234 s per build_summary.json) and state one consistent wall time and CPU-hour figure for each in the manuscript and the README.
  * Required experiment: Run `python run_all.py --mode quick` and `--mode full` from a fresh clone on the reference 4-core machine with timing logged per stage, and report the totals.

Verified previous fixes: C92 (difficulty-model timing on a smaller training set): fix applied. Section 3.13 now says 'the fold-specific models of this study used 332,391–511,908 residuals'. The disclosure is adequate, but the timing was not re-run at the deployed size, and the timed model used constant novelty features.; C93 (per-candidate timing excludes featurisation and scoring): fix applied, but INADEQUATE. The text adds '(featurisation and base-model scoring excluded)', but the benchmark still sets maxsim = seen = 1.0, so the method-specific Tanimoto search (about 7.4 ms per candidate, measured) is neither timed nor disclosed, and the 'few seconds for 10,000 candidates' and 'no practical cost' claims remain (see R7-3).; C94 (peak-memory attribution): fix applied. The text now reads 'peak resident memory of the benchmark process, including base-model training, 2660 MB'. The correction is accurate, but the paper still gives no deployment-time memory figure (see R7-4).; C23 (restriction of P-spline, GP and ensemble comparators to n ∈ {10, 20, 40}): fix applied in Section 2.2. Adequate, but the stated 'computational cost' reason is not quantified anywhere (see R7-7).

### R8_literature_citations — minor revision

I checked 21 DOIs against Crossref (all 2026 entries, the ACA entries, Boswell 2015, Samaraweera 2018, Malm 2024/2025, Metz 2025, Ngan 2025 and Wilcoxon 1945), the RDKit Zenodo DOI against DataCite, and six cited claims against Europe PMC abstracts. All 21 DOIs resolve, and titles, first authors and volumes/pages match the rendered list. The FastRet DOI timed out on the first query and resolved on the retry. The quoted claims for Boswell 2015 (windows 'only needed to be relaxed by 50 % to make them appropriate for all labs'), Malm 2025 (pH and column chemistry most impactful; 37 systems), Morger 2022 (updating the calibration set restores validity) and Samaraweera 2018 ('12 of the ninety-one metabolites were eliminated') are supported. 'Where 4–5 had been expected' is not in the Samaraweera abstract, and the verification record is stale. Most fixes from the previous citation audit are in place. The novelty claim (no conformal RT windows for few-calibrant transfer) survived two further web searches. They found no conformal RT-interval paper for small molecules or peptides. The main literature weaknesses are positioning gaps rather than wrong citations: (i) competing approaches that avoid hard RT windows (retention-order scoring, Bach et al. 2018/2022; RTI-based identification probability, Ngan et al. 2025) are already in the .bib but not cited; (ii) prior art on conformal regression in cheminformatics and on normalised out-of-sample (OOB/LOO) conformal scores is missing, although the normalised jackknife+ is presented as 'proposed'; (iii) regulatory and NORMAN guidance on RT tolerances, and classic NTS window studies, are absent; (iv) the closest prior method (Boswell 2015) is not discussed beyond the Introduction, although its 50 % relaxation closely parallels the paper's c_rho = 1.50–1.70. Several ACA-style formatting defects remain: full journal names, a missing Talanta volume, incomplete conference entries, 'others' in author lists, and inconsistent forms of one author's name.

* **[major] R8-1: Competing RT-evidence approaches that avoid hard windows are omitted, although already in references.bib** (Final_Manuscript.md, Introduction para. 3 ('In practice windows are fixed ...' through '... pass the tolerance windows [@metz2025identification]') and Discussion; manuscript/references.bib keys bach2018retention, bach2022joint, ngan2025machine; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Six of the 11 bibliography entries not cited in Final_Manuscript.md are retention-order or RT-evidence approaches: bach2018retention (Bioinformatics 2018, retention-order prediction for metabolite identification), bach2022joint (Nat Mach Intell 2022, joint MS/MS + retention-order annotation), ngan2025machine (Anal Chem 97 (2025) 18028–18035, doi 10.1021/acs.analchem.5c01873; Europe PMC abstract: 'leveraging data from MS/MS spectra and calibrant-free predicted retention time indices ... to enhance identification probability'), aalizadeh2019development, bonini2020retip and yang2021prediction. The Introduction claims that a too-narrow window 'silently removes the right answer, which no downstream scoring can recover'. The case for calibrated hard windows depends on this claim, yet the manuscript never mentions approaches that use RT as soft evidence (retention-order likelihoods, RT-error features in a classifier) and therefore do not discard candidates. A reader at ACA will ask why a hard filter with guaranteed coverage is preferable to soft scoring.
  * Corrective action: In Introduction para. 2–3, add one or two sentences on the soft-scoring alternatives (Bach et al. 2018, 2022; Ngan et al. 2025), and cite Aalizadeh et al. 2019 for model-error-based RT windows in NTS of emerging contaminants. State when a hard filter is needed: candidate lists too large for MS/MS scoring, identification-probability frameworks that count candidates inside tolerances (Metz 2025), and suspect-screening triage. In the Discussion, note that the conformal p-value of a candidate's observed RT is a calibrated soft score, so the method is compatible with soft scoring.
  * Required experiment: Optional but recommended: on the existing isomeric-decoy sets (Tables S13–S14), report the rank of the correct candidate when candidates are ordered by their jackknife+ conformal p-value instead of being filtered, compared with ordering by absolute RT error. This shows whether the calibrated score adds value as soft evidence. It needs no new data.
* **[moderate] R8-2: Prior art on conformal regression in cheminformatics and on normalised out-of-sample conformal scores is not cited** (Final_Manuscript.md, Introduction para. 4 ('Conformal prediction provides ...' / 'Conformal methods have recently entered analytical chemistry ...'); Theory, 'Normalised jackknife+ with a meta-learned difficulty model (proposed)'; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: The Introduction says 'Conformal methods have recently entered analytical chemistry' and cites only 2026 papers (wiens2026calibrated, bhargav2026group, rakhshaninejad2026reliable, kreutzer2026molecular, ferrandoplo2026candidate). The established cheminformatics literature is not cited: Norinder et al., J Chem Inf Model 54 (2014) 1596–1603 (doi 10.1021/ci5001168), and Svensson et al., J Chem Inf Model 58 (2018) 1132–1140 (doi 10.1021/acs.jcim.8b00054), which is conformal regression for QSAR with normalised nonconformity from an error model, the direct QSPR analogue of the difficulty-normalised windows. The normalised jackknife+ is labelled '(proposed)' in Theory, and Theory states that Barber's argument 'applies verbatim to the scaled score'. Normalised nonconformity combined with out-of-sample (out-of-bag) residuals has an established precedent: Johansson et al., Machine Learning 97 (2014) 155–176 (doi 10.1007/s10994-014-5453-0). Conformalized quantile regression (Romano, Patterson, Candès, NeurIPS 2019), the standard adaptive conformal method, is neither cited nor discussed as an alternative to normalisation. All three DOIs were verified in Crossref.
  * Corrective action: Cite Norinder 2014 and Svensson 2018 as the cheminformatics precedents, and rephrase 'recently entered analytical chemistry' so the 2026 papers are the analytical-chemistry applications within a longer QSAR/QSPR tradition. Cite Johansson et al. 2014 (and Papadopoulos 2011, already cited) for normalised scores with out-of-sample residuals. Make clear that the novelty is the meta-learned, calibrant-geometry- and system-conditioned scale function within jackknife+ for few-calibrant transfer, not normalisation of a LOO score as such. Cite CQR and give one sentence on why it was not used (with n = 10–40 calibrants, quantile regression cannot be fitted on the target system; a meta-learned quantile model would be the analogue).
  * Required experiment: None required. Optional: a CQR-style comparator whose lower/upper quantile models are meta-learned on the training systems (the same LightGBM features as the difficulty model, quantile loss) and conformalised with jackknife+ on the target calibrants. Report coverage and interval score at n = 20, alpha = 0.10, in the same format as Table 2.
* **[moderate] R8-3: Regulatory and NORMAN guidance on RT tolerances, and classic NTS window studies, are not cited** (Final_Manuscript.md, Introduction para. 3; manuscript/references.bib key aca2024qsrrwindows (uncited); confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Introduction para. 3 lists window practice ('fixed (e.g. ±0.1–0.5 min [Metz], ±0.40 min [Kretschmer] or ±3 min [McEachran]) ... ±2 RMSE [Malm]') but omits the sources ACA readers apply in practice. These are the RT-tolerance criteria of Commission Implementing Regulation (EU) 2021/808 and SANTE/11312/2021 for confirmatory identification (±0.1 min), and the NORMAN guidance on suspect and non-target screening (Hollender et al., Environ Sci Eur 35 (2023) 75, doi 10.1186/s12302-023-00779-4, verified in Crossref), which discusses RT-prediction plausibility checks. A widely cited early NTS RT-window study is also absent: Bade et al., Sci Total Environ 538 (2015) 934–941 (doi 10.1016/j.scitotenv.2015.08.078, verified), which used ANN-predicted RTs with error-derived windows for suspect screening. Di Francesco et al., Anal Chim Acta 1303 (2024) 342529 (in the .bib as aca2024qsrrwindows, uncited) used QSRR-predicted RTs to set scheduled-MRM windows for suspect screening. It is a substantive ACA example of predicted-RT windows, not just a journal-fit citation.
  * Corrective action: Add one sentence contrasting confirmatory-analysis tolerances (Regulation (EU) 2021/808; SANTE/11312/2021), which assume a reference standard on the same system, with predicted-RT windows in NTS (Hollender et al. 2023; Bade et al. 2015; Aalizadeh et al. 2019; Di Francesco et al. 2024). Remove the uncited aca2025dualcolumn from the .bib unless a substantive use is found.
  * Required experiment: None.
* **[moderate] R8-4: Boswell et al. 2015, the closest prior method, is not discussed beyond the Introduction; its 50 % relaxation parallels the paper's c_rho = 1.50–1.70** (Final_Manuscript.md, Introduction para. 3; Results section on selection-aware calibration (line ~217); Discussion (no mention of Boswell); confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Boswell 2015 is cited once, in the Introduction ('these windows had to be relaxed by 50 % to be appropriate for all of them'). The Europe PMC abstract (PMID 26292624) confirms this wording. The Results report that the selection-aware factor c 'was 1.50–1.70 for the commonly-measured' calibrants, a near-identical empirical inflation of 50–70 %, but the Discussion does not connect the two. B1–B7 also contain no comparator for confidence-calibrated windows of the Boswell type, and the manuscript does not say why. The reason is presumably that error-propagation windows for retention projection need isocratic retention-factor databases and back-calculated gradients, which RepoRT does not provide.
  * Corrective action: In the Discussion, (a) state that Boswell-type windows could not be benchmarked because RepoRT lacks isocratic retention-factor data and measured gradient profiles, and (b) relate the 1.50–1.70 selection-aware factor to Boswell's 50 % inter-laboratory relaxation. Both are system-level corrections for discrepancies that the per-system error model does not capture, and this paper's factor is derived from a replayed selection rule with a stated (average-over-systems) guarantee rather than set ad hoc.
  * Required experiment: None.
* **[moderate] R8-5: Claim-verification record is stale and does not cover two quantitative claims now in the manuscript** (supplementary/claim_source_verification.md (rows for Samaraweera; no row for Boswell 2015); Final_Manuscript.md Introduction para. 3; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: claim_source_verification.md still lists Samaraweera et al. as '**unverified → removed from manuscript**' (row 'ANN RI windows ±2 SE / ±3 SE'). The manuscript now cites Samaraweera for a different quantitative claim: 'retention-index filtering eliminated 12 of 91 true metabolites where 4–5 had been expected [@samaraweera2018evaluation]'. The table has no row for this claim, nor for Boswell 2015 ('relaxed by 50 %'). Europe PMC abstract PMID 30350614 supports '12 of the ninety-one metabolites were eliminated'. The '4–5 had been expected' part is not in the abstract and appears to be the authors' own derivation (5 % of 91 = 4.55). It is attributable to the source only if the full text (PMC8378237) states the nominal window coverage.
  * Corrective action: Check the Samaraweera full text (PMC8378237) for the nominal coverage of the RI window. If the paper states that about 5 % false negatives were expected, keep the sentence and record the verbatim quote. Otherwise rephrase to separate source fact from inference, e.g. 'eliminated 12 of 91 true metabolites (13 %), compared with about 5 % (4–5 compounds) implied by the nominal window width'. Add rows for Samaraweera (new claim) and Boswell 2015 (abstract PMID 26292624, verified) to claim_source_verification.md, and delete or update the obsolete 'removed from manuscript' rows.
  * Required experiment: None.
* **[minor] R8-6: Bian & Barber 2023 cited for a group-conditional (per-system) coverage point it does not address** (Final_Manuscript.md, Results, selection-aware calibration paragraph (line ~217); confidence: medium)
  * Evidence: Results: 'Because the factor equalises the average over systems rather than the coverage of each system [@bian2023training], individual systems still varied (21 % of the systems remained below 0.…)'. Bian & Barber (EJS 17 (2023) 2044–2066) concerns training-conditional coverage, i.e. coverage conditional on the calibration/training sample, of jackknife+ and full conformal. The point here is marginal-over-groups versus group-conditional coverage in a hierarchical setting, which is what Dunn et al. 2023 and Lee et al. 2026 (both already cited) treat. The general impossibility of conditional guarantees is in Foygel Barber et al., Inf. Inference 10 (2021) 455–482 (doi 10.1093/imaiai/iaaa017, verified).
  * Corrective action: Replace or supplement the citation with dunn2023distribution / lee2026hierarchical (marginal over groups versus within-group coverage) and, if desired, Foygel Barber et al. 2021. Keep bian2023training only where calibrant-draw-conditional coverage is meant (e.g. variability over calibrant draws within a system).
  * Required experiment: None.
* **[minor] R8-7: Bibliography deviates from Analytica Chimica Acta reference style in several entries** (manuscript/references.bib; manuscript/build/elsevier-with-titles.csl; rendered reference list in Final_Manuscript.docx/.pdf ([19], [31], [35], [42], [46], [48], [51], [54], [60], [67]); confidence: high)
  * Evidence: (a) Journal names print in full ('Analytical Chemistry', 'Journal of Chromatography A', ...). elsevier-with-titles.csl requests container-title form='short' (lines 105, 131), but references.bib has no abbreviated journal field, so the CSL falls back to full titles. The ACA Guide for Authors asks for abbreviations according to the ISSN List of Title Word Abbreviations (e.g. 'Anal. Chem.', 'J. Chromatogr. A'). (b) Ref [35] prints 'Talanta (2026) 130941' with no volume. Crossref now gives volume 314, issue date April 2027, article 130941. (c) Ref [51] prints 'ACM Journal of Data Science (2026) 3786352': the DOI suffix appears as an article number, and the author is 'R. Barber'. The same person is 'R.F. Barber' in [32], [68] and [69] and 'R. Foygel Barber' in [67]. (d) Conference entries are incomplete: [48] LightGBM (NeurIPS 30, 2017, pp. 3146–3154), [67] Tibshirani et al. (NeurIPS 32, 2019, pp. 2530–2540) and [46] Fisch et al. (PMLR vol. 139 missing). (e) 'others' appears in the author lists of [42] RDKit and [54] RepoRT v1.0 (bib lines 787 and 837) instead of 'et al.'. (f) '[19] The Analyst' should be 'Analyst', and '[31] Foundations and Trends® in Machine Learning' carries a registered-trademark symbol. (g) The dataset/software references ([54] RepoRT v1.0, [42] RDKit, [49] LightGBM) lack the '[dataset]' label recommended by Elsevier's data-citation guidance. (h) [60] Wilcoxon prints page '80'; the article spans 80–83.
  * Corrective action: Add a 'shortjournal' or 'journal-abbreviation' field (ISO 4) to every article entry, or switch to a CSL that applies an abbreviation list, and rebuild. Update bhargav2026group to volume = {314}, year = {2027}. For lee2026hierarchical, use author 'Barber, Rina Foygel', drop the article-number field, and keep the DOI. Harmonise all Barber entries to one form. Complete the NeurIPS/PMLR volume and page fields. Replace 'and others' with complete author lists or let the CSL produce 'et al.'. Set journal = {Analyst} and remove the ® symbol. Add '[dataset]' to the RepoRT entry. Set pages = {80--83} for Wilcoxon.
  * Required experiment: None.
* **[minor] R8-8: Generic ACA tutorial used as sole authority for leave-source-out validation; uncited ACA entries left in the .bib** (Final_Manuscript.md, Experimental, fold definition (line ~104); manuscript/references.bib; confidence: medium)
  * Evidence: Experimental: '... so that no retention time or chromatographic system of a held-out source group entered model development, as required for a realistic estimate of performance on new systems [@aca2023validation]' (Lopez et al., Anal Chim Acta 1275 (2023) 341532, a general validation tutorial with spectroscopic examples). The bib key name ('aca2023validation') and the two uncited ACA entries (aca2024qsrrwindows, aca2025dualcolumn) suggest journal-fit citations. A reviewer may read the sole reliance on a generic tutorial for grouped/blocked validation as weak support. I have not read the tutorial's full text, so its support for grouped (leave-group-out) validation specifically is unverified.
  * Corrective action: Add a methodological source specific to grouped/hierarchical cross-validation, e.g. Roberts et al., Ecography 40 (2017) 913–929 (doi 10.1111/ecog.02881, verified). Optionally add an RT-domain example of leave-system-out evaluation (Malm & Kruve 2025 or Kretschmer et al. 2026, both already cited). Keep Lopez et al. only if its text explicitly covers group-wise splitting. Rename bib keys to author-year form.
  * Required experiment: None.

Verified previous fixes: schymanski2014identifying: journal now renders 'Environmental Science & Technology' (the HTML entity is gone); vovk2005algorithmic: authors now V. Vovk, A. Gammerman, G. Shafer (not 'Springer-Verlag'); Year/volume corrected: hupatz2025critical 417 (2025), metz2025identification 97 (2025), wiens2026calibrated 98 (2026); Crossref print dates match; papadopoulos2002inductive: booktitle 'Machine Learning: ECML 2002' with editors now present; chen2016xgboost: full title 'XGBoost: A Scalable Tree Boosting System' and proceedings now present; rdkit and lightgbm now print versions; the RDKit Zenodo DOI 10.5281/zenodo.23981945 resolves in DataCite to 'rdkit/rdkit: 2026_09_2 (Q3 2026) Release'; wiens title now capitalises 'Raman'; malm2024norman author 'Frøkjær' now renders correctly; McEachran window now written as '±3 min'; Barber jackknife+ statement now conditions on a symmetric algorithm and on stability for near-nominal coverage; NORMAN data now described as 37 routine NTS methods; Malm 2025 abstract confirms 41 calibration chemicals, 45 suspects, 37 CSs; B3 now explicitly distinguished from García's meta-learned prior, and B7 added as a meta-learned-prior GP comparator; Wiens no longer cited for deployment shift; Bhargav Teja 2026 (Talanta) now carries that point; Previously missing citations now cited and rendered: boswell2015calculation, park2024fewshot, fisch2021fewshot, dunn2023distribution, lee2026hierarchical, papadopoulos2011regression, pedregosa2011scikit, virtanen2020scipy; No citation key in the manuscript is missing from references.bib, and all 69 cited keys render in the DOCX reference list

### R9_associate_editor — major revision

Editorial decision: the paper fits ACA's scope ("data treatment … quantifiable advantages over existing approaches"). It is large, carefully controlled (leave-source-out, 228 RP and 37 HILIC systems, NORMAN external data) and candid about its limits. I would send it to review, but only after a technical return. In its current form it carries a real risk of desk rejection on impact.

Main editorial problems:
(1) The base model's point accuracy on the target systems is never reported. So readers cannot tell whether the very wide windows (median 4.68 min, 35 % of the RT span) and the poor fixed-window results reflect current practice or a weak base model.
(2) The contribution labelled "Proposed" (the meta-learned normalisation) gives a small and inconsistent gain:
   - Most of the validity comes from off-the-shelf jackknife+.
   - At matched retention, the conformal windows filter slightly worse than the parametric ones.
   - In HILIC, the "invalid" Gaussian window has the lowest interval score.
(3) The obvious practical competitor, a ±k·RMSE window with k calibrated on public systems, is missing. The fixed ±2·RMSE rule already reaches 91.9 % coverage with no system below 0.85, yet the abstract says "conventional windows discard many correct structures".
(4) There is no real NTS case with database candidate lists. The authors' own ACA benchmark recommended one.
(5) The generative-AI declaration falls short:
   - The AI was used for code development and data analysis, which is not described in Methods.
   - Placeholder text remains in the declaration.
   - The git history shows the work was authored by an AI agent.
(6) Pre-specification is a selling point, but only H1 and H2 are adjudicated. H5(c) failed, and a post hoc RT-span subgroup is presented in the abstract without a post hoc label.

Also needed:
- Remove the submission placeholders.
- Condense the length.
- Recalibrate the claims about the guarantee.
- Fix the cover letter, data citation and data-availability details.

* **[major] R9-01: Point accuracy of the transferred base model is never reported, so window widths and fixed-window losses cannot be interpreted against the state of the art** (Sections 2.2, 4.1, 4.2, 4.6, 4.9, Table 2, Fig. 2b; EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md Metrics; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: A search of Final_Manuscript.md and Supplementary_Material.md finds no MAE, RMSE, median absolute error or rank correlation for the transferred predictions on the target systems. The only RMSE mentioned is the ±k·RMSE window rule. The scale can be inferred from Table 2: the Gaussian ±1.645·RMSE window has a median width of 4.25 min, so the LOO RMSE is about 1.3 min. Proposed windows have a median width of 4.68 min, 'i.e. 35 % of the system's RT span' (Section 4.2). Fig. 2b shows per-system mean widths up to about 40 min. HILIC proposed windows are 12.79 min wide (Table S21). The Introduction cites much tighter windows used in practice with stronger models ('±0.1–0.5 min [@metz2025identification], ±0.40 min [@kretschmer2026order]'). Fixed windows are then shown to lose correct structures ('fixed ±1 min windows kept only 71 %', Conclusions). How badly a fixed tolerance fails depends on the base model's error level, which is not given. Only 24.3 % of isomeric decoys are eliminated (Section 4.6). The pre-specified metric 'width (minutes and % of the system's RT span)' (EXPERIMENT_PLAN.md, Metrics) appears only once in the text and in no table.
  * Corrective action: Add a table for n = 10/20/40 of the transferred point accuracy on the target systems: per-system MAE, median AE and RMSE (in min and in % of RT span), and Kendall τ between base score and RT. Report it for both base models and HILIC. On the NORMAN methods, compare it directly with the projection/prediction errors that Malm & Kruve 2025 reported for the same 37 methods. Add median width as % of RT span to Table 2. Report the share of systems where the median window exceeds 50 % of the span, i.e. where filtering is practically non-informative. Present the fixed-window results as conditional on this base model.
  * Required experiment: Re-run the n = 20, α = 0.10 evaluation (conformal, Gaussian, fixed ±0.5/1/2 min) with a stronger, condition-aware base model on RepoRT and NORMAN. Use the publicly released retention-order model of the cited 2026 order-model study if available; otherwise, an oracle-like upper bound trained with target-system data from other draws. Show how width, decoy elimination and fixed-window coverage scale with base accuracy.
* **[major] R9-02: The novel component adds a small, inconsistent gain and conformal windows do not filter better; ACA's 'quantifiable advantage' criterion is at risk** (Abstract (Results), Table 3, Sections 4.3, 4.6, 4.9, 4.10, Table S21, Conclusions; cover letter; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Table 3, per-fold change versus jackknife+: '+3 / −18 / +0 / −10 / +0', so the gain comes from two of five sources (BGC p = 0.09). On the external NORMAN data: 'no efficiency gain (11.14 versus 10.70 min; p = 0.32)', and the method was worse with calibration chemicals (Holm p = 0.02 and 0.01). In HILIC the proposed windows scored 16.70 against 14.33 for jackknife+. Table S21, HILIC n = 20, shows the under-covering Gaussian window had the lowest interval score of all (14.13), with P-spline at 14.33. This is not mentioned in Section 4.9. At matched retention, the proposed windows eliminate the fewest decoys: 'at 85 % retention … 32.9 % (proposed) … 35.3 % (P-spline)' (Section 4.6). The Conclusions concede 'Conformal windows do not remove more false candidates than conventional windows'. Validity itself comes from plain jackknife+ (0.909), an existing method. Recommendation (iii) advises plain jackknife+ for short gradients and HILIC. selected_journal.md itself lists 'perceived as application of an existing ML tool' as a key rejection risk.
  * Corrective action: Recentre the claimed contribution on what the data show:
(a) the first multi-system calibration audit of RT-window practice;
(b) a validated, simple recipe: monotone calibrant map + jackknife+, n ≥ 1/α − 1;
(c) selection-aware calibration.
Present the meta-learned normalisation as an optional refinement, not as 'the Proposed' method. State the HILIC result (Gaussian lowest interval score) explicitly. In the Significance paragraph, quantify the analytical advantage in decision terms, e.g. the known versus unknown operating point and the expected number of correct structures lost per 1,000 features.
  * Required experiment: None beyond R9-03. Optionally, report how decoy elimination at each method's realised retention changes when every method is first recalibrated on training systems to the same target retention (see R9-03). This isolates the value of per-system adaptivity.
* **[major] R9-03: Missing natural competitor (±k·RMSE with k calibrated on public systems), and the abstract overgeneralises the failure of 'conventional windows'** (Abstract (Significance), Highlight 1, Table 2, Section 4.1 (second paragraph), graphical_abstract.png; confidence: high)
  * Evidence: Table 2: ±2·RMSE reaches coverage 0.919 [0.916, 0.921], 0 % of systems below 0.85, median width 5.17 min and interval score 9.97 min. The proposed windows reach 0.911, 0 %, 4.68 and 9.07. Section 4.1 says ±2·RMSE delivered '89.8 % … at n = 10'. A laboratory could therefore get nominal-level average coverage by picking k empirically on public systems. The paper's own SASC machinery (Section 2.6) does exactly this for scale factors, but it is never applied to the Gaussian window under random calibrants. Even so, the Abstract Significance says 'conventional windows discard many correct structures on new systems', and Highlight 1 says 'Parametric and fixed RT windows lose correct structures'. The ±2·RMSE result is absent from the abstract and the graphical abstract.
